❯ THis text describes the system in this directory:                             
                                                                                
  Jamie Montgomerie’s World Wide Web Log                                        
  Home Tags All About                                                           
  Rescuing vintage microcontrollers part 3: Life at long last                   
  Posted on 2 Mar 2026                                                          
                                                                                
  In this series of posts, I’m rescuing vintage microcontrollers from their     
  decades-long purgatory in a drawer in my local makerspace. To this end, I’m   
  attempting to use them to drive a LED sign made of WS2812 strips              
                                                                                
  So far in this series, I found some decades-old Signetics SCN8031AH and OKI   
  MSM83C154 microcontrollers in a drawer, and worked out that they’re 8051 and  
  8052 derivatives (members of the MCS-51 family) from the 1980s, and           
  calculated that it should be possible to make them control modern WS2812 LED  
  strips.                                                                       
                                                                                
  In this installment, I get one of the microcontrollers set up on breadboards  
  and get it to, at last, fulfill its purpose and BLINK AN LED!                 
                                                                                
  Unfortunately, the makerspace has been closed for the last month or so for a  
  move to a new location (which seems great - seriously check it out if you’re  
  in the Asheville area, monthly open days will be starting again soon).        
                                                                                
  Happily, I took one of the OKI MSM83C154s home before everything was packed   
  up, so I could still work on this project!                                    
  Cool Runnings                                                                 
                                                                                
  So, first thing to check: do these chips work? Perhaps should’ve worked that  
  out before now - although, honestly, there are so many of them that surely    
  some will. Let’s hope that the one I brought home is one of them!             
                                                                                
  The first thing I tried was to ‘free run’ the processor - also known as ‘NOP  
  testing’1. A ‘NOP test’ is when you connect a processor to a system -         
  sometimes called a “NOP tester” - that’s set up to fool it into thinking that 
  all its code consists of NOP (No Operation) instructions. Whatever address    
  the processor tries to read, it’ll get a NOP and move on to the next address, 
  cycling through all of ‘memory’ before starting at the beginning again.       
                                                                                
  On MCS-51 processors2 the NOP instruction is conveniently represented by an   
  all-zero byte, so this can be done just by pulling all the data lines down to 
  0V.                                                                           
                                                                                
  The obvious way to do this is with a simple breadboard - but there’s a        
  practical problem here: the processors from the makerspace drawers are all    
  square PLCC ICs that can’t plug into a breadboard directly. They have no      
  ‘pins’, only metal contacts on the outer/bottom edges.                        
                                                                                
  I ordered some PLCC sockets, thinking that I’d build a convertor myself - but 
  I turned out not to need to! I also ordered an EPROM programmer from eBay,    
  intending to use it                                                           
  ──── (207 lines hidden) ───────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
  nd a ‘high’ level representing a 1. ↩︎                                         
                                                                                
      Possibly I was remembering the “8-Bit Reviver” - which has the same       
  conclusion in its first project log - or the “ROM-emulator”, which ends up    
  doing an almost-but-not-quite the same thing as I do. There’s also “PicoROM”, 
  based on the RP2040, which does use the RP2040’s internal RAM.                
                                                                                
      And if I look way back, the solution I ended up with was also similar to  
  the “EEPROM programmer” in the aforementioned Veronica project, which I think 
  is where I first saw the idea of emulating ROM by using a microcontroller to  
  program some RAM before releasing reset and allowing another processor to     
  take over. ↩︎                                                                  
                                                                                
      …and clocking it fast, which would in itself be problematic, because it’s 
  not really possible to get the 16 bits of the address and data bus sensibly   
  spread across the GPIO pins of the ATmega without using the external-crystal  
  pins for GPIO, which then limits its speed to an 8MHz internal clock. ↩︎       
                                                                                
      Well, slightly thicker. ↩︎                                                 
                                                                                
      I wonder if it might be possible to come up with a way for the 8052 to    
  interface with the ATmega and use its UART to send debug logging to the       
  development system? Perhaps worth playing with later. ↩︎                       
                                                                                
      Don’t let its SourceForge URL deceive you - it is still under active      
  development - C language support is pretty up to date. ↩︎                      
                                                                                
      Burning the bootloader needs an ISP programmer - I already have one of    
  those, but it’s also possible to make one out of an Arduino in a pinch. ↩︎     
                                                                                
      Note that this is a link to a tag showing the project as it stands for    
  this blog post. Ongoing development will be on the main branch. ↩︎             
                                                                                
      If you’re not familiar with AVR programming the crash-course is that the  
  PORTx variables correspond to the Px pins in the circuit diagram - one bit    
  per pin. The DDRx (“Data DiRection”) variables set pins to input or output,   
  and the PINx (“Port Input”) variables let the program read signals on pins    
  set to input. ↩︎                                                               
                                                                                
      The MCS-51 also has instructions allowing some bits to be addressed and   
  altered directly! Those might come in handy in future installments. ↩︎

⏺ I've read the post and checked it against the repository. It describes the
  project as it stood at that blog post. The repository has moved on since then.

  Matches the post:
  - avr_host/ holds the ATmega328 code. It tests the SRAM, writes the ROM image,
    verifies it, then releases RST.
  - mcs51/ holds the 8052 code: main.c calls into assembly.asm.
  - scripts/integrate_mcs51_firmware.py and scripts/override_mcs51_upload.py
    handle the upload chain: the mcs51 upload rebuilds and flashes the AVR host.
  - platformio.ini sets up both environments.

  Not in the post:
  - hardware/8052LEDSign/: KiCad schematic, a main PCB and a test PCB, plus
    Gerbers and milling files.
  - docs/: notes on pulse timing, RAM/ROM shadowing, SPI ROM timings and PCB
    details.
  - avr_host/src/serial.* and packedStrings.*: serial code, possibly the
    8052-to-AVR debug logging idea from footnote 9. Not yet checked.
  - Character Set.png: probably the font for the sign.
  - Untracked: pioold/ and the milling outputs in Gerbers/.

  The repository has no CLAUDE.md.

  What would you like done next? For example, I could write a CLAUDE.md from
  this description, or start on the WS2812 driving code.

✻ Cooked for 11s · done 4:10 PM

❯ I've forgotten how to actually build and upload the system.                   

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ One command builds the 8052 firmware, puts it inside the AVR program, and
  flashes the AVR:

  pio run -e mcs51 -t upload

  mcs51 is the default environment, so pio run -t upload does the same. This
  comes from platformio.ini and the two scripts in scripts/.

  What happens when you run it:
  1. SDCC compiles mcs51/src/ to .pio/build/mcs51/firmware.hex. Code size is
     capped at 16384 bytes.
  2. scripts/override_mcs51_upload.py converts that to firmware.bin with
     objcopy.
  3. When the upload finishes, the same script runs pio run -e avr_host -t 
     upload --disable-auto-clean as a separate command. That flag stops
     PlatformIO deleting the mcs51 build folder before the AVR build can read
     it.
  4. scripts/integrate_mcs51_firmware.py runs xxd -i on firmware.bin and turns
     it into a C array stored in flash. That array is compiled into the AVR host
     program.
  5. avrdude flashes the ATmega328 over the USB serial adapter at 250000 baud,
     using the urclock protocol.
  6. The AVR resets, tests the SRAM, writes and verifies the ROM image, then
     releases the 8052's RST line.

  Requirements:
  - pio and xxd must be on your PATH.
  - The USB serial adapter must be connected to the ATmega's serial header. No
    upload port is set for avr_host, so PlatformIO picks the port automatically.
  - The ATmega needs the urboot bootloader already on it.

  Other commands:
  - To build the 8052 firmware without uploading: pio run -e mcs51
  - To flash the AVR host without rebuilding the 8052 code: pio run -e avr_host 
    -t upload. This only works if .pio/build/mcs51/firmware.bin already exists.
  - To burn the bootloader onto a new ATmega: pio run -e avr_host_bootloader -t 
    bootloader. This is a one-time step. It needs an ISP programmer (stk500v1 at
    19200 baud) on /dev/cu.usbserial-212130, so edit that port if your
    programmer shows up under a different name.
  - To watch serial output: pio device monitor -e avr_host, at 250000 baud.
  - To run the firmware in the emulator: use the VS Code task "Build + Run 8051
    emulator", the default build task. It runs emu8051 on firmware.hex at the
    configured clock speed.

❯ Wait, why are these all native? Is the wrong version of pio installed?        

⏺ Checking PlatformIO's install and how it picks packages...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ pio itself is native: arm64 Python, system type darwin_arm64. Checking what
  the PlatformIO registry offers for arm64...

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No, pio is the right version. It's native arm64 (Python 3.15, system type
  darwin_arm64). The problem is the packages PlatformIO downloads for it.

  Why they're Intel:
  - The PlatformIO registry has only one macOS build of each package, and it's
    Intel. For example, toolchain-sdcc-darwin_x86_64-1.40600.0.tar.gz is tagged
    as working on both darwin_x86_64 and darwin_arm64.
  - The same holds for every version of toolchain-sdcc, toolchain-atmelavr and
    tool-avrdude, including the newest. SDCC 1.40600.0 was published 2026-11-12
    and is still Intel-only.
  - So pio correctly asks for arm64 packages and receives Intel binaries.
    PlatformIO relies on Rosetta to run them.
  - Rosetta isn't running on this machine. Its daemon, oahd, isn't present, so
    every Intel tool fails with "Bad CPU type".
  - Updating pio or its packages won't fix this.

  The Homebrew tools are native because Homebrew builds them for arm64. Native
  versions are already installed:
  - SDCC 4.6.0
  - avr-gcc 15.3.0
  - avrdude 8.3, which supports urclock

  Two ways to fix it:
  1. Install Rosetta: softwareupdate --install-rosetta --agree-to-license. This
     fixes all the packages at once without changing the project. Apple has said
     macOS 27 is the last release with full Rosetta support, so this is a
     short-term fix.
  2. Use the Homebrew toolchains: add platform_packages overrides in
     platformio.ini pointing at local package folders. Each folder would contain
     a package.json and symlinks into /opt/homebrew/opt/.... This avoids
     Rosetta for good. The catch is a big compiler jump: SDCC 4.4 to 4.6 and
     avr-gcc 7.3 to 15.3. That could bring new warnings or code-size changes,
     which matters with the 16K ROM limit.

  I'd go with option 2. Which do you want?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ There's no sign of a fix coming. People have asked for native builds, but I
  found no official plan to provide them.

  - AVR toolchain: platform-atmelavr issue #345, "Add AVR compiler for ARM64"
    (https://github.com/platformio/platform-atmelavr/issues/345) was opened
    2025-09-09 and is still open. It has one comment, a +1 from 2026-10-26
    confirming the problem we found: the arm64 entry is the Intel build. No
    maintainer has replied.
  - A maintainer's earlier answer: in a PlatformIO Community thread
    (https://community.platformio.org/t/cant-build-with-toolchain-atmelavr-on-da
    rwin-arm64/26688), a maintainer said Mac users need Rosetta and that native
    packages would take time.
  - Older packages may never be rebuilt: a 2023 platformio-core issue
    (https://github.com/platformio/platformio-core/issues/4507) says many older
    toolchains will likely never be recompiled for arm64 Macs.
  - Mixed picture elsewhere: a few packages have native builds, such as newer
    tool-openocd and toolchain-gccarmnoneeabi, per a March 2026 forum post (http
    s://community.platformio.org/t/fresh-pio-install-on-apple-silicon/53994).
    Even where an arm64 build exists, PlatformIO sometimes picks the Intel one
    (espressif32 thread
    (https://community.platformio.org/t/platformio-seems-to-install-x86-version-
    of-esp32-toolchain-when-arm64-apple-silicon-is-available-and-works/34366),
    issue #897 (https://github.com/platformio/platform-espressif32/issues/897)).
  - MCS-51: the platform-intel_mcs51 repository has no issues mentioning arm64.
    SDCC 4.5 and 4.6 were added to the registry on 2026-11-12, still Intel-only.
    So the packages are being maintained, but nobody is building arm64
    versions.
  - Timing: Rosetta's support ends after macOS 27, the version on this machine.
    Unless the registry is updated by then, every Mac user of these platforms
    hits the same failure.

  You could add your own report to issue #345, or open one for
  platform-intel_mcs51. It's unlikely to fix things soon, so I still recommend
  the Homebrew-toolchain override for this project.

  Sources:
  - platform-atmelavr issue #345
    (https://github.com/platformio/platform-atmelavr/issues/345)
  - Can't build with toolchain-atmelavr on darwin_arm64 (PlatformIO Community)
    (https://community.platformio.org/t/cant-build-with-toolchain-atmelavr-on-da
    rwin-arm64/26688)
  - platformio-core issue #4507
    (https://github.com/platformio/platformio-core/issues/4507)
  - Fresh PIO install on Apple Silicon (PlatformIO Community) (https://community
    .platformio.org/t/fresh-pio-install-on-apple-silicon/53994)
  - x86 esp32 toolchain installed when arm64 available (PlatformIO Community)
    (https://community.platformio.org/t/platformio-seems-to-install-x86-version-
    of-esp32-toolchain-when-arm64-apple-silicon-is-available-and-works/34366)
  - platform-espressif32 issue #897
    (https://github.com/platformio/platform-espressif32/issues/897)
  - inkflow: pio needs Rosetta on Apple silicon (dotfiles issue #13)
    (https://github.com/wittyreference/dotfiles/issues/13)
  - ESPHome ESP8266 platform docs (https://esphome.io/components/esp8266/)

✻ Crunched for 29s · done 4:14 PM

❯ COuld I do it and propose a PR soewhere?                                      

⏺ Checking where these packages are built...

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Not as a PR. There's no public repository for one to go into. The best you can
  do is publish your own builds to the registry and offer them on the open
  issue.

  Why a PR won't work:
  - The registry's toolchain builds come from PlatformIO's maintainers. The
    build scripts aren't public.
  - platformio/toolchain-sdcc on GitHub holds only a package.json, last pushed
    2016-09-24. No build recipe is published for SDCC, avr-gcc or avrdude.
  - Only the platformio registry account can upload files to
    platformio/toolchain-sdcc and the others. Nobody else can add an arm64 build
    there.
  - The platform repos (platform-atmelavr, platform-intel_mcs51) do accept PRs,
    but they only choose package names and versions. They don't hold any
    binaries.
  - Merges are slow. In platform-atmelavr, "Update toolchain-atmelavr version"
    has been open since 2025-02-05. In platform-intel_mcs51, a PR from 2025-02
    and one from 2024-07 are still open.

  What you could do:
  1. Publish your own packages. Build arm64 SDCC, avr-gcc and avrdude, or
     repackage the Homebrew builds. Upload them under your own registry account
     with pio pkg publish, tagged darwin_arm64 only. Projects can then use them
     through platform_packages = <owner>/toolchain-sdcc@.... This needs a
     PlatformIO account.
  2. Offer them upstream. Comment on issue #345 and open a matching issue on
     platform-intel_mcs51, linking your packages and the build recipe. A
     maintainer could then upload them as official builds.
  3. Optionally, PR the platform repos to use the new versions once official
     arm64 builds exist.

  One catch: Homebrew's binaries link to libraries under /opt/homebrew, such as
  readline and zstd for SDCC. A package for other people needs to be built so it
  doesn't depend on Homebrew, or bundle those libraries. That makes the build
  work bigger than the PR.

  To get this project building, the local override is still the fastest route.
  It's also the first step of option 1: the same layout of package.json plus
  binaries becomes the published package later. Should I set up the local
  override?

✻ Worked for 27s · done 4:15 PM
❯ Is there serial output I should be ab;e to read with -t monitor?              

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes. The ATmega prints a startup report at 250000 baud. The 8052 itself prints
  nothing, so after "8052 reset released." the output stops.

  What you should see, from avr_host/src/main.cpp:
  AVR Host starting up...

  RAM test...
  Passed - 32768 bytes tested!

  Writing <n> bytes to 'ROM'...
   XXXXXXXX XXXXXXXX ...      (hex dump, 64 bytes per line)

  Verifying <n> bytes in 'ROM'...
   XXXXXXXX XXXXXXXX ...
  Passed - <n> bytes tested!

  Letting 8052 take control.
  8052 reset released.

  Reading it:
  - pio run -t monitor and pio device monitor -e avr_host both work. The two
    environments both set monitor_speed = 250000.
  - Opening the monitor toggles the adapter's DTR line, which is wired to the
    ATmega's reset. So each time you connect, the startup sequence runs again
    and you see the full report.
  - 250000 baud is exact at 8 MHz, so there are no baud-rate errors.
  - The 8052's own serial pin isn't wired to the AVR or the header. Getting
    output from the 8052 would need the debug-logging idea from footnote 9 of
    your post.

  Two small bugs I spotted:
  - main.cpp:256: the program ends in while(true); without sending what's left
    in the output buffer. The final newline is never sent, though all the
    visible text arrives.
  - main.cpp RAM test and verify step: errorCount is a uint8_t. The RAM test
    makes 131072 comparisons, so a count of 256 errors (or any multiple of 256)
    wraps to 0 and the test reports "Passed". A uint16_t or uint32_t would fix
    that.

  None of this runs until the toolchain problem is fixed, because the AVR can't
  be built or flashed yet.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ Ah! There's no power going to the top breadboard. Although I'd still expect   
  to see the initial output.                                                    

⏺ The software is fine. Run in simavr, the firmware prints the full startup
  report: "AVR Host starting up...", the RAM test, the ROM write and verify
  dumps, and the reset release. The simulator has no SRAM attached, so the test
  pass/fail lines there mean nothing. So the fault is in the hardware, and the
  unpowered top breadboard fits it.

  Why an unpowered breadboard causes trouble:
  - The 8052 and SRAM are on the top breadboard. With no supply, each chip's
    input protection diodes let it draw power through its signal pins instead.
  - When the ATmega drives the bus, it tries to power both chips through its I/O
    pins. That pulls a lot of current and drags its own supply down.
  - That can trigger brownout resets, which would explain the reset loop and why
    the output stops.
  - The USB adapter dropped off the bus once ("Device not configured") during my
    tests. That fits a current spike on a board powered from USB.

  What this doesn't explain, as you said: the first line goes out before the
  program touches any bus pins. One possibility is that the ATmega's own power
  is marginal. If it shares a rail or ground path with the dead breadboard, or
  is being partly powered through its RX pin from the adapter, the bootloader
  can still manage at low current while the app brownouts before its first byte
  finishes.

  Next steps:
  1. Fix the power feed to the top breadboard.
  2. Measure VCC at the ATmega's pins 7 and 20, and check that pin 8 (GND)
     shares ground with the adapter.
  3. Reconnect the monitor with pio device monitor -e avr_host.

  If it's still silent after that, I'll write a minimal test firmware for the
  AVR that only prints, with nothing on the bus, so we can isolate the serial
  path.

※ recap: Uploads to the 8052 LED sign rig now work, but the ATmega's startup message over serial isn't arriving, though it prints fine in a simulator. Next, restore power to the top breadboard, check the ATmega's 5V supply, then reopen the monitor. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ Okay, power restored to the top board. I'm seeing garbage now - did you       
  change the rate?                                                              

⏺ No, I haven't changed any files. Everything I ran was read-only: serial
  captures, a flash read, and the simulator.

  Garbage at the right nominal rate usually means the ATmega's clock is off. It
  runs on its internal RC oscillator, which can be off by several percent, and
  at 250000 baud there's little room for error. The bootloader measures the
  incoming baud rate and adjusts, so uploads keep working even when the app's
  fixed rate is off. Measuring the actual rate by capturing at nearby speeds...

⏺ No, I haven't changed the rate or any files.

  Every rate I tried gave noise. The rates were 115200, 230400, 250000, 460800,
  and a sweep from 220000 to 240000. Each capture was only 120–160 bytes,
  against roughly 800 characters in the full report.

  What I've confirmed:
  - The firmware is correct. It prints the full report in the simulator, and the
    chip's flash matches the build.
  - The adapter is slow to set non-standard rates on macOS. Opening at 250000
    takes 6.7–8.2 s, against 0.01 s at 115200 or 230400. That delay alone loses
    output when the monitor opens.
  - A working upload doesn't prove the adapter really runs at 250000. urboot
    measures the incoming baud rate and adjusts, so it hides any mismatch on the
    adapter's side.

  What I can't tell from software alone is which side is off:
  - The adapter may not really produce 250000. It might round it or fall back to
    another rate.
  - The ATmega's internal RC oscillator may be several percent off, which is
    common without calibration.

  Fastest way to find out: put your scope on the ATmega's TX pin (PD1, pin 3)
  and reset it. At 250000 baud, one bit should be 4.0 µs wide. A noticeably
  different width means the AVR clock is off. A correct width means the adapter
  is the problem.

  Fix that avoids both problems: switch the app's serial to 38400 baud, a
  standard rate. At 8 MHz that has only 0.2% error, and the adapter opens it
  instantly. That means changing serialInit(250000) in avr_host/src/main.cpp and
  monitor_speed in both environments in platformio.ini. Uploads can stay at
  250000, since the bootloader adjusts anyway.

  Should I make the 38400 change, or do you want to check with the scope first?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Serial output works at 38400. The full report arrives, 849 bytes with no
  corruption. The adapter's slow handling of non-standard rates was the problem,
  not the wiring.

  The report now shows a real fault, and a second problem that was hiding it.

  The verify step reads back the address instead of the data. Every byte comes
  back as its own address: 00 01 02 03 …. When the ATmega releases the bus, the
  address it last put there stays on the wires as stray charge. If the SRAM
  never drives the bus during a read, read_byte() just reads that leftover
  address. So the SRAM isn't responding to reads.

  The RAM test's "Passed" is false. It writes a byte and reads it back straight
  away, without putting the address on the bus again. The pattern it just wrote
  is still on the bus as leftover charge, so the read always matches whether the
  SRAM works or not. The simulator, which has no SRAM at all, also prints
  "Passed".

  Likely causes, most likely first:
  1. Wiring. The SRAM's OE or CE input isn't going low during reads. Check the
     path from ATmega PD5 (/RD) through the 7408 AND gate to the SRAM's OE pin,
     and check that CE is tied low.
  2. Timing. read_byte() waits only one nop (125 ns at 8 MHz) after taking /RD
     low. The signal also passes through the old 7408, and the SRAM needs its
     own access time. That may not be long enough.
  3. Writes not landing either. The current tests can't tell good writes from
     bad ones, so /WR (PD4) through the 7408 to the SRAM's WE pin is also worth
     checking.

  Suggested next step: rewrite the RAM test so it writes the whole SRAM first,
  then reads it all back, with a few extra nops in read_byte(). That gives a
  real pass/fail and rules timing in or out. If it still fails, check the OE, CE
  and WE wiring with a meter or the scope.

  Should I make those two changes?

  Ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I traced the SRAM's control pins in the KiCad schematic. One detail rules out
  most wires: all 8 bits read back as exactly the address. That means the SRAM's
  outputs never turn on at all. A single broken data wire would corrupt only
  one bit, and a broken address wire would give wrong data rather than the
  address. So the fault is in something all 8 outputs depend on.

  Read path for SRAM /OE (pin 22), from the schematic:
  - ATmega PD5 (pin 11) → 7408 pin 2.
  - 7408 pin 1 is the 8052's /PSEN (now floating). Gate output, pin 3 → jumper
    to pin 4.
  - 7408 pin 5 is the 8052's /WR (now floating). Gate output, pin 6 → SRAM pin
    22 (/OE).
  - SRAM pin 20 (/CS) → GND.

  Likely culprits, most likely first:
  1. SRAM power or ground. The SRAM is on the top breadboard, which just had a
     power fault. Check pin 28 = 5 V, and pins 14 and 20 = 0 V. Breadboard power
     rails are often split halfway along, so a chip on one half can have no
     power even when the other half does.
  2. 7408 power. Check pin 14 = 5 V and pin 7 = 0 V.
  3. The pin 3 → pin 4 jumper or the pin 6 → SRAM pin 22 wire on the 7408.
  4. The 8052's floating inputs. With the 8052 removed, 7408 pins 1, 5 and 12
     and 7410 pins 3 and 4 are left floating. If the gates are 74LS, unconnected
     inputs read as high, which is fine. If they're 74HC or 74HCT, those inputs
     float to unpredictable levels and can hold /OE inactive. Tie them to 5 V
     while the 8052 is out, ideally through about 10 kΩ.

  Checking it: each read pulses /OE for only about 250 ns, which a multimeter
  won't catch. I can write a test firmware that holds the read line low
  continuously. Then you can check with a meter that SRAM pin 22 goes low and
  that the data pins show a stable value.

  Separate bug in the schematic, important before you mill the board: the 8052's
  /RD and /WR are swapped going into the 7408.
  - /RD (P3.7) goes to pin 12, which feeds the write enable: SRAM /WE, pin 27.
  - /WR (P3.6) goes to pin 5, which feeds the output enable: SRAM /OE, pin 22.
  - So when the 8052 does a data read (MOVX read), the SRAM gets a write
    instead.
  - The blink code never does data reads or writes, only instruction fetches, so
    this hasn't shown up yet.
  - I checked only the schematic, not the breadboard. If the breadboard is wired
    the same way, it needs the same fix.

  Want me to write the hold-/RD-low test firmware?

  Notes:
  - PB6 and PB7 are the crystal pins, used here for data. That's why the ATmega
    runs on its internal 8 MHz oscillator.
  - Each 7475 latch takes its four data bits in reverse order: D0 goes to input
    D3, D1 to D2, and so on. That's harmless as long as the latch outputs are
    reversed the same way on the way to the SRAM's address pins. I haven't
    traced that part.
  - The schematic lists the gates as 74LS08 and 74LS10. If the chips on the
    breadboard really are LS, the 8052's floating inputs read as high and aren't
    the fault.
  - J1 has only RX and TX on it. On the breadboard, reset is clearly wired to
    the adapter's DTR line, since it works, but that connection isn't in this
    schematic.

✻ Churned for 16s · done 4:59 PM

❯ What explains the pattern of the read-backs? Why is it seemingly incrementing 
  every byte?                                                                   

⏺ The read-back pattern is the low byte of each address. Each read sees whatever
  the ATmega itself last put on the bus, because the SRAM never drives it.

  The verify loop, step by step:
  1. place_address_on_bus(i) puts the low byte of i on PORTB (data bus D0–D7)
     and pulses ALE so the 7475 latches capture it.
  2. It then sets DDRB = 0 and PORTB = 0. The ATmega stops driving the bus, with
     no pull-ups.
  3. read_byte() pulls /RD low, waits one nop, and reads PINB. That's about 4
     clock cycles, or 0.5 µs, after the bus was released.
  4. If the SRAM's outputs were on, PINB would show the stored byte. They
     aren't, so nothing drives the bus.
  5. The wires and chip inputs on the bus still hold the voltage they were last
     given, like small capacitors. Half a microsecond later they still carry the
     address byte, so PINB reads i & 0xFF.
  6. i counts 0 to 142, so the reads go 00 01 02 … 8E. With more than 256 bytes
     the pattern would wrap back to 00.

  Why the voltages hold for 0.5 µs (my reasoning, not measured):
  - Bits that were high: 74LS inputs draw only about 20 µA when high. Draining
    around 30 pF of breadboard and pin capacitance from 5 V down to the ATmega's
    3 V high threshold takes about 3 µs, much longer than the 0.5 µs.
  - Bits that were low: a floating 74LS input drifts up to only about 1.4 V. The
    ATmega still reads anything below 1.5 V as low.
  - So both 1s and 0s survive until PINB is read.

  The same effect fakes the RAM test pass. That test writes a pattern and reads
  it back straight away, so it reads its own pattern still sitting on the bus.
  simavr reproduces both effects with no SRAM at all.

  Simple way to confirm: enable the pull-ups during the read by setting PORTB = 
  0xFF while DDRB = 0, just before reading PINB.
  - If the SRAM isn't driving the bus, every read becomes FF.
  - Once the SRAM works, its data overrides the weak pull-ups and the real bytes
    appear.
  - As a permanent change, this stops a floating bus from ever looking like
    valid data.

  Want me to add that to read_byte() and change the RAM test to write everything
  before reading anything back?

✻ Crunched for 28s · done 5:02 PM

  - My capture ended at 8 seconds, partway through the verify dump. The test
    itself isn't affected.

  powered or selected. Writes can't be checked until reads work. Check in this
  order:
  1. Power and select: SRAM pin 28 at 5 V, and pins 14 and 20 at 0 V.
  2. 7408 power: pin 14 at 5 V and pin 7 at 0 V.
  3. /OE chain:
     - ATmega pin 11 (PD5) → 7408 pin 2.
     - 7408 pin 3 → pin 4.
     - 7408 pin 6 → SRAM pin 22.
  4. Floating inputs left by removing the 8052: 7408 pins 1 (/PSEN) and 5 (/RD)
     must sit high. If the gates are 74LS that happens on its own, but tying
     them to 5 V rules this out.

  A single read pulse is only about 300 ns, too short for a multimeter. If you
  probe 7408 pin 6 or SRAM pin 22 with the scope during the RAM test, it should
  show constant short low pulses. If it stays high, follow the chain back toward
  pin 2.

✻ Worked for 50s · done 5:53 PM

❯ Okay, bit of a diversion. I want to write something that will dump the ROM of 
  the 8052. I have recently learned that the one I'm using may be               
  preprogrammed. Can we use the circuit to do that?                             

⏺ Yes, probably, with one wiring change. Whether it works depends on how this
  particular chip handles EA. Looking up the MSM80C154/83C154 details...

⏺ Web Search("MSM80C154 MSM83C154 datasheet EA pin internal ROM 16K MOVC
            security")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 10s

⏺ Web Search("dumping 8051 mask ROM EA pin toggle MOVC external code read
            internal ROM technique")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 9s

⏺ Yes. The circuit can do it with one extra wire: EA has to be driven by the
  ATmega instead of tied to ground. Whether it works depends on two things I
  couldn't confirm for the OKI part.

  Method (the standard 8051 mask-ROM dump):
  1. The ATmega holds the 8052 in reset with EA low, so the 8052 boots from the
     SRAM.
  2. The ATmega loads a dumper program. A jump at 0x0000 leads to the real code
     at 0x4000 or above. The OKI datasheet
     (https://www.keil.com/dd/docs/datashts/oki/msm8xc154s_ds.pdf) says that
     with EA high, the MSM83C154 runs internal ROM for 0x0000–0x3FFF and
     external memory above that. So code at 0x4000 or higher keeps running from
     the SRAM whichever way EA is set.
  3. The ATmega releases reset. The 8052 jumps to 0x4000 and waits about 50 ms.
  4. Meanwhile the ATmega raises EA high. From then on, code reads (MOVC 
     A,@A+DPTR) of addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF fetch from the internal mask ROM.
  5. The dumper copies all 16 KB with MOVC into external RAM using MOVX, then
     loops forever. That takes about 0.15 s at 13.875 MHz.
  6. The ATmega waits about 1 s, puts the 8052 back into reset, reads the 16 KB
     out of the SRAM and prints it as hex over serial.

  How it fits the current circuit:
  - EA is hard-wired to ground (the black wire in your post). Rewire it to the
    ATmega's PD2 (pin 4), which is currently unused. On the DIP-style pinout, EA
    is pin 31.
  - The ATmega can already write 8052 code address 0x4000 and up. Writing at
    ATmega address 0x4000 lands in the lower half of the SRAM, which is where
    the 8052 fetches 0x4000–0x7FFF.
  - The dumped bytes come back through the SRAM, so no extra output wiring is
    needed.
  - The dumper does its own MOVX writes, so it's also the first real test of the
    8052's /WR path. That's the path whose schematic wiring you just fixed.
    Make sure the breadboard matches.

  The two unknowns:
  1. EA latched at reset. The Intel MCS-51 manual
     (https://web.mit.edu/6.115/www/document/8051.pdf) and some CMOS variants
     say EA is sampled and latched at reset. If the MSM83C154 does that, raising
     EA later does nothing, and the method fails. The 8051dumper project
     (https://github.com/JohnDMcMaster/8051dumper) only claims support for
     original Intel parts and exact clones.
  2. ROM security. The TEMIC 83C154
     (https://pe2bz.philpem.me.uk/pdf%20on%20typenumber/0-9/80C154.pdf) offers
     an optional "secret ROM" setting. It blocks MOVC reads of internal ROM from
     code running in external memory, and it latches EA at reset. The OKI
     datasheet doesn't mention anything like it, but I didn't find a statement
     that it's absent either.

  If it fails, it's easy to tell. Every byte comes back the same, e.g. all FF,
  or the dump is just a copy of the SRAM contents.

  Something I noticed while working this out: the RAM mapping in your blog
  post's table doesn't match the 7410 NAND as wired. With /RD or /WR low, the
  NAND always outputs 1. So 8052 data RAM shares an SRAM half with code
  addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF, not 0x4000–0x7FFF as the table says. That means data
  writes would land on the program image. The dumper isn't affected, because its
  code lives at 0x4000. It will matter later for the generated code that drives
  the WS2812 LEDs, though.

  What building it involves:
  - A separate mcs51_romdump environment with an assembly dumper: a reset stub
    at 0x0000 and the main code at 0x4000.
  - A dump mode in the ATmega host: drive EA, raise it after releasing reset,
    then read the SRAM back and print it.
  - The size limit on the 8052 binary goes up from 16 KB to about 16.5 KB,
    because of the 0x4000 offset. It still fits in the ATmega's flash.

  Want me to build it?

✻ Worked for 1m 24s · done 5:55 PM

❯ Oh. What did I do wrong?                                                      

⏺ Your notes had the right design, but the A14 inverter got dropped when it was
  built.

  What docs/RAM:ROM Shadowing.txt specifies:
  - Data RAM should share an SRAM half with code addresses 0x4000–0x7FFF.
  - The truth table gives SRAM A14 = A14 unchanged for code fetches, and 1 for
    data accesses. In words: ORIG_A14 OR (~PSEN AND ~ROMWR).
  - The NAND-only version in the same notes is (!ORIG_A14) NAND (...), with A14
    inverted going into the NAND.

  What was built:
  - 7410 gate 2 is wired as NAND(A14, ~RD, ~WR), taking A14 straight from the
    bus.
  - Without that inversion, code fetches see SRAM A14 = NOT A14. Data accesses
    still force it to 1.
  - 1 is what code addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF produce (A14 = 0, inverted). So data
    RAM ends up sharing a half with the low 16 KB of code, not the high 16 KB.

  Nobody noticed because nothing has done a data RAM access yet. The ATmega and
  the 8052's code fetches both go through the same inversion, so they agree with
  each other, and the blink works. The table in the blog post was worked out
  from the circuit as built, so it records the inverted mapping.

  The fix uses a gate you already have. The 7410 has two unused gates, 1 and 3,
  so make one an inverter:
  1. Disconnect A14 from 7410 pin 5.
  2. Connect A14 (ATmega PD7 and 8052 A14) to 7410 pins 1, 2 and 13, gate 1's
     three inputs tied together.
  3. Connect 7410 pin 12, gate 1's output, to pin 5.
  4. In KiCad: tie pins 1, 2 and 13 to /A14, connect pin 12 to pin 5, and remove
     /A14 from pin 5.

  Result:
  - Code fetches: SRAM A14 = A14. Data accesses: 1. Data RAM now shares a half
    with code 0x4000–0x7FFF, as the notes intended.
  - The ATmega host code needs no changes. Its writes also pass straight through
    now, so ATmega address 0 still lands at 8052 code address 0x0000.
  - Timing is unaffected. The extra gate sits only on the A14 path, which
    settles long before /RD, /WR or /PSEN go low. The path from /RD or /WR to
    SRAM A14 is still a single gate delay.

  Effect on the ROM dumper: with the fix, data RAM shares a half with code
  0x4000–0x7FFF, which is where the dumper's own code sits. Its MOVX writes
  would overwrite itself. Two ways round it:
  - Do the dump before making this fix.
  - Or have the dumper write only to data addresses outside the range its code
    occupies. For example, code at 0x4000–0x40FF and the dump written to
    0x0100–0x40FF. Data RAM has only 16 KB, so data address 0x0100 and 0x4100
    are the same cell. The two ranges overlap by 256 bytes, and the dumper would
    need to handle that carefully.

  The first option is simpler: dump the ROM, then rewire the inverter.

✻ Churned for 30s · done 5:57 PM

❯ This is right:                                                                
                                                                                
  The only time the output of this three-input NAND will be 0 is when all its   
  inputs are 1. If A14 is low - 0 - it will always output 1 (with that 0        
  obviously not all the inputs are 1). If A14 is high - 1 - it will output 1 if 
  the 8052 is asserting RD or WR (and so one is low - these are active-low      
  signals), otherwise it will output 0 because all three inputs are high.       

⏺ Agreed, that paragraph describes the NAND correctly. The mistake is in the
  table, and you can check it using the paragraph alone.

  Applying the paragraph to each kind of access:

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────┬────────┬─────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │         Access         │ A14 │ /RD,   │ NAND output =   │  SRAM range   │
  │                        │     │  /WR   │    SRAM A14     │               │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────┼────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Code fetch             │ 0   │ both   │ 1               │ 0x4000–0x7FFF │
  │ 0x0000–0x3FFF          │     │ high   │                 │               │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────┼────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Code fetch             │ 1   │ both   │ 0               │ 0x0000–0x3FFF │
  │ 0x4000–0x7FFF          │     │ high   │                 │               │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────┼────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Data read/write        │ 0   │ one    │ 1               │ 0x4000–0x7FFF │
  │ 0x0000–0x3FFF          │     │ low    │                 │               │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────┼────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ Data read/write        │ 1   │ one    │ 1               │ 0x4000–0x7FFF │
  │ 0x4000–0x7FFF          │     │ low    │                 │               │
  └────────────────────────┴─────┴────────┴─────────────────┴───────────────┘

  Compared with the blog's table:
  - Code fetch rows: match. Code 0x0000 → SRAM 0x4000, and code 0x4000 → SRAM
    0x0000.
  - Data rows: don't match. The table says data accesses reach SRAM
    0x0000–0x3FFF. The paragraph says the NAND outputs 1 whenever /RD or /WR is
    low, so data accesses always reach SRAM 0x4000–0x7FFF.

  What that means:
  - SRAM 0x4000–0x7FFF holds the code image at 8052 addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF.
    Data writes land there too.
  - The intended shared area, code 0x4000–0x7FFF, is SRAM 0x0000–0x3FFF. Data
    accesses can't reach it at all.
  - To send data accesses to SRAM 0x0000–0x3FFF, the gate would have to output 0
    when /RD or /WR is low. A NAND can't produce that from these inputs. An
    inverter on A14 ahead of the NAND fixes it, as in my previous message, or as
    in the (!ORIG_A14) NAND (...) form in your notes.

  So the circuit does what the paragraph says. The table's data RAM column, and
  the plan to have the 8052 run code it generates into data RAM, assume a
  mapping the circuit doesn't provide.

※ recap: We're building tooling to dump the 8052's internal mask ROM using the existing ATmega and SRAM circuit. Next, you decide whether I should build the dumper, and run it before adding the A14 inverter fix to the circuit.

❯ Ah - I think you're correct about the table. But I think the prose is         
  correct?                                                                      
                                                                                
  The intention is to simulate a system where the RAM is also mapped into the   
  top half of the address space, but the RAM and ROM both begin at address 0    
  logically. As if the ROM is a seperate chip.                                  

⏺ The intention is clear, and the NAND part of the prose is accurate. The
  circuit still doesn't achieve that intention, and the relabelled table above
  shows why.

  What the intention requires:
  - A "ROM chip" at code addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF.
  - A "RAM chip" at data addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF, also visible at code addresses
    0x4000–0x7FFF.
  - So data accesses must reach the same SRAM half as code 0x4000–0x7FFF. Which
    physical half that is doesn't matter. Only that the two match.

  What the circuit does, from the prose's own description of the NAND:
  - Code 0x4000–0x7FFF: NAND output 0, the lower half of the SRAM.
  - Data accesses: NAND output 1, the upper half. That's the same half as code
    0x0000–0x3FFF.
  - So the 8052's data accesses go to the "ROM chip". The "RAM chip" can be
    fetched as code at 0x4000, but the 8052 can't write to it.

  Where the prose goes wrong:
  - Correct: the description of the NAND gate (the paragraph you quoted).
  - Wrong as built: "the RAM section is also mapped to respond to ROM reads in
    the second 16K address space". The half that responds to code fetches at
    0x4000 isn't the half data accesses reach.
  - Wrong as built: "the 8052 is unable to write to the 'ROM' range". The 8052's
    data writes land in the ROM image.

  The fix is the same either way: put an inverter on A14 ahead of the NAND,
  using spare 7410 gate 1 (pins 1, 2 and 13 in, pin 12 out to pin 5). Then:
  - Code 0x0000–0x3FFF: SRAM A14 = 0, lower half. This is the "ROM chip".
  - Code 0x4000–0x7FFF: SRAM A14 = 1, upper half. This is the "RAM chip".
  - Data accesses: SRAM A14 = 1, upper half. The same "RAM chip". That's what
    you intended.

  The ATmega host code needs no changes, and its ROM image still starts at
  ATmega address 0.

✻ Baked for 12s · done 6:56 PM

❯ Ah. Right. As it stands we can only ever write into the lower half of ROM and 
  the other half is completely inaccessible...                                  

⏺ Yes, that's right for the 8052:
  - Its data reads and writes can reach only the half that holds code
    0x0000–0x3FFF. Writes there overwrite the program image.
  - It can't read or write the half at code 0x4000–0x7FFF as data. It can only
    fetch from it as code.

  One exception: the ATmega can still write that second half. ATmega address
  0x4000 and up lands there, because the 8052's /RD and /WR are both high while
  the ATmega has the bus. So that half works like a second ROM that only the
  ATmega can load.

  This wiring happens to suit the ROM dumper:
  - The dumper's code goes at 0x4000, which the ATmega can load and the 8052 can
    run.
  - Its MOVX writes go to the other half, so they can't overwrite the dumper's
    own code.
  - The 16 KB dump fills that half exactly. All it overwrites is the reset stub
    at 0x0000, which has already run by then.
  - Afterwards the ATmega reads the dump back from ATmega addresses
    0x0000–0x3FFF.

  So I suggest doing the dump with the current wiring, then adding the A14
  inverter (7410 gate 1) on the breadboard and in KiCad. Want me to build the
  dumper, an mcs51_romdump environment plus a dump mode in the ATmega host? The
  breadboard needs one change first: EA moved from ground to ATmega PD2 (pin 4).

✻ Cooked for 8s · done 6:59 PM

❯ Hmm. Could we acheieve the desired result with one nAND gate if we used PSEN  
  in ther esomehow,                                                             

⏺ Not with one NAND, with or without PSEN. The 7410 already has two spare gates,
  though, so the inverter fix needs no extra chip.

  Why one NAND can't do it:
  - A NAND can only force its output to 1, when any input is 0. Otherwise it
    passes the inverse of the remaining input.
  - During a code fetch the output has to follow A14. So A14 must be an input,
    and every other input must be 1 during fetches. That gives output = NOT A14.
  - With NOT A14, the half at code 0x4000 is the one with output 0.
  - So data accesses need the output forced to 0, which a NAND can't do.

  Where PSEN fits:
  - PSEN is 0 during every code fetch. As a NAND input it would force the output
    to 1 on all fetches, so A14 would have no effect. It can't be an input to
    this gate.
  - The opposite polarity would work: OR(A14, PSEN).
    - Fetches (PSEN = 0) pass A14 through unchanged.
    - Data accesses (PSEN = 1) force 1, the same half as code 0x4000.
  - The problem is the ATmega's writes. PSEN sits high while the 8052 is in
    reset, so the output is stuck at 1 and the ATmega could never load the ROM
    half. There's also no spare OR gate.

  Other single gates fail the same way:
  - AND(A14, /RD, /WR) passes A14 through on fetches but forces 0 on data
    accesses. That's the code 0x0000 half again, the same fault in reverse.
  - Any one-gate version needs either an inverted A14 or an inverted data-access
    signal.

  So the cheapest fix is the two-gate version on the existing 7410:
  - Gate 1 as an inverter: A14 → pins 1, 2 and 13. Pin 12 → pin 5.
  - Gate 2 as now: pins 3 and 4 still take /WR and /RD. Pin 6 → SRAM A14.

  It costs one wire change on the breadboard and one gate's connections in
  KiCad, with no new parts and no extra delay on the read and write path.

✻ Brewed for 28s · done 7:00 PM

❯ It would be fine if the ROM was at 0x4000 up.                                 

⏺ That works, and it needs no hardware change. The circuit as wired already
  gives exactly that layout:

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       8052 access        │  SRAM   │                Role                │
  │                          │  half   │                                    │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Code fetch 0x0000–0x3FFF │ upper   │ RAM: also reached by every data    │
  │                          │         │ read and write                     │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Data read/write (any     │ upper   │ RAM                                │
  │ address)                 │         │                                    │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Code fetch 0x4000–0x7FFF │ lower   │ ROM: the 8052 can't write it       │
  └──────────────────────────┴─────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the RAM can be fetched as code at 0x0000, and the protected ROM is at
  0x4000 and up. Generated code written into data RAM becomes runnable at the
  same address in code space, which is the WS2812 plan.

  What changes in software:
  1. The program is linked at 0x4000. SDCC's --code-loc 0x4000 does this.
     board_build.size_code has to cover 0x4000 plus the program size.
  2. The reset and interrupt vectors are in RAM. The 8052 starts at 0x0000 and
     its interrupt vectors run up to 0x002B, all in the RAM half. The ATmega
     loads a stub there: LJMP 0x4000 for reset, and an LJMP to the matching
     handler for each interrupt.
  3. The 8052 must not write data addresses 0x0000–0x002F, or it overwrites its
     own vectors. Start data allocation above them.
  4. ATmega host: almost no change. ATmega address X reaches the same SRAM cell
     as 8052 code address X. So a binary covering code 0x0000 to the end of the
     program loads with no translation. It just gets longer, a 16 KB gap plus
     the program, which still fits in the ATmega's 32 KB flash.

  The ROM dumper also fits. Its code goes in the ROM half at 0x4000. Its MOVX
  writes fill the RAM half, overwriting the vector stub, which isn't needed by
  then.

  One catch for later: if the finished project moves to a real EPROM, a plain
  EPROM at 0x0000 is the conventional layout. Your vectors and code would then
  need relinking at 0x0000, or a different decode circuit. Since the code is
  being built for this SRAM-based setup either way, that seems acceptable.

  Should I fix the blog's table and prose in a corrections note, or go ahead
  with the linker changes and the dumper?

✻ Baked for 20s · done 7:01 PM

❯ Oh, no, I meant if would be fine if rom read 0x0000 mapped to 0x4000 and ROM  
  read 0x4000 mapped to 0x0000                                                  

⏺ Understood. You meant the physical SRAM addresses, keeping code 0x0000 → SRAM
  0x4000 and code 0x4000 → SRAM 0x0000 as now. That's fine logically. Data
  accesses would then have to go to SRAM 0x0000–0x3FFF, so the gate must output
  0 for data accesses. One NAND still can't do that, and keeping the swap costs
  an extra gate:

  Keeping the swap (code 0x0000 → SRAM 0x4000):
  - Output must be NOT A14 on fetches and 0 on data accesses, which is AND(NOT 
    A14, /RD, /WR).
  - Built from the 7410, that takes all three gates: inverter → 3-input NAND →
    inverter.
  - PSEN version: NOR(A14, PSEN) works for the 8052, but PSEN is high while the
    8052 is in reset. The output would be stuck at 0, so the ATmega couldn't
    load the ROM half. There's also no NOR chip on the board.

  Dropping the swap (code 0x0000 → SRAM 0x0000):
  - Output must be A14 on fetches and 1 on data accesses, which is NAND(NOT A14,
    /RD, /WR).
  - That takes two 7410 gates: an inverter, then the existing NAND. It's the fix
    I described before.

  Which physical half is which makes no difference to anything:
  - The ATmega goes through the same gate as the 8052. ATmega address X always
    reaches the same SRAM cell as 8052 code address X, whichever version is
    built.
  - The host code, the ROM image and the 8052 programs are identical either way.
    Only the physical layout inside the SRAM differs, and nothing ever sees it.

  So I recommend the two-gate version, with the swap dropped:
  - A14 → 7410 pins 1, 2 and 13. Pin 12 → pin 5. Pins 3 and 4 stay on /WR and
    /RD. Pin 6 → SRAM A14.
  - One gate remains spare (gate 3, pins 9, 10, 11 → 8).

✻ Churned for 13s · done 7:03 PM
❯ The reason I'm finding the two gate version sketchy is gate delay.            

⏺ The extra gate delay isn't a real problem. Working through the timing did turn
  up a different one, though. It's in the circuit already, and the inverter fix
  would make it worse.

  The extra gate delay: numbers below use Intel 8051 AC timing at 13.875 MHz
  (one clock = 72 ns) and 74LS worst-case delays (about 15 ns per gate). The OKI
  part's figures may differ slightly.
  - The inverter sits only on the A14 input, and A14 settles early in every
    cycle. On a code fetch, the 8052 needs data back about 255 ns after the
    address appears.
  - A14 through two gates takes about 30 ns, about the same as A0–A7 through the
    7475 latches. Add the SRAM's 70 ns access time and there's about 150 ns of
    margin.
  - The path that has to be fast, from /RD or /WR to SRAM A14, stays one gate
    either way. For a data read: one gate plus 70 ns SRAM access is about 85 ns,
    against roughly 195 ns available.

  The real problem: the end of a write.
  - SRAM A14 depends on /WR through one gate, the NAND.
  - SRAM /WE depends on /WR through two gates (7408 gates 4 and 3, the second
    added deliberately as a delay).
  - At the start of a write that's correct: A14 settles before /WE goes low.
  - At the end it's backwards. When /WR goes high, SRAM A14 switches about one
    gate delay before /WE goes high. For roughly 15–20 ns the SRAM sees a
    different address with /WE still low, and it can write the data into the
    other half as well.
  - Today: this happens only for data writes at 0x4000 and up, which rarely
    occur. The stray copy lands in the code 0x4000 half.
  - With the inverter fix: it happens on every data write below 0x4000, the
    normal case. The stray copy lands in the ROM half at the same offset.
  - Adjusting delays can't fix it. Any signal triggered by /WR's edges needs to
    change before /WE at the start of a write and after /WE at the end.

  A sounder approach: switch on PSEN instead of /RD and /WR.
  - During a MOVX cycle PSEN stays high from well before /RD or /WR goes low
    until well after it goes high. By Intel timing, the next PSEN pulse comes at
    least about 150 ns later.
  - SRAM A14 = A14 OR PSEN: code fetches see A14 unchanged, and data accesses
    see 1. This gives the intended mapping with a single gate and no timing race
    on writes.
  - Problem: PSEN stays high while the 8052 is held in reset. During loading,
    SRAM A14 would be stuck at 1, so the ATmega couldn't write the ROM half. The
    ATmega would need its own way to override that, for example a free pin
    combined in through another gate. It also needs an OR gate, or the
    equivalent built from spare 7410 NANDs plus inverters.

  For the ROM dump: the current wiring is safe. The dumper writes only below
  0x4000, where the race does no harm today.

  Want me to work out a PSEN-based design with an ATmega override, using only
  the 7408 and 7410 you already have? Or build the dumper first and come back to
  the decoding?

✻ Crunched for 1m 1s · done 7:05 PM

❯ There must be a way to use PSEN, A14 and a signal from the ATMega and one     
  three input NAND.                                                             

⏺ Not with one 3-input NAND. With PSEN, A14 and an ATmega signal, it takes all
  three gates of the 7410, so still no new chips.

  Why one NAND can't do it:
  1. During code fetches, the output has to follow A14. So A14, or its inverse,
     is one input, and the other inputs must be 1 during fetches.
  2. A NAND can only force its output to 1. So data accesses always go to the
     half where the output is 1.
  3. For data RAM to share the code 0x4000 half, fetches with A14 = 1 must
     produce 1. So the A14 input has to be NOT A14.
  4. The forcing input must be 0 during data accesses and 1 during fetches. That
     makes it NOT PSEN.
  5. Neither inverse exists on the board, and the ATmega signal can't stand in
     for either. It only changes when the ATmega sets it, while A14 and PSEN
     change on every bus cycle.

  Three-gate version: SRAM A14 = A14 OR (PSEN AND S), where S is the ATmega
  signal.
  - ATmega loading (S = 0): SRAM A14 = A14, so ATmega address X is code address
    X. The ROM image still starts at 0, and both halves are reachable.
  - 8052 running (S = 1): fetches give A14 unchanged. Data accesses (PSEN high)
    give 1, the same half as code 0x4000. That's the intended mapping.
  - No write race: /RD and /WR no longer feed the A14 logic. PSEN stays high
    from well before each data read or write until well after it.

  Wiring on the 7410:

  ┌──────┬────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │ Gate │             Inputs             │    Output    │    Function     │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ 1    │ pin 1 = PSEN, pins 2 and 13 =  │ pin 12       │ NOT (PSEN AND   │
  │      │ S                              │              │ S)              │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ 3    │ pins 9, 10, 11 = A14           │ pin 8        │ NOT A14         │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ 2    │ pin 3 = pin 8, pin 4 = pin 12, │ pin 6 → SRAM │ A14 OR (PSEN    │
  │      │  pin 5 = +5 V                  │  A14         │ AND S)          │
  └──────┴────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────────┘

  - /RD and /WR are taken off 7410 pins 3 and 4. They stay on the 7408 for /OE
    and /WE.
  - S would come from ATmega PD2 (pin 4), the only free pin. The ROM dumper also
    wanted PD2 for EA. Doing the dump first, on the current wiring, avoids the
    clash.
  - ATmega host changes: drive PD2 low while loading and reading back, and high
    just before releasing the 8052's reset.

  The cost: the fetch timing gets tight.
  - Code fetches from the ROM half now have to wait for PSEN: two gate delays
    (about 30 ns worst-case for 74LS) plus 70 ns SRAM access, about 100 ns after
    PSEN goes low.
  - By Intel timing at 13.875 MHz, the 8052 needs instruction data 111 ns after
    PSEN goes low. That's about 10 ns of margin at worst-case gate delays, or
    about 25 ns at typical ones.
  - This hits every fetch from the ROM half, because SRAM A14 switches back to
    the RAM half every time PSEN goes high between fetches. Any PSEN-based
    scheme behaves this way.
  - The current /RD and /WR scheme doesn't have this problem, because it only
    switches on data accesses, which have a 195 ns budget. That's why it has the
    write race instead.

  The 111 ns figure is Intel's. The OKI datasheet's figure for PSEN low to valid
  instruction in decides whether this is safe. Do you have that datasheet
  handy, or should I look up the figure?

✻ Sautéed for 1m 12s · done 7:07 PM

❯ AND(ORIG_A14, PSEN, ROMWR)                                                    

⏺ Taken literally, AND(A14, PSEN, ROMWR) doesn't work. The OR form in your notes
  does, though, and using /ROMWR as the ATmega signal is a good idea: it needs
  no spare pin.

  The literal AND:

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────┬──────┬────────┬────────┐
  │          Access          │ A14 │ PSEN │ /ROMWR │ Output │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Code fetch 0x0000–0x3FFF │ 0   │ 0    │ 1      │ 0      │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────┼──────┼────────┼────────┤
  │ Code fetch 0x4000–0x7FFF │ 1   │ 0    │ 1      │ 0      │
  └──────────────────────────┴─────┴──────┴────────┴────────┘

  PSEN is 0 on every fetch, so both code halves land in the same SRAM half. Code
  0x0000 and code 0x4000 would be the same memory.

  The OR form from docs/RAM:ROM Shadowing.txt: SRAM A14 = A14 OR (PSEN AND 
  /ROMWR), where /ROMWR is the ATmega's write strobe on PD4, active low.

  ┌──────────────────────────┬────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Situation         │ Output │               Result                │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 code fetch          │ A14    │ code 0x0000 and code 0x4000 reach   │
  │                          │        │ different halves                    │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 data access         │ 1      │ same half as code 0x4000, as        │
  │                          │        │ intended                            │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega write (/ROMWR     │ A14    │ ATmega address X is code address X  │
  │ low)                     │        │                                     │
  ├──────────────────────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega read (PSEN and    │ 1      │ only the RAM half can be read       │
  │ /ROMWR high)             │        │                                     │
  └──────────────────────────┴────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  Fix for ATmega reads: add the ATmega's read strobe, /PRERD on PD5, as a third
  input. SRAM A14 = A14 OR (PSEN AND /ROMWR AND /PRERD). That uses the spare
  third input on 7410 gate 1, so it's still three gates:

  ┌──────┬──────────────────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │ Gate │            Inputs            │   Output   │      Function       │
  ├──────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │      │ pin 1 = PSEN, pin 2 = /ROMWR │            │ NOT (PSEN AND       │
  │ 1    │  (PD4), pin 13 = /PRERD      │ pin 12     │ /ROMWR AND /PRERD)  │
  │      │ (PD5)                        │            │                     │
  ├──────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 3    │ pins 9, 10, 11 = A14         │ pin 8      │ NOT A14             │
  ├──────┼──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 2    │ pin 3 = pin 8, pin 4 = pin   │ pin 6 →    │ A14 OR (PSEN AND    │
  │      │ 12, pin 5 = +5 V             │ SRAM A14   │ /ROMWR AND /PRERD)  │
  └──────┴──────────────────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  /RD and /WR come off 7410 pins 3 and 4. They still feed the 7408 for /OE and
  /WE.

  Advantages:
  - No new ATmega pin. PD2 stays free for EA, so the ROM dumper works with this
    wiring too.
  - No host code changes. ATmega address X stays code address X.
  - No write race for the 8052. PSEN stays steady across its data reads and
    writes.

  Caveats:
  1. ATmega writes now have the same race at their end. When /ROMWR goes high,
     SRAM A14 switches one gate delay before /WE does. A write below 0x4000 can
     leave a stray copy at the same offset in the RAM half.
     - It's harmless as long as the ATmega writes addresses in ascending order.
       The RAM half (0x4000 and up) gets written after the ROM half, so any
       stray copies are overwritten.
     - The RAM test and the image loader both already write in ascending order.
  2. Fetch timing is tight. ROM-half fetches wait on PSEN through two gates plus
     70 ns SRAM access, about 100 ns at worst-case 74LS delays. Intel timing
     allows 111 ns. The OKI datasheet's figure for PSEN low to valid instruction
     decides whether that's safe.

  Should I look up the OKI timing figure, update the KiCad schematic with this
  wiring, or both?

✻ Cogitated for 32s · done 7:08 PM

❯ Let's go back the curent circuit, but with A14 through a NOT gate first.      
  Timing problems>?                                                             

⏺ Most of it is fine. The one timing problem is at the end of each 8052 data
  write, and adding the NOT gate makes it hit the common case.

  Circuit: SRAM A14 = NAND(NOT A14, /RD, /WR), with the NOT built from spare
  7410 gate 1 or 3.
  - Code fetch: SRAM A14 = A14.
  - Data access: SRAM A14 = 1, the same half as code 0x4000.
  - ATmega access: SRAM A14 = A14. The 8052's /RD and /WR stay high, so ATmega
    address X is code address X, and both halves can be read and written.

  Timing case by case (Intel 8051 figures at 13.875 MHz, 74LS worst-case gate
  delays, CY62256-70 SRAM):

  Case: Code fetch
  Path: A14 → NOT → NAND, then SRAM access
  Time needed: about 30 + 70 = 100 ns from address valid
  Time available: 255 ns
  Result: fine. /RD and /WR don't switch during fetches.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Data read
  Path: /RD → NAND, then SRAM access
  Time needed: about 15 + 70 = 85 ns
  Time available: 195 ns
  Result: fine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Data read, end
  Path: SRAM A14 switches back about when /OE goes high
  Time needed: —
  Time available: 8052 needs 0 ns data hold
  Result: harmless. The 8052 has already captured the data.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Data write, start
  Path: SRAM A14 after 1 gate, /WE after 2 gates (7408 gates 4 and 3)
  Time needed: address before /WE
  Time available: SRAM needs 0 ns address setup
  Result: fine, by one gate delay
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Data write, end
  Path: SRAM A14 switches after 1 gate, /WE goes high after 2
  Time needed: address held until /WE is high
  Time available: SRAM needs 0 ns address hold
  Result: violated

  The write-end race in detail:
  - When /WR goes high, SRAM A14 drops from 1 to 0 about 3–23 ns before /WE goes
    high. For that time, the SRAM sees a ROM-half address with /WE still low.
  - The 8052 holds the data on the bus for about 22 ns after /WR goes high. The
    byte can therefore be written into the ROM half, at the same offset as the
    intended address.
  - Today: this only affects data writes at 0x4000 and up. With the NOT gate, it
    affects every data write below 0x4000.
  - Severity: the window is shorter than the SRAM's minimum write pulse, about
    50 ns for the -70 part. Whether a stray write happens isn't defined. It
    might never show up, or it might corrupt the program now and then.

  Options:
  1. Build it and test it. Run an 8052 program that writes all of data RAM
     thousands of times. Then have the ATmega hold reset and compare the ROM
     half against the original image. A clean result over many passes is good
     evidence the race doesn't happen in practice with your chips.
  2. Delay only the rising edge of /WR into the NAND. Put about 1 kΩ in series
     with 7410 pin 3, about 100 pF from pin 3 to ground, and a small-signal
     diode across the resistor, cathode toward /WR.
     - Falling edges pass straight through the diode.
     - Rising edges are slowed by roughly 50–100 ns, so SRAM A14 switches back
       after /WE has gone high.
     - It costs three passive parts. The KiCad schematic and PCB would need them
       too.
  3. Switch to the PSEN-based version. It has no write race, but the fetch
     timing is tight (about 100 ns needed against 111 ns available), and it uses
     all three 7410 gates.

  I suggest option 1 first, keeping option 2 as the fix if the test finds
  corruption. The NOT gate is one wire change, and the test is cheap to write.
  Want me to write that write-stress test?

✻ Cogitated for 53s · done 7:11 PM

❯ Remember the OE/WE signals are also buffered through two and gates on the     
  7408                                                                          

⏺ I did include them. The two AND gates on /WE are what cause the race at the
  end of the write. A chain of gates delays both edges of the signal passing
  through it:

  ┌───────────┬────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │           │ SRAM A14   │  SRAM /WE    │                │              │
  │ Edge of   │ (through   │  (through    │  Requirement   │    Result    │
  │    /WR    │ the NAND)  │ 7408 gates 4 │                │              │
  │           │            │    and 3)    │                │              │
  ├───────────┼────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Falling   │ switches   │ goes low     │ address        │ met, thanks  │
  │ (write    │ after 1    │ after 2      │ settles before │ to the extra │
  │ starts)   │ gate       │ gates        │  /WE goes low  │  gate        │
  ├───────────┼────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Rising    │ switches   │ goes high    │ address holds  │ violated,    │
  │ (write    │ back after │ after 2      │ until /WE is   │ because of   │
  │ ends)     │  1 gate    │ gates        │ high           │ the extra    │
  │           │            │              │                │ gate         │
  └───────────┴────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────┘

  The delay gate does what your post says at the start of a write. But /WE's
  rising edge comes through the same two gates, so it also arrives a gate later
  than SRAM A14's change at the end.

  One correction to "two AND gates" for /OE. From the schematic, after your /RD
  and /WR fix:
  - 8052 /RD → 7408 pin 5 → gate 2 → pin 6 → SRAM /OE. That's one gate.
  - 8052 /PSEN and the ATmega's /PRERD → gate 1 (pins 1 and 2) → pin 3 → pin 4 →
    gate 2 → /OE. That's two gates.
  - 8052 /WR → pin 12 → gate 4 → pin 11 → pins 9 and 10 → gate 3 → pin 8 → SRAM
    /WE. That's two gates.

  For data reads, SRAM A14 and /OE therefore both switch one gate after /RD.
  That's harmless in both directions:
  - Start of a read: the SRAM sees the right address after about 15 ns. Its 70
    ns access time is counted from then, about 85 ns in all, well inside the 195
    ns available.
  - End of a read: the 8052 has already captured the data when /RD goes high, so
    a change of address at the same moment doesn't matter.

  If the breadboard puts 8052 /RD through two gates as well, reads are still
  fine. Only the end of writes needs attention. That's where the write-stress
  test (option 1) or the diode-and-capacitor delay on the NAND's /WR input
  (option 2) comes in.

✻ Brewed for 15s · done 7:14 PM

❯ Wait, but in our fixed circuit the SRAM A14 would be through two NAND gates.  

⏺ Only the A14 input goes through two gates. /WR goes straight into the final
  NAND, so the write-end race is unchanged:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬───────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Path            │ Gates │    Changes during a data write?     │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A14 → NOT → NAND → SRAM   │ 2     │ no. The 8052 holds A14 steady for   │
  │ A14                       │       │ the whole cycle.                    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ /WR → NAND → SRAM A14     │ 1     │ yes, on both edges of /WR           │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ /WR → 7408 gate 4 → gate  │ 2     │ yes, on both edges                  │
  │ 3 → SRAM /WE              │       │                                     │
  └───────────────────────────┴───────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  During a write, the only input to the NAND that changes is /WR, which passes
  through one gate. A14 settled long before. The NOT gate only adds delay when
  A14 itself changes, which happens at the start of each bus cycle with plenty
  of time to spare.

  If /WR were put through the NOT path as well, so that /WR → SRAM A14 took two
  gates to match /WE, the delays would be equal. Then both the start and the end
  of each write become races decided by small differences between individual
  gates, which is worse than a guaranteed one-gate lead at the start.

  The race at the end of a write is caused by the A14 decode taking its timing
  from /WR, whatever gates it passes through. To fix it, either the decode stops
  depending on /WR (the PSEN version), or /WR's rising edge reaches the NAND
  later than it reaches /WE (the diode, resistor and capacitor delay).
  Otherwise, test whether it ever causes a problem in practice.
