❯ Okay, lay out the PSEN version again?                                         

⏺ Here is the PSEN version in full, using /ROMWR and /PRERD as the ATmega's
  control signals.

  Function: SRAM A14 = A14 OR (PSEN AND /ROMWR AND /PRERD)
  - PSEN is the 8052's code-fetch strobe.
  - /ROMWR and /PRERD are the ATmega's write and read strobes, on PD4 and PD5.
    All three are active low.

  Wiring on the 7410:

  ┌──────┬─────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │ Gate │           Inputs            │    Output    │      Function      │
  ├──────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │      │ pin 1 = 8052 /PSEN, pin 2 = │              │ NOT (PSEN AND      │
  │ 1    │  /ROMWR (PD4), pin 13 =     │ pin 12       │ /ROMWR AND /PRERD) │
  │      │ /PRERD (PD5)                │              │                    │
  ├──────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 3    │ pins 9, 10, 11 = A14 (8052  │ pin 8        │ NOT A14            │
  │      │ P2.6 and ATmega PD7)        │              │                    │
  ├──────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 2    │ pin 3 = pin 8, pin 4 = pin  │ pin 6 → SRAM │ A14 OR (PSEN AND   │
  │      │ 12, pin 5 = +5 V            │  A14 (pin 1) │ /ROMWR AND /PRERD) │
  └──────┴─────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────────┘

  - 8052 /RD and /WR come off the 7410 (pins 4 and 3). They still feed the 7408
    for SRAM /OE and /WE.
  - The 7408 doesn't change.
  - All three 7410 gates are now in use.

  Behaviour:

  ┌───────────────┬──────┬────────┬────────┬───────┬────────────────────────┐
  │   Situation   │ PSEN │ /ROMWR │ /PRERD │ SRAM  │         Result         │
  │               │      │        │        │  A14  │                        │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 code     │      │        │        │       │ code 0x0000 and code   │
  │ fetch         │ 0    │ 1      │ 1      │ A14   │ 0x4000 reach different │
  │               │      │        │        │       │  halves                │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 data     │ 1    │ 1      │ 1      │ 1     │ the RAM half, same as  │
  │ read or write │      │        │        │       │ code 0x4000            │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega write  │ 1    │ 0      │ 1      │ A14   │ ATmega address X =     │
  │               │      │        │        │       │ code address X         │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega read   │ 1    │ 1      │ 0      │ A14   │ ATmega can read both   │
  │               │      │        │        │       │ halves                 │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Idle, or 8052 │      │        │        │       │ the SRAM isn't being   │
  │  in reset     │ 1    │ 1      │ 1      │ 1     │ accessed, so it        │
  │               │      │        │        │       │ doesn't matter         │
  └───────────────┴──────┴────────┴────────┴───────┴────────────────────────┘

  Timing (Intel 8051 figures at 13.875 MHz, 74LS worst-case gate delays,
  CY62256-70 SRAM):

  Case: Fetch from the ROM half (A14 = 0)
  Time needed: about 30 ns (2 gates after PSEN goes low) + 70 ns SRAM = 100 ns
  Time available: 111 ns after PSEN goes low
  Notes: Tight. About 86 ns with typical gate delays. Happens on every ROM-half
    fetch.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Fetch from the RAM half (A14 = 1)
  Time needed: SRAM A14 stays 1 and never switches
  Time available: —
  Notes: fine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: 8052 data read
  Time needed: SRAM A14 steady at 1 throughout
  Time available: 195 ns
  Notes: fine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: 8052 data write
  Time needed: SRAM A14 steady at 1 throughout
  Time available: —
  Notes: no race: PSEN stays steady before, during and after the write
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: ATmega write, both edges
  Time needed: SRAM A14 and /WE each 2 gates behind /ROMWR
  Time available: —
  Notes: a race at both edges, with stray copies landing only in the RAM half,
  at
    the same offset. Harmless when writing in ascending address order.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: ATmega read
  Time needed: the ATmega waits about 375 ns before sampling
  Time available: about 30 + 70 ns needed
  Notes: fine

  What changes elsewhere:
  - ATmega host: nothing. The loader and the RAM test already write in ascending
    order. PD2 stays free for EA.
  - KiCad: rewire 7410 gates 1, 2 and 3 as above and take /RD and /WR off U6.
    Then update the PCB, reroute and regenerate the Gerbers.

  Compared with the NOT + NAND version:

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │                 │      NOT + NAND       │             PSEN             │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ 7410 gates used │ 2                     │ 3                            │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 write race │ yes, every data write │ none                         │
  │                 │  below 0x4000         │                              │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ ROM-half fetch  │ large (about 150 ns)  │ tight (about 10 ns at        │
  │ margin          │                       │ worst-case delays)           │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Race on ATmega  │ none                  │ yes, harmless if writing in  │
  │ writes          │                       │ ascending order              │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  Deciding factor: the OKI datasheet's figure for PSEN low to valid instruction
  in, at your clock speed. If it's near Intel's 111 ns, the PSEN version is
  marginal on paper, though it would probably work with real gate delays. If OKI
  allows more time, the PSEN version is the cleaner design.

✻ Sautéed for 27s · done 7:18 PM

❯ I don't like this. So many gates.                                             

⏺ Then the simplest option is to drop the A14 logic altogether and wire SRAM A14
  straight to bus A14. That's zero gates, and the 7410 can come out.

  Result: one shared 32 KB space for code and data:

  ┌───────────────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    8052 access    │  SRAM    │                   Role                   │
  │                   │   half   │                                          │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Code              │ lower    │ ROM, the program image                   │
  │ 0x0000–0x3FFF     │          │                                          │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Code              │ upper    │ RAM, runnable as code                    │
  │ 0x4000–0x7FFF     │          │                                          │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Data              │ upper    │ RAM, the same cells as code              │
  │ 0x4000–0x7FFF     │          │ 0x4000–0x7FFF                            │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Data              │ lower    │ the ROM image. Software must not write   │
  │ 0x0000–0x3FFF     │          │ here.                                    │
  └───────────────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The 7408 already combines /PSEN and /RD into /OE, which is the standard way to
  give an 8051 a shared code-and-data memory. The NAND was there only to move
  data RAM down to address 0.

  What you gain:
  - No timing races. A14 comes straight from the 8052 and stays steady for the
    whole bus cycle. The 8052 write race and the ATmega write race both
    disappear.
  - Best fetch timing. There's no gate delay on A14 at all.
  - Simpler WS2812 plan. Generated code written at data address N runs at code
    address N, with no translation.
  - ATmega host: no changes. ATmega address X is still code address X.

  What you give up:
  - Data RAM starts at 0x4000, not 0. In SDCC this is one flag, --xram-loc 
    0x4000. Assembly uses DPTR values from 0x4000 up.
  - No write protection. A buggy data write below 0x4000 corrupts the program
    image. That's recoverable: an ATmega reset reloads it.
  - 8-bit MOVX @R0/@R1 accesses take the address high byte from P2. P2 must be
    set to 0x40 or above first. SDCC's __pdata handles this if told the page.

  Hardware changes:
  - Breadboard: remove the 7410. Connect SRAM pin 1 (A14) straight to the A14
    line (8052 P2.6 and ATmega PD7).
  - KiCad: delete U6, connect /A14 to U4 pin 1, update the PCB, reroute and
    regenerate the Gerbers.

  ROM dumper: it still works. The dumper's code goes at code 0x4000, in the
  upper half. The dump has to go to data addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF, which is now
  the lower half, on top of the reset stub. That's fine once the stub has run.
  The ATmega reads it back from 0x0000–0x3FFF as before.

  If you later move to a real EPROM, the EPROM would sit at 0x0000–0x3FFF,
  selected when A14 is low, with the SRAM at 0x4000 and up. One inverter does
  that decode, and the memory map stays the same, so code written now carries
  over unchanged.

✻ Worked for 23s · done 7:19 PM

※ recap: Goal: make the 8052 LED sign's SRAM memory map work. The task now is the A14 decoding, and I proposed removing the 7410 to wire SRAM A14 straight to bus A14, putting data RAM at 0x4000. Next: decide whether to adopt that.

❯ At what pooint does the logical error happen in "Ram/ROM Shadowing.txt"       

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The error is at line 115: "If we invert the memory spaces, it's literally just
  NAND(ORIG_A14, ~RD, ~WR)!" Everything up to line 113 is correct.

  Lines 84–100, correct:
  - The left-hand table (lines 86–93) says: code fetches give A14, and data
    accesses give 1. That's A14 OR NOT ~RD OR NOT ~WR (line 96).
  - The right-hand table repeats the same rows with the A14 input column
    inverted. That shows the function equals NAND(NOT A14, ~RD, ~WR), which is
    "NOT A NAND B NAND C" (line 100).
  - Lines 106–113 restate that as a NAND truth table, where the first input is
    NOT A14.

  Line 115, the mistake:
  - Removing the NOT inverts the gate's input.
  - "Inverting the memory spaces", meaning swapping which SRAM half is which,
    would invert the gate's output.
  - The two aren't the same. Inverting the output turns the forced 1 for data
    accesses into a forced 0. A NAND can't force a 0.
  - Removing the input NOT keeps data forced to 1, but code fetches now give NOT
    A14. So 1 is now the value for code 0x0000–0x3FFF. Data RAM ends up
    attached to the ROM half instead of code 0x4000–0x7FFF.

  You can see it by checking the A14 = 0 rows of NAND(A14, ~RD, ~WR) on line
  115:
  - Code fetch at 0x0000 (A14 = 0, ~RD = ~WR = 1) → 1
  - Any data access (~RD or ~WR = 0) → 1

  Both give 1, so they reach the same SRAM half.

  Line 129 has the same mistake, plus another. AND(ORIG_A14, PSEN, ROMWR)
  doesn't match its own table on lines 120–127. Rows 124–127 (first input 0)
  should all give 1, but an AND gives 0 for them. Even apart from the inverted
  A14, that table would need an OR form, not an AND.

  Smaller inconsistency: line 21 ("On RAM writes, A14 should always be zero")
  contradicts line 18 ("on RAM reads, A14 should always be set"). The truth
  tables follow line 18, so line 21 looks like a slip that never fed into the
  design.

✻ Brewed for 30s · done 7:25 PM

※ recap: You're getting the 8052 LED sign's SRAM memory map right, and found the decode error at line 115 of the shadowing notes. Next, choose a fix: probably wiring SRAM A14 straight to the bus and removing the 7410.

❯ What if we flipper ~WR to also go through only one AND gate to the WE?        

⏺ That swaps one guaranteed violation for two races that come down to small
  differences between individual gates. It doesn't fix the problem.

  Change: connect 7408 pin 11 (gate 4's output) straight to SRAM /WE (pin 27),
  bypassing gate 3. Then /WR reaches both SRAM A14 (through the NAND) and /WE
  (through gate 4) via one gate each.

  Both edges become races (typical and maximum 74LS delays):

  Edge: /WR falls (start)
  SRAM A14 (74LS10 NAND): output goes high: typical 9 ns, max 15 ns
  SRAM /WE (74LS08 AND): output goes low: typical 10 ns, max 20 ns
  Requirement: A14 before /WE
  Result: about 1 ns margin typical. Not guaranteed.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Edge: /WR rises (end)
  SRAM A14 (74LS10 NAND): output goes low: typical 10 ns, max 15 ns
  SRAM /WE (74LS08 AND): output goes high: typical 8 ns, max 15 ns
  Requirement: /WE before A14
  Result: about 2 ns margin typical. Not guaranteed.

  - Today there's a one-gate guaranteed margin at the start and a guaranteed
    violation at the end.
  - With the bypass, each edge works only if each particular chip happens to
    switch the right way round. That could change with temperature, supply
    voltage, or a replacement chip.
  - A race at the start is also a problem. The 8052 puts its data on the bus
    about 12 ns before /WR falls. If /WE goes low before SRAM A14 settles, the
    byte can land in the ROM half.

  Why adjusting delays can't fix it: SRAM A14 and /WE both take their timing
  from /WR, and the two edges need opposite orderings:
  - Start of write: A14 must change before /WE.
  - End of write: A14 must change after /WE.

  Any delay that's the same on both edges can satisfy only one of these, or
  neither if it splits the difference. A fix needs one of these:
  - Different delays on the two edges: the diode, resistor and capacitor on the
    NAND's /WR input, which slows only the rising edge.
  - A decode that doesn't depend on /WR: the PSEN version.
  - No decode at all: SRAM A14 wired straight to bus A14, the zero-gate
    shared-memory version. A14 then never changes during a bus cycle, so there's
    nothing to race. Gate 3 can keep providing the extra delay at the start of
    writes, and it no longer causes a problem at the end.

✻ Sautéed for 22s · done 7:45 PM

❯ Please don't suggest not splitting the RAM.                                   

  Ran 1 shell command, wrote 1 memory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Understood. I won't suggest dropping the split again, and I've saved that to
  memory.

  With the split kept, the NOT + NAND circuit is the base. For the race at the
  end of writes, three options remain:

  1. Delay only the rising edge of /WR into the NAND. No extra gates.
     - Parts: about 1 kΩ in series between 8052 /WR and 7410 pin 3, about 100 pF
       from pin 3 to ground, and a small-signal diode such as a 1N4148 across
       the resistor, cathode toward /WR.
     - Falling edge: the diode pulls pin 3 low at once, so SRAM A14 still
       switches one gate ahead of /WE at the start of a write.
     - Rising edge: pin 3 charges slowly through the resistor, roughly 50–100
       ns. /WE (two gates, about 30 ns) goes high well before SRAM A14 switches
       back.
     - Reads are unaffected, because /RD feeds a separate NAND input.
  2. Build without a fix and test. An 8052 program writes all of data RAM
     thousands of times. Then the ATmega holds reset and compares the ROM half
     against the original image. If corruption shows up, add option 1.
  3. The PSEN version. No write race, but it uses all three 7410 gates and its
     fetch timing is tight.

  I suggest option 1. It's three passive parts on one existing net, no gates,
  and it fixes the race by design rather than by luck. Doing option 2 as well
  would confirm the fix works.

  │   Situation   │ PSEN │ /ROMWR │ /PRERD │ SRAM  │         Result         │
  │               │      │        │        │  A14  │                        │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤

  │               │      │        │        │       │  halves                │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 data     │ 1    │ 1      │ 1      │ 1     │ the RAM half, same as  │
  │ read or write │      │        │        │       │ code 0x4000            │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega write  │ 1    │ 0      │ 1      │ A14   │ ATmega address X =     │
  │               │      │        │        │       │ code address X         │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega read   │ 1    │ 1      │ 0      │ A14   │ the ATmega can read    │
  │               │      │        │        │       │ both halves            │
  ├───────────────┼──────┼────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Idle, or 8052 │      │        │        │       │ the SRAM isn't being   │
  │  in reset     │ 1    │ 1      │ 1      │ 1     │ accessed, so it        │
  │               │      │        │        │       │ doesn't matter         │
  └───────────────┴──────┴────────┴────────┴───────┴────────────────────────┘

  Timing (Intel 8051 figures at 13.875 MHz, 74LS worst-case gate delays,
  CY62256-70 SRAM):

  Case: Fetch from the ROM half (A14 = 0)
  Time needed: 2 gates after PSEN goes low (about 30 ns) + 70 ns SRAM = about
  100
    ns, or about 86 ns typical
  Time available: 111 ns
  Result: tight, on every ROM-half fetch
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Fetch from the RAM half (A14 = 1)
  Time needed: SRAM A14 stays 1
  Time available: —
  Result: fine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: 8052 data read
  Time needed: SRAM A14 steady at 1
  Time available: 195 ns
  Result: fine
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: 8052 data write
  Time needed: SRAM A14 steady at 1. PSEN doesn't change around /WR.
  Time available: —
  Result: no race
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: ATmega write
  Time needed: SRAM A14 and /WE both 2 gates behind /ROMWR
  Time available: —
  Result: a race at both edges, with stray copies landing in the RAM half at the

    same offset. Harmless when writing in ascending order, which the loader and
    the RAM test already do.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: ATmega read
  Time needed: the ATmega samples about 375 ns after /PRERD goes low
  Time available: about 100 ns needed
  Result: fine

  Elsewhere:
  - ATmega host: no changes. PD2 stays free for EA.
  - KiCad: rewire U6 as above and take /RD and /WR off it. Then update the PCB,
    reroute, and regenerate the Gerbers.

  The open question: the OKI datasheet's figure for PSEN low to valid
  instruction in. If it's about 111 ns like Intel's, ROM-half fetches have
  roughly 10 ns of worst-case margin.

✻ Cooked for 12s · done 7:48 PM

❯ This seems complex when all I want to do is basically flip A14 on either ROM  
  or RAM accesses.                                                              

⏺ That's an XOR, and with PSEN as the second input it's a single gate:

  SRAM A14 = A14 XOR PSEN

  ┌──────────────┬────────────┬───────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Access    │    PSEN    │ SRAM  │               Result               │
  │              │            │  A14  │                                    │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 code    │ 0          │ A14   │ code 0x0000 → lower half (ROM),    │
  │ fetch        │            │       │ code 0x4000 → upper half (RAM)     │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8052 data    │ 1          │ NOT   │ data 0x0000–0x3FFF → upper half,   │
  │ access       │            │ A14   │ the same cells as code 0x4000      │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼───────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ATmega read  │ 1 (8052 in │ NOT   │ ATmega address X = 8052 data       │
  │ or write     │  reset)    │ A14   │ address X                          │
  └──────────────┴────────────┴───────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  Properties:
  - One gate. No ATmega control signals, and /RD and /WR stay out of the decode.
  - No write races. PSEN stays steady through every 8052 data write and every
    ATmega write.
  - The ROM is write-protected from data addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF. Data addresses
    0x4000–0x7FFF reach the ROM half. Data RAM is 16 KB at 0x0000, so software
    just never uses them.

  Changes:
  - Hardware: a 74LS86 (quad XOR) replaces the 7410. Gate 1: pin 1 = A14 (8052
    P2.6 and ATmega PD7), pin 2 = 8052 /PSEN, pin 3 → SRAM A14 (pin 1). Power on
    pins 14 and 7 as usual. Three gates are left spare. The 7410's spare gates
    can't stand in, because an XOR built from NANDs takes four.
  - ATmega host: one change. The ATmega looks like a data access, so the ROM
    half is at ATmega address 0x4000. The loader and the verify step need to add
    0x4000 to the address. The RAM test is unaffected.
  - KiCad: replace U6 with a 74LS86, wire as above, update the PCB and reroute.

  The cost is fetch timing:
  - SRAM A14 flips when PSEN goes low, on every fetch from either half. It flips
    back each time PSEN goes high between fetches.
  - A 74LS86 is slower than a NAND: up to about 30 ns worst case. Add 70 ns of
    SRAM access and that's about 100 ns, against Intel's 111 ns budget after
    PSEN goes low. That's about 10 ns of worst-case margin, or about 25 ns with
    typical delays.
  - Every PSEN-based decode is this tight. That OKI datasheet figure for PSEN
    low to valid instruction in is still the thing to check.

  For the ROM dump this works too: the dumper's code goes at code 0x4000 (ATmega
  address 0x0000), and the dump goes to data 0x0000 and up, which is the same
  half, so the dump would overwrite the dumper. So either do the dump before
  this change, or have the dumper store its output starting above its own code.

✻ Churned for 29s · done 7:51 PM

❯ I'm using logic gates a lot here -can I actually just wire some of these      
  signals together?                                                             

⏺ Partly. Active-low strobes can be joined with a plain wire if every driver on
  the line can only pull it low and nothing ever drives it high hard. That's a
  "wired-AND": the line is low if any device pulls it low. Push-pull outputs
  can't be joined, and OR, XOR or NAND can't be made by wiring at all.

  Which signals qualify:

  Signal: 8052 /RD and /WR (P3.7, P3.6)
  Output type: 8051 "quasi-bidirectional" port: strong pull-down, weak pull-up
  Can share a wire?: Yes. Its high is weak, apart from a brief strong pulse of
    about 2 clocks when the pin goes high.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Signal: ATmega /PRERD and /PREWR (PD5, PD4)
  Output type: push-pull, but software can make them open-drain
  Can share a wire?: Yes, if the host only ever drives them low or leaves them
  as
    inputs
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Signal: 8052 /PSEN
  Output type: push-pull
  Can share a wire?: No
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Signal: 74LS gate outputs
  Output type: push-pull
  Can share a wire?: No

  What it saves:
  - SRAM /WE: wire 8052 /WR and ATmega /PREWR together and connect them straight
    to SRAM pin 27. That removes 7408 gates 3 and 4.
  - SRAM /OE: wire 8052 /RD and ATmega /PRERD together. /OE = AND(/PSEN,
    combined /RD), which is one gate instead of two.

  Combined with the XOR decode, the whole memory interface is one XOR gate plus 
  one AND gate.

  Conditions:
  1. The host must never drive PD4 or PD5 high. Each strobe becomes: set DDR bit
     = output with PORT = 0 to assert it, DDR bit = input to release it. The
     current code drives them high after loading ("Write and Read signals should
     also be held high"). That would fight the 8052 every time it pulled /RD or
     /WR low, so it has to change.
  2. Pull-ups: add about 4.7 kΩ from each shared line to +5 V. While the 8052 is
     in reset only its weak pull-up holds the line high, which would make the
     ATmega's strobes slow to go back high. The 8052 can sink 1 mA comfortably.
  3. Only with a decode that ignores /WR, like the XOR. The 7408's extra gate
     exists to delay /WE until SRAM A14 has settled. With the NAND decode,
     connecting /WR straight to /WE would make the start of every write a race
     as well. With the XOR decode, SRAM A14 depends only on A14 and PSEN, which
     are settled long before /WR goes low, so no delay is needed.
  4. Check the OKI port type. The 80C51-compatible CMOS parts use
     quasi-bidirectional ports on P1–P3. The OKI datasheet should confirm P3.6
     and P3.7 work this way.

  You're already doing this: the 8052 and the ATmega share the address lines,
  data lines and ALE with no gates. That works because one side is always
  high-impedance or in reset. A wired-AND on /RD and /WR follows the same rule,
  just applied while both sides are active.

⏺ Yes. The dump works on the current circuit, and it's actually the best circuit
  for it:
  - Code at 0x4000 runs from the lower SRAM half, which the ATmega can load.
  - Data writes go to the upper half.
  - The write race only affects data writes at 0x4000 and up, and the dumper
    writes only below 0x4000.

  --disable-auto-clean as the upload script does. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The ROM dumper is written and every part builds, but it hasn't run on the
  board yet. It needs one wiring change first.

  Wiring:
  1. Move the 8052's EA wire (DIP pin 31) from ground to ATmega PD2 (pin 4).
  2. Put the 8052 back in the circuit.
  3. Make sure the breadboard's 8052 /RD and /WR go to the right 7408 inputs,
     matching your schematic fix. The dumper does data writes, so a swap would
     make them act as reads, and the dump would fail.

  To run it:
  pio run -e mcs51_romdump -t upload
  ~/.platformio/penv/bin/python scripts/capture_romdump.py
  The second command resets the ATmega and saves the dump to romdump.bin. Allow
  about 30 s in all: the RAM test takes a few seconds, the dump itself about 2.5
  s, and printing about 9 s.

  What happens:
  1. The ATmega writes LJMP 0x4000 at 0x0000 and the 193-byte dumper at 0x4000.
     EA is held low.
  2. It releases reset. The 8052 runs the jump, then the dumper.
  3. 10 ms later the ATmega raises EA. The dumper waits about 200 ms, then
     copies code 0x0000–0x3FFF into data 0x0000–0x3FFF, and turns on the LED on
     P1.0.
  4. After 2 s the ATmega puts the 8052 back into reset and prints 16 KB as hex
     between ROMDUMP BEGIN and ROMDUMP END.

  Reading the result:
  - If the dump starts with 02 40 00 (the jump), EA is latched at reset or the
    ROM is protected, and what you have is just a copy of the SRAM. The script
    warns about this.
  - If the LED never turns on, the 8052 isn't reaching the dumper's code.
  - Varied data that doesn't start with 02 40 00 is very likely the real mask
    ROM.

  What I added or changed:
  - mcs51/romdump/romdump.c: the dumper, in C.
  - scripts/mcs51_code_loc.py: passes --code-loc to the linker, from
    custom_code_loc.
  - scripts/capture_romdump.py: reads the serial output, saves the .bin and
    checks it.
  - scripts/integrate_mcs51_firmware.py and scripts/override_mcs51_upload.py:
    the environment names are now settings (custom_mcs51_env, custom_avr_env).
    The defaults keep the existing behaviour.
  - platformio.ini: new mcs51_romdump and avr_host_romdump environments.
  - avr_host/src/main.cpp: a ROMDUMP build option that drives EA, writes the
    jump at 0x0000, loads the program at 0x4000 and dumps the result. The normal
    build is unchanged.
  - docs/RAM:ROM Shadowing.txt: the XOR design notes you asked for, plus the
    line 115 explanation and the write race.

  The normal mcs51 → avr_host build still compiles, and nothing is committed.

⏺ The INT0 handler appears to service receive characters across 8 channels,
  reading status and data registers and stripping parity, and I'm now checking

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The program is firmware for an 8-channel serial communications controller,
  something like a terminal multiplexer or protocol converter. It isn't for an
  LED sign or any kind of display. That's an inference from the code's
  structure, not a name found anywhere in the ROM, so the details below are
  hedged where needed.

  Confirmed:
  - The dump is intact. The ROM's own checksum matches. About 12.7 KB is code.
    The rest is lookup tables and zero fill.
  - Startup (0x10E8):
    - Writes to external registers 0xFC and 0xFB and sets the stack.
    - Reads status registers 0xD9 and 0xDF to find out why it reset. The paths
      look like power-on vs. other kinds of restart.
    - Clears internal RAM, parking a few values in the timer registers during
      the clear.
    - Checksums the ROM. The configuration byte at 0x3FFD controls two startup
      options.
  - Timer 2 tick (0x0637): auto-reload every 384 machine cycles, feeding a chain
    of ÷10 counters used for timeouts. The real tick rate depends on the
    original crystal, which I don't know.
  - Receive interrupt (INT0, 0x0030):
    - Status register 0xD9 says what caused the interrupt, and 0xDE says which
      of 8 channels has data.
    - The character is read from 0xF0–0xF7, one register per channel. It's
      checked for parity and treated as 7-bit.
    - ASCII NAK (0x15) and SYN (0x16) get special handling. SYN is the idle/sync
      character of IBM-style "bisync" protocols, so this may be a synchronous
      link.
    - A per-channel bit is toggled in a byte written to 0xFB, plausibly an
      activity LED for each channel.
  - Main loop: for each channel 8 → 1, it reads that channel's state byte
    (internal RAM 0x30–0x37) and jumps through a 35-entry table at 0x1791. So
    each channel runs its own state machine. Six entries share one handler
    (0x2CEC), probably the "invalid state" catch-all. At startup every channel
    is set to the state for table entry 23, with a timeout of 10 ticks.
  - No hardware UART. SCON and SBUF are never used, so all the serial traffic
    goes through external chips.
  - Switches: P1 is read while P3.0 and P3.1 are pulsed, which looks like two
    banks of DIP switches multiplexed onto P1. P3.5 and P3.3 are driven as
    outputs.
  - Lookup tables: a 256-byte bit-reversal table at 0x308F, typical when serial
    data goes out MSB-first through some hardware and LSB-first through others.
    Bit masks at 0x318E (01 02 … 80) and 0x3196 (inverted).

  Implied original hardware:
  - Memory-mapped I/O at external addresses 0xFFD9–0xFFFF, reached with 8-bit
    MOVX @R1 while P2 stays at 0xFF:
    - 0xD9–0xDF: status registers.
    - 0xE0–0xE7: control registers. 0xE3 gets written about 35 times with any
      value, probably a watchdog kick.
    - 0xF0–0xF7: per-channel receive data.
    - 0xF8–0xFF: other registers, including 0xFB for the channel LEDs.
  - A second device at 0x0000–0x00FF, reached with MOVX @DPTR. Address 0x0002
    looks like a channel or register select, and 0x0007 like a command register.

  What it doesn't tell us: the product, the manufacturer, or the original clock
  speed. The code inside the chip's printed part number (MSM83C154-xxx) might
  identify the customer.

  Files:
  - romdump.bin is in the repository root, untracked.
  - The annotated disassembly (2,370 lines of listing) and the disassembler are
    in the scratchpad:
    - /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jamie-Development-8052LEDSign/224e1727-d140
      -423d-a562-f82bad5b3002/scratchpad/rom.lst
    - /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jamie-Development-8052LEDSign/224e1727-d140
      -423d-a562-f82bad5b3002/scratchpad/dis51.py

  I can copy the dump, listing and disassembler into the repository, say under
  docs/romdump/. Or I can dig into one area further, such as the 35 channel
  states or the transmit side.

✻ Crunched for 3m 21s · done 8:18 PM

❯ Could this be for music? Or MIDI?                                             

⏺ Possibly. A few details could fit MIDI. Checking the constants the program
  compares received bytes against, which should tell the two apart...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Very unlikely. The code expects ASCII text with parity, and normal MIDI
  messages would be rejected.

  1. It enforces parity, which MIDI doesn't use.
  - The receive interrupt checks the 8051's parity flag on each incoming byte.
    Bytes with an even number of 1 bits go to an error path (0x00A4), which sets
    a per-channel error flag at 0x23 and two global error bits.
  - So it expects 7 data bits plus an odd-parity bit in bit 7. That's standard
    for ASCII terminals and data links.
  - MIDI uses all 8 bits, with no parity. Bit 7 marks status bytes. Ordinary
    MIDI data such as middle C (0x3C, four 1 bits) would be flagged as an error,
    and stripping bit 7 (ANL A,#7Fh) would throw away the status/data
    distinction MIDI depends on.

  2. The constants it compares against are ASCII link-control characters, not 
  MIDI values.

  ┌──────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │      Value       │ Times compared │      ASCII meaning       │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x02             │ 13             │ STX, start of text       │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x04             │ 11             │ EOT, end of transmission │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x06             │ 15             │ ACK, acknowledge         │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x01             │ 8              │ SOH, start of header     │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x03, 0x05, 0x10 │ 3 each         │ ETX, ENQ, DLE            │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x15, 0x16       │ 3 each         │ NAK, SYN                 │
  └──────────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  That set (SOH, STX, ETX, EOT, ENQ, ACK, DLE, NAK, SYN) is exactly what polled
  multi-drop protocols such as IBM bisync use. Some of the small values, like
  0x07 and 0x0B, may be the program's own internal codes rather than characters.

  3. MIDI's signature values are missing. There are no comparisons with 0xF0 or
  0xF7 (System Exclusive start and end), 0xF8 (timing clock) or 0xFE (active
  sensing). A MIDI program almost always checks for these. The values 0x80–0x88
  do appear, four times each, but they look like the program's own command or
  channel codes, not MIDI Note Off/On.

  Most likely purpose: a controller that polls up to 8 terminals or devices over
  serial lines, using an ASCII block protocol with ACK/NAK. Think terminal
  concentrator, point-of-sale network controller, or industrial data collection.
  The 8 per-channel LEDs, the DIP-switch banks and the watchdog kicks all fit
  that kind of hardware.

✻ Cogitated for 32s · done 8:20 PM

❯ "8 per-channel LEDs,"                                                         

⏺ That was a guess, and I stated it too firmly. Checking every use of that
  register...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Right, there's no evidence it's LEDs. Here is everything the code does with
  the register at 0xFB:

  - What it is: an 8-bit output register at external address 0xFB. The program
    keeps a copy in internal RAM at 0x77, because the register can't be read
    back.
  - One bit per channel: every change uses the channel's bit from the mask table
    (01 02 04 … 80).
  - Cleared at startup (0x10E8) and in one other routine (0x05FC).
  - Flipped, never set or cleared, at 7 places:
    - In the receive interrupt, when a character arrives that isn't NAK, SYN, or
      0x20–0x2F (0x0082).
    - In the per-channel state handling (0x16A6, 0x16F5, 0x1734).
    - In two of the state handlers (0x2AE3, 0x2DA7).
  - At 0x169C–0x16B1 the flip sits between two writes to control register 0xE2:
    first 0xF8, then the flip, then 0xF9. That looks like a deliberate sequence,
    not a simple indicator update.
  - Also written at 0x35B3 and 0x35C8, in what may be a self-test, near code
    that reads and writes all of P1, P2 and P3.

  Flipping a bit on each event fits several things:
  - An activity LED, which would blink as traffic passes.
  - A toggle handshake line to the device on that channel, where each change
    signals "message received" or "your turn".
  - A per-channel control line driven through whatever chip 0xE2 controls.

  The code alone can't tell these apart. That would need the original board, or
  the identity of the chips at 0xD9–0xFF.

❯ Could it be for networking? Or are you pretty sure it's text-based serial?    

⏺ "Networking" and "text-based serial" overlap here. Checking whether messages
  carry a checksum, which shows how the link works...

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It's networking in the older sense: devices linked over serial lines.
  Confidence differs for each part of that.

  Fairly sure: character-at-a-time serial with ASCII control codes.
  - Every received byte is a 7-bit character plus a parity bit, checked one byte
    at a time.
  - The protocol logic compares against ASCII link-control characters: SOH, STX,
    ETX, EOT, ENQ, ACK, NAK, DLE, SYN.
  - I found no CRC. There are no CRC lookup tables and no common CRC polynomial
    constants in code. The only checksum code verifies the program itself: once
    at startup, and again from a second routine at 0x07EE.
  - So it isn't a packet LAN like Ethernet or Token Ring. Those send whole
    frames with a hardware-checked CRC, not individual characters with parity.

  Less sure: "text-based".
  - The framing is ASCII. The contents may not be readable text. Messages could
    be digits, codes or terminal data.
  - Nothing I've looked at shows what the data is.

  Plausible but unproven: a poll-and-reply network.
  - 8 independent channels, each with its own 35-state state machine and timeout
    counter.
  - ACK/NAK handling and ENQ/EOT. That's the classic "controller polls each
    station, station answers or says nothing to send" pattern.

  New finding: the second device, at 0x0000–0x00FF. The routines at
  0x0DA8–0x0DEF write a run of 0x55 bytes to its register 0x08, read them back
  and compare, then repeat with 0xAA. That's a loopback or buffer self-test,
  which points to another communications chip with a data buffer. One possible
  layout is 8 terminal-side channels plus one link to a host computer, which is
  a common shape for a concentrator. That's speculation until the transmit code
  and that chip's other registers have been worked through.

  Tracing what the program sends back after a byte arrives would show the
  message format. I can do that next if you like.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I have to correct my earlier ASCII conclusion. Most of those "ASCII control
  character" comparisons aren't made against received bytes at all:

  ┌──────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Constant │                       Compared against                        │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x04     │ per-channel RAM variable (@R0) 10 times, the device's buffer  │
  │          │ once                                                          │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x02     │ per-channel RAM variable 5 times, plus a few jump targets     │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x01     │ RAM variable 3 times, device buffer 2 times, external         │
  │          │ register once                                                 │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0x0B     │ RAM variable once                                             │
  └──────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The RAM variables are per-channel states and counters. So 0x02 and 0x04 are
  mostly state numbers or counts, not STX and EOT. I counted all comparisons
  regardless of what they compared against, and that made the case for ASCII
  look much stronger than it was.

  What still holds:
  - Receive side: 8 channels. Each byte is checked for odd parity and reduced to
    7 bits. Within those 7 bits, bits 4–6 look like a type field: the interrupt
    handler specially treats type 2 (AND 0x70 = 0x20) and the values 0x15 and
    0x16 (type 1, codes 5 and 6).
  - Send side: whole messages go through the chip at 0x0000. The program fills a
    buffer, sets a destination address and issues a "send" command, after
    checking the chip is ready. Replies are read back from the same buffer.
    Destinations are 0x30–0x37 and 0x00–0x07, two addresses per channel.
  - Startup handshake: each channel gets 26 04 88 and 66, and the program
    expects 66 04 back.

  What that suggests:
  - It's more like an addressed message network than text over serial lines. The
    chip at 0x0000 behaves like a network or link controller, or a mailbox to
    another processor, with buffered, addressed messages.
  - The receive side's 7-bit values with type fields look like a compact binary
    protocol, not human-readable text.
  - I'd now put "ASCII text terminals" as less likely than a custom or
    industrial protocol. That might be a control network, or an intelligent
    controller with 8 nodes or devices behind it.

  What would settle it: the chips that sat at those addresses on the original
  board. Without them, every register role I've given is inferred from usage. If
  you know where these microcontrollers came from, such as a donated piece of
  equipment or a manufacturer's name on the reels or tubes, that would narrow it
  down quickly.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Analysis pass complete. Summary follows.

  Revised picture

  The 8052 is an I/O controller sitting under a larger host computer. It has
  three interfaces:

  1. Host mailbox, at external registers F8h–FFh:
     - Status reads: the host writes an internal-RAM address to FAh. Firmware
       returns that byte at F8h and echoes the address at F9h. Flag bytes
       20h–25h and 2Eh clear when read, so they are host-visible error and
       status registers.
     - Start command: host writes 01h to FDh. Firmware builds per-channel masks
       and returns status byte 21h at FDh.
     - Debug monitor (35A7–36C1): previously disassembled but not analysed.
       - Entry: host writes FCh to FBh, firmware answers FCh at FAh.
       - Exit: host writes FAh.
       - Command 8xh writes and Axh reads. Bits 0–3 pick the target: internal
         RAM, XRAM, a register of the link chip, or ports P1/P2/P3.
       - Address comes from FFh and data from FEh.
     - In debug mode the main loop stops normal work. It kicks the watchdog and
       drives P3.5 as a health output: steady when OK, toggling when error flags
       21h.1 or 21h.2 are set.
  2. 8 channels, at external registers E0h–EFh and F0h–F7h:
     - A command byte arrives per channel at F0h+n: 7 bits plus even parity,
       delivered via INT0.
     - Two readings per channel at E0h+2n and E1h+2n: 7 bits plus odd parity.
     - Each accepted command flips that channel's bit in 77h, which is written
       to FBh. Commands 15h and 16h don't flip it. Function not proven:
       handshake line or indicator.
  3. Link controller chip at 0000h–000Ah, accessed through DPTR. Part number
     unknown.
     - Reset pulse comes from P3.3.
     - Two ways in: direct registers, plus an indirect register file where reg 2
       holds the index and command and reg 7 the data.
     - Setup writes config C2h←28h, C3h←01h, C4h←37h. It also fills table
       80h–87h with 81h–88h and table A0h–A7h with 97h, 80h–86h. Every value is
       read back. Any mismatch goes to the 0CBE error path.
     - Sending: bytes go into a FIFO at reg 8. The destination goes in reg 0Ah:
       30h+n, or n. Command 01h to reg 3 sends it.

  Command 2xh: read the channel

  - Bits 4–6 = 010 means a data request.
  - The reply has five bytes: 30 | value(E1h+2n) hi lo | value(E0h+2n) hi lo. It
    goes to destination 30h+n.
  - A parity failure puts 11 2B in place of that value.
  - Each 7-bit reading goes through a lookup table: 128 entries of 2 bytes, held
    in 4 tables at 31A7, 32A7, 33A7 and 34A7.
  - The tables join into one monotonic curve of about 360 entries. Command bits
    0 and 1 choose the starting offset: 0 or +128 for the first reading, −110 or
    +18 for the second. In effect each bit selects one of two ranges.
  - Entries 251–255 hold a sentinel value, 17 0B (out of range).
  - So the controller converts raw 7-bit samples into a calibrated quantity
    before forwarding them. That points to sensor or control values, not text.

  Encoding of the table values: unresolved

  - The low nibble of byte 0 takes only the values 0–4 and steps down along the
    curve, like an exponent or range.
  - The high nibble of byte 0 counts cleanly if each nibble is decoded as x≥8 ? 
    x−8 : 15−x. Only 13 small glitches remain, at boundaries.
  - Byte 1 reverses direction depending on its own low nibble, which looks like
    a reflected code.
  - 8 decodings tested, none monotonic: plain binary under all 8-bit
    permutations, Gray code under all bit orders and XOR masks, signed-digit and
    mixed-radix forms. The best still had 46 out-of-order steps.
  - Leading hypothesis: two fields, a coarse range/mantissa plus a signed fine
    correction. It could also be a native format of the receiving equipment.

  Corrections to earlier claims

  - The message first bytes are types that come in variants. 03/43, 0B/4B,
    13/53, 18/58, 23/63 and 2B/6B differ only in bit 6. 06/26/66 differ in bits
    5–6. Calling this "3-bit type + 4-bit code" was wrong.
  - Commands 15h and 16h are not discarded. They are processed but don't flip
    the channel bit.

  Assessment

  The firmware converts 8 channels of 7-bit readings into calibrated values and
  sends them as addressed messages. It is set up by, and reports status to, a
  host processor, which has full peek/poke debug access. It is a
  data-acquisition or control front end. Text-serial is ruled out. Music/MIDI is
  unlikely: there are no MIDI status bytes and the data has parity.

  Scratch listing with the debug handlers included:
  /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-jamie-Development-8052LEDSign/224e1727-d140-423
  d-a562-f82bad5b3002/scratchpad/rom2.lst

  I can write this analysis up in the repo under docs/romdump/ if you want it
  kept.

       17 +def dname(a):                                                 
       18 +    if a in SFR: return SFR[a]                                
       19 +    if a < 0x80: return f'{a:02X}h'                           
       20 +    return f'SFR_{a:02X}'                                     
       21 +def bname(b):                                                 
       22 +    if b < 0x80: return f'{0x20 + (b>>3):02X}h.{b&7}'         
       23 +    base = b & 0xF8                                           
       24 +    if base in BITSFR: return BITSFR[base][b&7]               
       25 +    return f'{dname(base)}.{b&7}'                             
       26 +                                                              
       27 +def decode(rom, pc):                                          
       28 +    """Return (length, text, targets, kind) kind in {'', 'jmp'
          +,'ret','call','cond','ijmp'}"""                               
       29 +    op = rom[pc]                                              
       30 +    b1 = rom[(pc+1) % len(rom)]; b2 = rom[(pc+2) % len(rom)]  
       31 +    rel = lambda x, n: (pc + n + (x - 256 if x > 127 else x)) 
          +& 0xFFFF                                                      
       32 +    rn = f'R{op & 7}'; ri = f'@R{op & 1}'                     
       33 +    lo = op & 0x0F; hi = op >> 4                              
       34 +    if lo == 1:                                               
       35 +        t = ((pc + 2) & 0xF800) | ((op & 0xE0) << 3) | b1     
       36 +        return (2, f'{"AJMP" if not (op & 0x10) else "ACALL"} 
          +L{t:04X}', [t], 'jmp' if not (op & 0x10) else 'call')

⏺ Write(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 58 lines, removed 6 lines
       1 -# 8052LEDSign                                                  
       1 +# MSM83C154 ROM Dump                                           
       2  
       3 -An attempt to make a scrolling LED sign using the pile of MCS-5
         -1 (8051/8052) microcontrollers I found in my [makerspace](https
         -://ashevillemakers.org/)'s electronics supply drawers.         
       3 +A dump and analysis of the 16 KB internal mask ROM of an OKI MS
         +M83C154                                                        
       4 +(8051-family) microcontroller, found among the pile of MCS-51 p
         +arts in my                                                     
       5 +[makerspace](https://ashevillemakers.org/)'s electronics supply
         + drawers.                                                      
       6  
       5 -I'm documenting the project on my blog, https://www.blog.montgo
         -merie.net/tags/makerspace-mcu-series/.                         
       7 +The chips turned out to be preprogrammed. This repository conta
         +ins the tooling                                                
       8 +used to read the ROM out, the dump itself, and an analysis of w
         +hat the program                                                
       9 +does.                                                          
      10  
      11 +It is a branch of my 8052 LED sign project, which provided the 
         +hardware: an                                                   
      12 +8052 running from SRAM that an ATmega328 loads on its behalf. I
         +'m documenting                                                 
      13 +that project on my blog,                                       
      14 +https://www.blog.montgomerie.net/tags/makerspace-mcu-series/.  
      15  
       8 -# Acknowledgements                                             
      16  
      10 -This project is designed to be compiled by the PlatformIO syste
         -m (https://platformio.org).                                    
      17 +## Contents                                                    
      18  
      19 +| Path | What |                                                
      20 +|---|---|                                                      
      21 +| `romdump.bin` | The 16,384-byte ROM dump. Its internal checks
         +um verifies. |                                                 
      22 +| `ROM_ANALYSIS.md` | What the program does. |                 
      23 +| `tools/dis51.py` | Minimal recursive-descent 8051 disassemble
         +r used for the analysis. |                                     
      24 +| `mcs51/src/romdump.c` | Dumper, run by the 8052. |           
      25 +| `avr_host/src/` | AVR host: loads the dumper, runs it, prints
         + the dump over serial. |                                       
      26 +| `scripts/capture_romdump.py` | Captures the serial output int
         +o `romdump.bin`. |                                             
      27 +| `hardware/` | KiCad schematic and PCB of the circuit. |      
      28 +| `docs/RAM:ROM Shadowing.txt` | Notes on the SRAM address deco
         +de the dumper relies on. |                                     
      29  
      30 +                                                               
      31 +## How the dump works                                          
      32 +                                                               
      33 +The MSM83C154 does not latch its EA (External Access) pin at re
         +set, so the                                                    
      34 +internal ROM can be switched on after the chip has started runn
         +ing external                                                   
      35 +code:                                                          
      36 +                                                               
      37 +1. The AVR writes the dumper to SRAM at code address 0x4000, an
         +d a stub                                                       
      38 +   `LJMP 0x4000` at 0x0000.                                    
      39 +2. With EA held low, the AVR releases the 8052's reset. The 805
         +2 runs the stub                                                
      40 +   from external memory and jumps to the dumper.               
      41 +3. The AVR raises EA. Code addresses 0x0000–0x3FFF now read the
         + internal ROM.                                                 
      42 +   Code at 0x4000 and above is still fetched externally.       
      43 +4. The dumper copies code 0x0000–0x3FFF (MOVC) to data 0x0000–0
         +x3FFF (MOVX).                                                  
      44 +5. The AVR puts the 8052 back into reset, reads the SRAM, and p
         +rints it.                                                      
      45 +                                                               
      46 +The 8052's EA pin must be wired to AVR pin PD2.                
      47 +                                                               
      48 +                                                               
      49 +## Running it                                                  
      50 +                                                               
      51 +This is built with [PlatformIO](https://platformio.org). On App
         +le Silicon Macs,                                               
      52 +PlatformIO's MCS-51 and AVR toolchains need Rosetta.           
      53 +                                                               
      54 +```                                                            
      55 +pio run -e mcs51 -t upload                                     
      56 +~/.platformio/penv/bin/python scripts/capture_romdump.py [port]
         + [output.bin]                                                  
      57 +```                                                            
      58 +                                                               
      59 +Uploading the `mcs51` environment builds the dumper, then build
         +s and uploads                                                  
      60 +the AVR host with the dumper compiled into it. The capture scri
         +pt resets the                                                  
      61 +AVR, so the whole sequence (RAM test, load, dump) runs again wh
         +ile it listens.                                                
      62 +The serial monitor runs at 38400 baud.                         
      63 +                                                               
      64 +                                                               
      65  # Copyright
      66  
      67  Copyright 2026 James Montgomerie.
      68  
      17 -See `LICENSE.txt` for license terms.                           
      69   No newline at end of file
      70 +See `LICENSE.txt` for license terms.

⏺ I've written the annotated disassembly to romdump_annotated.lst: 8,556 lines
  covering the whole 16 KB ROM, with about 2,900 lines carrying comments.
  Nothing is committed yet.

  It's generated rather than hand-written:
  - tools/annotate.py produces the listing. Rerun it with python3 
    tools/annotate.py after changing the annotations.
  - tools/romdump_annotations.py holds everything written by hand:
    - names for 60 routines, the external registers, the link controller
      registers, internal RAM and flag bits;
    - 25 block comments, mainly on the interrupt handlers, init, self-tests,
      host commands, main poll and debug monitor;
    - line comments and the layout of the data tables.
  - tools/dis51.py can now be imported by the generator. Running it directly
    still works as before.

  Comments the generator adds by itself:
  - reads and writes of the external and link controller registers, with the
    value where it's known (e.g. LINK_CMD <- 01h: SEND);
  - per-channel pointers such as state[n], cmd[n] and CHn_CMD;
  - channel mask lookups and conversion-curve lookups;
  - state changes, with the handler each one leads to;
  - the 12-byte per-channel records in upper internal RAM;
  - a one-line description on every call to a named routine.

  Data tables are laid out in the listing: the bit-reverse table, the channel
  masks, and the four conversion-curve tables shown as numbered 2-byte entries.
  Unused space, the config byte and the checksum are marked.

  Limitation: the 35 state handlers only have automatic comments plus a header
  listing the states each one sets and the routines it calls. I haven't worked
  through their logic by hand.

  While doing this I found two mistakes in my earlier analysis and corrected
  them in ROM_ANALYSIS.md:
  - The command bytes at F0h–F7h use odd parity, not even.
  - Link register 2's bit 7 means "ready for an indirect access", not "data
    available".

  I also added new sections there: the reset-cause codes, the rolling background
  self-test, the per-channel records, the timer rate, and the P1 input banks.

  Smaller changes: the new files are in the README contents table, and
  .gitignore now ignores Python's __pycache__/. Say if you want this committed.

⏺ At 2C5Ch, the write via MOV @R0,#10h lands in bank 0 register n−1, which
  overwrites R7, the channel loop counter for channel 8, unlike the 0788h
  equivalent that uses base 3Fh. This looks like a bug in the original firmware,
  so I'm checking for other occurrences next. · summary

⏺ Not entirely. The generated structure was sound, but my review against the
  code turned up 10 errors in the annotations and in ROM_ANALYSIS.md. I've fixed
  all of them and regenerated the listing. Nothing is committed yet.

  Wrong automatic comments:
  1. Indirect reads labelled as writes. The generator didn't notice when code
     set a single bit in the accumulator, so it treated every indirect read as a
     write (e.g. 0A2Eh showed "write indirect 42h"). Fixed: those 36 accesses
     now read as "select indirect C2h for read" etc.
  2. Wrong meaning for bit 7 of link register 2. I had treated bit 7 of the
     value written there as a write signal. The code at 2F41h writes register
     numbers 10h–17h without it, so bit 7 is just part of the register number.
  3. Conversion-table titles swapped. The first reading (E1h) uses 33A7h/34A7h,
     chosen by command bit 0. The second (E0h) uses 31A7h/32A7h, chosen by
     bit 1. The analysis doc had the same swap.

  Wrong hand-written statements:
  4. Host dispatch bits misassigned. HOST_STATUS bit 4 is the channel command
  and bit 2 the status read; I had them the other way round. Bits 6 and 7
  trigger the ACK writes.
  5. The main poll doesn't return after one pass. It returns every 100 passes.
  In debug mode it never returns, which pauses the self-test.
  6. P3.5 isn't driven in debug mode. I'd said it was. It's updated in normal
  running only.
  7. "Timeout" at 3Fh+n isn't a timeout. It's never counted down, only set to
  values 01h–13h and compared, so I've renamed it a status code.
  8. The handshake reply is 66 04 88, not 66 04. I'd also missed that the
  handshake ends by sending 30 11 2B 11 2B (readings invalid).
  9. "March test" was the wrong name. The RAM test is a plain write/read test
  with FFh and 00h.
  10. The analysis doc had host commands at even parity. The listing was right,
  and the doc already has the odd-parity fix from last time.

  Possible bug in the original firmware: at 2C5Ch the code works out n−1 and
  writes 10h there. That address is one of the CPU's working registers (R0–R7).
  The same logic at 0788h writes to the channel's status code at 3Fh+n instead.
  For channel 8 this overwrites R7, the channel loop counter. It only runs when
  a channel that isn't faulty receives command 07h. I've marked it in the
  listing and added a section to ROM_ANALYSIS.md.

  Easier to read now:
  - State handlers are named after their state (state_45h etc.), so state
    changes show where they go.
  - Five more per-channel arrays are labelled (48h, 50h, 58h, 60h, 68h).
  - Stores into per-channel arrays are annotated.
  - Duplicate comments are gone.

  What I checked: I compared every hand-written block with its code, checked
  each kind of automatic comment against real examples, and read the state 45h
  handler in full. Of the other 29 state handlers I only looked over their
  summaries. I didn't read them line by line, so their automatic comments are
  the least checked part.

❯ Okay, do you have any more informed speculation on the purpose of this IC?    

⏺ Strong lead from earlier: entries 60 apart differed only in byte 0's low
  nibble (2405 → 2305 → 2205). If that holds, it would mean a logarithmic scale.
  Testing it across the whole curve before speculating...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes. One new piece of evidence changes the picture.

  New evidence: the conversion curve is logarithmic, 60 steps per decade

  I compared each table entry with the one 60 steps further along:

  ┌─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Gap between entries │ Exact matches (only the exponent nibble differs) │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 58                  │ 0                                                │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 59                  │ 0                                                │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 60                  │ 70                                               │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 61                  │ 3                                                │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 62                  │ 0                                                │
  └─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  - Byte 0's low nibble is a decimal exponent. It drops by 1 every 60 entries
    and nothing else changes: 2405 → 2305 → 2205, 14AB → 13AB, D303 → D203, and
    so on.
  - Mismatches are small and in the low digits. That fits each decade being
    calculated separately and rounded.
  - Precision runs out at the bottom. Once the exponent reaches 0 it can't drop
    further, so the values decay to small integers and then to 00 00. That
    explains the plateaus I couldn't account for before.
  - So the table holds decimal floating point: a mantissa of roughly 3 digits,
    in an unusual nibble encoding, times 10 to the exponent. The value falls by
    a factor of 10 every 60 input counts.
  - Range: the curve spans about 360 entries, so about 6 decades. A single 7-bit
    reading covers about 2.1 decades. The two command bits move that window
    around within the 6 decades.

  In short, each raw reading is on a log scale (proportional to the logarithm of
  the quantity). The firmware turns it into an engineering value as mantissa ×
  10ⁿ.

  What the whole design implies

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │                Feature                │           Implication           │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Six-decade log scale, decimal         │ A physical quantity measured    │
  │ floating-point output                 │ across decades                  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Two readings per channel, on          │ Two related measurements, or    │
  │ different parts of the curve          │ two detectors/ranges per head   │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sentinel for out of range, 11 2B for  │ The receiver displays or logs   │
  │ "invalid"                             │ these values                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Per channel: command byte, two        │ Smart remote sensor heads, not  │
  │ readings, a configuration block with  │ bare analog inputs              │
  │ checksum, six device types            │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 7-bit odd parity everywhere. NAK      │ The heads talk to the board     │
  │ (15h) and SYN (16h) handled specially │ over ASCII-style asynchronous   │
  │                                       │ serial links                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ROM checksum, RAM tests, watchdog,    │ Built for high reliability.     │
  │ stack check, reset cause reported to  │ Unusual for consumer kit        │
  │ the host                              │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                       │ Intelligent I/O card under a    │
  │ Host peek/poke debug monitor          │ supervisory CPU, serviced in    │
  │                                       │ the field                       │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Mask ROM                              │ Made in volume. Thousands of    │
  │                                       │ units at least                  │
  └───────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  Ranked hypotheses

  1. Multi-channel monitoring concentrator for log-range instruments. Most
     likely.
     - Candidates: radiation monitoring (dose-rate and count-rate meters
       commonly span 6 decades on a log scale), vacuum gauges (ion and Pirani
       gauges have log outputs spanning decades), or gas detection.
     - It fits the heavy self-testing, the reset-cause reporting and decimal
       values for display.
     - The two readings per channel would suit dose rate plus integrated dose,
       or two detector ranges.
  2. Audio or RF level system:
     - The case for it: 60 counts per decade is ⅓ dB per count for amplitude, or
       ⅙ dB for power. Converting a dB reading to a linear value fits, and 8
       channels is typical of a console or a metering system.
     - The case against it: decimal floating point is an odd choice for gain
       coefficients and suits displayed values better. The self-test effort also
       seems more than a mixer would carry.
  3. Building or process control (temperatures, setpoints): least likely. Those
     quantities don't span decades, so a log table would make no sense.

  Confidence is moderate on the overall architecture and low on the specific
  application.

  What would narrow it down

  - The markings on the chip. The mask-ROM code (usually a suffix such as
    MSM83C154-xxx) and the date code would identify the customer and date the
    part.
  - Where the chips came from. Whatever else was in the same donated drawer, and
    any equipment boards they came from.
  - Decoding the mantissa nibbles fully. With the exponent now known, the
    remaining 3 digits are a much smaller problem. If they decode to familiar
    values, the units may become obvious.

  I'll update ROM_ANALYSIS.md with the logarithmic-scale finding if you want it
  recorded.

  │ M83C154-42 │ be the number OKI gave this customer program. │ Medium     │
  │            │  That reading is from OKI's suffix pattern;   │            │
  │            │ no source confirms "-42" itself.              │            │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ ©OKI       │ Standard OKI copyright line. It's not a date  │            │
  │ '80/85     │ code: '80 likely refers to the MCS-51         │ Medium     │
  │            │ architecture, '85 to OKI's CMOS version.      │            │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │            │ Probably date and lot. OKI's format isn't     │            │
  │ JAPAN      │ documented online. Most plausible readings:   │ Low        │
  │ 94000      │ 1994, or 1989/1999 with a month and lot       │            │
  │            │ number.                                       │            │
  └────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  What the markings do tell us:
  - The chip was made for one company's product under its own part number. That
    fits a commercial instrument or communications product, not something sold
    over the counter.
  - If 94 is the year, it was made around 1994. That's late for a mask-ROM 8051,
    which would suggest a mature, long-running product line.
  - Custom OKI MSM83C154s did turn up in communications gear: a Hayes modem used
    one (listing (https://www.electronicsurplus.com/oki-msm83c154-ic-hayes-copy
    right-p-n-04-00119-v2-5)). Our chip is marked differently, so it isn't
    evidence of a Hayes connection.

  The most useful leads now are physical:
  1. The rest of the hoard. Original tubes or reels, any labels, other parts
     with the same 79X prefix, or boards these chips came off.
  2. Other markings in the batch. A different 79Xnnnn number or -nn suffix on
     any chip would mean other ROM programs from the same maker.
  3. Makerspace provenance. Whoever donated the drawer of parts might know the
     employer or equipment.
  4. OKI data books. The 1986 and 1988 microcontroller books on Bitsavers (1986
     (https://bitsavers.trailing-edge.com/components/oki/_dataBooks/1986_OKI_Mic
     rocontroller.pdf), 1988 (https://bitsavers.trailing-edge.com/components/oki
     /_dataBooks/1988_OKI_Microcontroller_Data_Book.pdf)) may explain OKI's
     marking and date-code layout. I can work through them if you want to pin
     down the date.

  I can add the markings and these readings to ROM_ANALYSIS.md, together with
  the logarithmic-scale finding.

※ recap: Repurposing the repo on branch romdump-analysis to document the dumped MSM83C154 ROM, which looks like an 8-channel log-scale sensor front end. The analysis and annotated disassembly are committed; next, push the branch or decode the mantissa digits.

❯ I asked someone else to look at this. Here is their reply. Please comment:    
  I re-checked the dump directly: re-ran the recursive disassembly, verified    
  the checksum, jump table, table offsets, message encoding and the key         
  routines, and cross-checked the OKI data book/user manual. The overall        
  architecture holds up well. Most of the errors are notation/indexing issues   
  and a few places where the document states an inference as fact.              
  What checks out                                                               
  - Checksum: sum of 0x0000–0x3FFC is 0x45B9; stored as B9 45 at 3FFE/3FFF.     
  - Disassembly coverage: 12,685 bytes reached; 35-entry state jump table at    
  0x1791; states 4Eh–5Dh all go to 0x2CEC; state = 3 × entry.                   
  - Parity: odd parity is enforced with JNB P; even parity is an error.         
  - UART: no SBUF/SCON accesses; the UART is unused. P3.0/P3.1 are used as GPIO 
  for the P1 bank select.                                                       
  - Timer 2: RCAP2 = FE80h, 384 machine cycles; the ISR drives the 10/100/1000  
  tick counters.                                                                
  - Table geometry: 33A7 is exactly 110 entries before 31A7; 34A7 is exactly 18 
  entries after; the 60-entry exponent period is real.                          
  - Suspected bug at 0x2C5C: real. The equivalent code at 0x0788–0x07A6 writes  
  the channel status code at 3Fh+n; 0x2C60 writes 10h to n−1, i.e. bank-0 RAM.  
  For channel 8 that is R7, which can corrupt the main loop counter.            
  Concrete corrections                                                          
  The most important issue is a mix of 0-based and 1-based channel numbering.   
  The code uses R7 = 1..8. The external-register formulas in the document use n 
  as if it were 0-based, while the internal-RAM formulas use it as 1-based.     
  Location in ROM_ANALYSIS.md    Current text    Correct value    Evidence      
  Channel command    “F0h+n”    EFh+n = F0h+(n−1)    0x0058–0x005B: A = EFh +   
  R7                                                                            
  Channel readings    “E0h+2n / E1h+2n”    E0h+2(n−1) / E1h+2(n−1)              
  0x0F88–0x0F8E, 0x0FF9–0x0FFF: (R7−1)*2 + E0/E1                                
  Reply layout    “value(E1h+2n) … value(E0h+2n)”    E1h+2(n−1) and E0h+2(n−1)  
    same                                                                        
  Link destination    “30h+n”    2Fh+n = 30h+(n−1)    0x0F88–0x0F8E,            
  0x1047–0x1050, 0x2F8F–0x2F98                                                  
  66 destination    “n”    n−1    0x0C15–0x0C18: FFh + R7                       
  Host start    “state at 3Fh+n”    status code at 3Fh+n; state is at 2Fh+n     
  0x0606–0x0621 reads 3Fh+n; state array is 2Fh+n                               
  The document is internally inconsistent here: the startup handshake already   
  uses 30h+n−1, which is correct. The rest of the document says 30h+n, which is 
  off by one.                                                                   
  Other corrections:                                                            
  - Parity failure invalidates both readings. At 0x0F94 the code sends 11 2B 11 
  2B whether the first or second reading fails, then jumps to the common exit.  
  The docum                                                                     
  ──── (45 lines hidden) ────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
   than the document says. It sets 20h.0, 21h.3, clears 1Eh and 77h, writes     
  FBh=0, sets CH_ACTIVE for every channel whose status code is not 01h, and     
  sets 1Dh for status code 10h.                                                 
  Speculation on the application                                                
  The architecture is clear: 8 channel/head interfaces plus a host mailbox plus 
  a message-passing link controller. The specific application is still open.    
  The document’s ranking is reasonable, but I’d add two candidates:             
  1. Multi-channel radiation / vacuum / gas monitor — best fit for 6-decade log 
  values, two readings per head, per-head calibration/checksum, and heavy       
  self-test. The two readings could be rate + dose, ion + Pirani, or            
  measurement + reference.                                                      
  2. RF or audio level monitoring — 60 counts/decade gives ⅓ dB/count for       
  amplitude, and two readings per channel fit forward/reflected power,          
  peak/RMS, or two detector ranges. The decimal/log output and self-test are    
  less typical of audio, but plausible for a broadcast/transmitter monitor.     
  3. Optical photometry / densitometry — 6-decade log response,                 
  sample/reference pairs, per-head calibration; this fits the “two readings on  
  different parts of the curve” and the decimal output well.                    
  4. Telephone/PBX line monitoring — 8 lines, two readings (voltage/current),   
  per-line configuration and line types, addressed link messages. The presence  
  lines could be hook/ring detect.                                              
  5. Building/process control — still least likely because those quantities     
  rarely span decades.                                                          
  The most distinctive clue is the configuration block with checksum and six    
  device types. That looks like a smart sensor head being configured and read   
  back, not a bare analog input. The link controller’s part number and the      
  original board would settle it faster than more ROM analysis.                 
  Suggested next steps                                                          
  1. Fix the channel-index notation throughout the document (use R7 = 1..8, and 
  write external addresses as EFh+n, E0h+2(n−1), E1h+2(n−1), 2Fh+n, FFh+n for   
  the 66 destination).                                                          
  2. Correct the parity-failure, config-block-length, C4 readback, E3 count,    
  debug-monitor scope, and unused-range statements.                             
  3. Re-examine the two exponent anomalies (0x1401 at curve −70; 0x1101 at      
  curve 110) — they may be intentional special codes.                           
  4. Try to decode the 12-bit mantissa as a binary/log fraction rather than a   
  decimal mantissa; the exponent period is real, but the mantissa is not        
  behaving like a stable 3-digit decimal.                                       
  5. If the original board turns up, identify the link controller first; its    
  register map is the strongest fingerprint for the equipment family.
