⏺ The review is accurate and well grounded. I checked its claims against the
  listing. Every factual correction holds, and the indexing mix-up is real. In
  two places I think the reviewer has the code wrong or draws the wrong
  conclusion.

  Confirmed in the code

  Claim: Channel numbering is mixed (n used as 0-based in some formulas, 1-based

    in others)
  Check: Confirmed. The document has F0h+n, E0h+2n, E1h+2n and 30h+n (lines 118,

    120, 144, 189–201), but the handshake section uses 30h+n−1.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: Host start reads "state at 3Fh+n"
  Check: Confirmed, line 85. It should say status code.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: The type 3 block (18h/58h) is shorter
  Check: Confirmed. The loop ends at CJNE R1,#E5h, so it sends E1h–E4h with the
    checksum at E5h.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: C4h is never read back
  Check: Confirmed, in both the initial setup and the background check.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: 37 watchdog writes, not 35
  Check: Confirmed. Two of them go through @R0.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: 308Fh, 319Fh and 36C2h are unreferenced
  Check: Confirmed: no DPTR setup points at them, and there is no MOVC A,@A+PC.
  I
    labelled the bit-reverse table without noting that nothing uses it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: Unused range should end at 3FFCh
  Check: Confirmed (line 69).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: "Full peek/poke" overstates it
  Check: Agreed. The monitor reaches internal RAM through @R0, which covers
  upper
    RAM but not the special function registers, plus external RAM, link
  registers
     and ports. It can't touch the special function registers or program memory.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: "70 matches" without the total
  Check: Agreed. It is 70 of about 300, mostly in the upper part of the curve.
    "Small and in the low digits" overstated it (line 225).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: NAK/SYN and "ASCII-style" are unproven
  Check: Agreed. That's the same mistake I corrected for 01h/02h/04h, repeated
    (line 345).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Claim: EA latching is an inference
  Check: Agreed. "Does not latch EA" should be stated as observed behaviour of
    this chip, not a datasheet property.

  Where I disagree

  1. Parity failure: the reviewer's correction is itself slightly wrong.
  - First reading fails: the code jumps straight to 0F94h and sends 30 11 2B 11 
    2B. That case matches the reviewer.
  - First reading passes, second fails: the first value's 2 bytes are already in
    the message buffer by 0FDDh. Then 1005h jumps to 0F94h, which adds 4 more
    bytes. The message becomes 30 hh ll 11 2B 11 2B: 7 bytes instead of 5.
  - So neither "replaces that value" (the document) nor "both are replaced" (the
    reviewer) is right. This may be a second firmware bug: the jump from 1005h
    looks as if it should have gone to a code path that writes only the second
    pair.

  2. The two exponent anomalies probably support the exponent reading rather 
  than undermine it.
  - Both sit exactly on decade boundaries: positions −70 and 110 are 180 apart,
    which is 3 × 60.
  - Both have the same shape:
    - E30A → 1401 → 7301
    - E00B → 1101 → 600B

    In each, the last entry of one decade is followed by a "1" written with the
    next exponent up, then the decade continues.
  - That looks like a rounding carry: a value that rounds to exactly 10.0 gets
    renormalised as 1.0 × 10ᵉ⁺¹.
  - The other two boundaries (−10 and 50) have no such entry. That's consistent
    with rounding landing on 10.0 at some boundaries and not others.
  - This is a hypothesis, not proof. It would be wrong to drop the 60-per-decade
    claim because of these two entries.

  Where I partly agree

  - 79X1396: the reviewer suggests it's an OKI lot code. I think the date and
    lot are more likely on the JAPAN 94000 line, and customer numbers on the top
    line are common. Neither reading is proven, though, so confidence should
    drop from medium to low with both readings listed.
  - "Appears online": I can't check whether your blog post contains the
    markings. The reviewer's wording, no datasheet or independent part listing,
    is safer either way.
  - "Decimal floating point": I'll adopt the reviewer's wording: "4-bit decimal
    exponent plus 12-bit mantissa, encoding unresolved".

  New candidates and details

  - Optical photometry or densitometry: a strong addition. Sample/reference
    pairs on a 6-decade log response fit the two readings sitting on different
    parts of the curve.
  - Telephone/PBX line monitoring: weak in my view. Line voltage and current
    don't span 6 decades, which is the strongest clue we have.
  - New details:
    - Already established, will add: the memory page for each device (FFxxh for
      the mailbox, 00xxh for the link controller), reg 3 08h meaning release
      receive buffer, and reg 1 bit 6 meaning message received.
    - Not yet checked, will check first: the 12-byte record layout, the two
      checksum conventions and the extra host-start details.

  I can apply all of this to ROM_ANALYSIS.md and the annotations, regenerate the
  listing and commit, if you want.

✻ Baked for 1m 31s · done 9:25 PM

❯ Here's their reply:                                                           
  You’re right on both substantive points. I re-traced the second parity path   
  and re-checked the anomalies; my earlier wording was too strong in one case   
  and simply wrong in the other.                                                
  1. Parity failure — you’re right, and it is a second bug                      
  Exact behaviour:                                                              
  - First reading fails at 0x0F91: only 30h has been written to the FIFO, so    
  0x0F94 produces                                                               
  30 11 2B 11 2B — 5 bytes.                                                     
  - Second reading fails at 0x1002: the first value’s two bytes are already in  
  the FIFO from 0x0FDD. 0x1005 jumps to the same 0x0F94 handler, which appends  
  four more bytes:                                                              
  30 hh ll 11 2B 11 2B — 7 bytes.                                               
  So the document’s “in place of that value” is wrong, and my “both replaced”   
  was wrong too. The correct description is case-dependent:                     
  - first failure → both values invalidated, 5-byte message;                    
  - second failure → first value left in place, then four invalid bytes         
  appended, 7-byte message.                                                     
  Your “second firmware bug” reading is the natural one: 0x1005 should go to a  
  path that writes only 11 2B, or the code should defer writing the first pair  
  until both parity checks pass. Whether the 7-byte output actually breaks the  
  receiver depends on whether the link controller/receiver treats 0x30 as a     
  fixed 5-byte message. If it does, the extra two bytes are a protocol          
  violation; if it length-delimits, they may be parsed as a new message.        
  Without the link controller’s datasheet I’d document it as a probable         
  protocol bug, not a certainty.                                                
  2. Exponent anomalies — your renormalisation reading is better than mine      
  I withdraw the “table defect” suggestion and the claim that these undermine   
  the 60-per-decade pattern. The 180-entry spacing is real, and the repeated    
  shape is too regular to be random:                                            
  - E30A → 1401 → 7301                                                          
  - E00B → 1101 → 600B                                                          
  That is consistent with a temporary carry/renormalisation or a special “10.0” 
  code, not a random bad entry.                                                 
  One small correction, though: they are not at the exponent transitions. The   
  exponent transitions are at -96, -36, 24, 84; the anomalies are 26 entries    
  into a decade, at the same phase of the 60-entry grid. So “decade boundaries” 
  is slightly off — it is a periodic anomaly at a fixed 60-entry phase, not at  
  the 10^E boundary. That makes the simple “10.0 carry” story less obvious,     
  because a 10.0 renormalisation would normally happen at the top of a decade,  
  not 26/60 of the way through. It could still be a carry if the table’s phase  
  is offset, or it could be a deliberate special code.                          
  The useful test is exactly what you suggest: decode the value at that phase.  
  If 0x1401/0x1101 is the code for 1.0 × 10^(E+1) and the neighbours are ~9.99  
  × 10^E, it is a carry. If the code for 1.0 differs between the two decades,   
  it is not a normalised float. Either way, the 60-per-decade claim stands; the 
  anomaly is a detail, not a refutation.                                        
  3. 79X1396                                                                    
  Agreed on lowering confidence and listing both readings. The more likely      
  split is:                                                                     
  - top line: customer/OKI code, possibly the equipment maker’s number;         
  - M83C154-42: the OKI part and mask code;                                     
  - JAPAN 94000: date/lot, with 1994 plausible but the fifth digit odd enough   
  that “date + lot” is safer than “January 1994.”                               
  Neither reading is proven without OKI’s marking guide.                        
  4. Details I checked, so you don’t have to                                    
  12-byte channel record (0x2E34–0x2E6D), base 80h + 12(n−1):                   
  Offset    Contents                                                            
  +0    WIN_E0 bits 6–4 (type)                                                  
  +1    WIN_E0 bits 3–0                                                         
  +2..+7    WIN_E1..E6                                                          
  +8    WIN_E7 bits 5–4                                                         
  +9    WIN_E8 bits 7–6                                                         
  +A    WIN_E8 bits 5–4                                                         
  +B    WIN_E9                                                                  
  Note WIN_E0 bit 7 is not stored in the record; it is used as the global       
  MSG_FLAGS bit 7. Record byte 0 bit 2 is therefore WIN_E0 bit 6 (the 04h/06h   
  selector), and record byte A == 2 is WIN_E8 bits 5–4.                         
  Host start (0x05F1):                                                          
  - sets 20h.0, 21h.3;                                                          
  - clears 1Eh, 77h, and FBh;                                                   
  - for each channel, code[n] != 01h → set CH_ACTIVE; code[n] == 10h → set 1Dh; 
  - writes STAT21 to FDh, then calls update_channel_status.                     
  Checksum conventions:                                                         
  - send_window_block: sends E1..E8 plus E9 = ~(sum E1..E8) & FFh;              
  check_window_checksum verifies sum + E9 + 1 == 0.                             
  - check_window_block: sums E0..E8 (types 1/2) or E0..EA (other types) and     
  expects FFh — one’s complement.                                               
  - receive_and_checksum: reads the first FIFO byte (message type) then R6 data 
  bytes, accumulates them into R2, then adds the local checksum at R1 plus 1    
  and expects zero. The initial R2/R6/R1 are caller-specific, so the exact      
  algebra is path-dependent.                                                    
  5. Application                                                                
  Optical photometry/densitometry is a strong addition for exactly the reason   
  you give: sample/reference pairs on a 6-decade log response. I agree PBX line 
  monitoring is weak — line voltage/current don’t span six decades, and that    
  is the strongest single clue in the ROM.                                      
  So my updated position is: parity = case-dependent, with a probable second    
  bug; exponent anomalies = periodic renormalisation/special code, not table    
  defects and not a reason to drop 60/decade; 79X = low confidence, both        
  readings listed; mantissa = “4-bit decimal exponent plus 12-bit mantissa,     
  encoding unresolved.”

  fall relative to the anomalies, and the 12-byte record layout.

⏺ We now agree on nearly everything. I verified the reviewer's new details: the
  record layout is correct. Their exponent-change positions are off by one, and
  comparing them with the anomalies turned up something useful.

  Exponent changes and anomalies

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │                 Event                  │        Curve positions         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Exponent nibble changes                │ −96, −35, 25, 85 (the reviewer │
  │                                        │  had −36, 24, 84)              │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ High nibble of byte 0 wraps from the   │ −70, −10, 50, 110              │
  │ A–F codes to the 7…1 codes             │                                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The 1x01 anomaly entries               │ −70, 110                       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  - The reviewer is right that the anomalies aren't where the exponent changes.
    "Decade boundaries" was wrong in that sense. The anomalies sit 25–26 entries
    after the exponent changes.
  - New observation: each anomaly falls exactly on the wrap from A–F codes to
    7…1 codes. That wrap also happens every 60 entries. The anomaly appears at 2
    of the 4 wraps.
  - So each 60-entry cycle has two fixed landmarks, 25–26 entries apart: the
    exponent change and the A–F→7 wrap.
  - This narrows down the mantissa encoding. The split between codes A–F and
    codes 0–7 is part of the number's structure, not noise. Possibly the true
    decade boundary is at the wrap and the exponent nibble changes at an offset
    point, or the A–F codes mean something other than plain digits.
  - The test the reviewer suggests still applies: decode the values either side
    of the wrap.

  Record layout: verified

  The code at 2E34h–2E6Dh fills the record exactly as the reviewer described.
  The bit fields are shifted down to the low bits when stored (SWAP, RR), so:

  - +0 = type, 0–7;
  - +9 = WIN_E8 bits 7–6, stored as 0–3.

  Record byte 0 bit 2 is WIN_E0 bit 6, as they say.

  Parity bug, 79X, application

  Agreed as they state them:
  - Parity: the result depends on which reading fails (5 or 7 bytes). Probably a
    protocol bug, unconfirmed without the link controller's datasheet.
  - 79X: low confidence, both readings listed. For the date line, "date and lot"
    is safer than a specific month.
  - Application: add optical photometry/densitometry. PBX line monitoring stays
    weak.

  Changes to apply

  1. Channel numbering throughout: R7 = 1..8, EFh+n, E0h+2(n−1), E1h+2(n−1),
     2Fh+n, FFh+n.
  2. Parity failure: describe both cases, and add it as a second suspected bug.
  3. Type 3 config block length, no read-back of C4h, 37 watchdog writes,
     debug-monitor scope, unused range ending at 3FFCh, the three unreferenced
     tables.
  4. Mantissa wording: "4-bit decimal exponent plus 12-bit mantissa, encoding
     unresolved". Give 70 out of about 300, drop "small mismatches", and add the
     two-landmark observation and the anomalies.
  5. Replace ASCII names (NAK/SYN, "ASCII-style") with neutral wording. State EA
     behaviour as observed. Lower confidence on the markings and change the
     "online" wording.
  6. Add the memory page for each device, link reg 3 08h and reg 1 bit 6, the
     record layout, the checksum conventions, the host-start details and the
     photometry candidate.
  7. Matching fixes in tools/romdump_annotations.py, then regenerate the
     listing.

      130 -### 3. Link controller chip (DPTR 0000h–000Ah)                
      152 +#### Channel records                                          
      153  
      154 +Upper internal RAM 80h–DFh holds a 12-byte record per channel,
          + at                                                           
      155 +80h + 12(n−1). `check_window_block` (0x2E34–0x2E6D) fills it f
          +rom the                                                       
      156 +configuration block, shifting bit fields down to the low bits:
      157 +                                                              
      158 +| Offset | Contents |                                         
      159 +|---|---|                                                     
      160 +| +0 | WIN_E0 bits 6–4 (type, 0–7) |                          
      161 +| +1 | WIN_E0 bits 3–0 |

      163 +| +8 | WIN_E7 bits 5–4 |                                      
      164 +| +9 | WIN_E8 bits 7–6 |                                      
      165 +| +A | WIN_E8 bits 5–4 |                                      
      166 +| +B | WIN_E9 |                                               
      167 +                                                              
      168 +WIN_E0 bit 7 is not stored; it sets MSG_FLAGS (74h) bit 7 inst
          +ead. The state                                                
      169 +45h handler tests record byte 0 bit 2 (WIN_E0 bit 6) and recor
          +d byte A == 2.                                                
      170 +                                                              
      171 +#### Checksum conventions                                     
      172 +                                                              
      173 +- `send_window_block` (0x04DA) sends E1h..E8h; `check_window_c
          +hecksum`                                                      
      174 +  (0x0504) then requires sum(E1h..E8h) + E9h + 1 = 0, i.e. E9h
          + is the one's                                                 
      175 +  complement of the sum. The type 3 path (0x0342) does the sam
          +e with E1h..E4h                                               
      176 +  and the checksum at E5h.                                    
      177 +- `check_window_block` (0x2DFA) sums E0h..E8h (types 1 and 2) 
          +or E0h..EAh                                                   
      178 +  (other types) and expects FFh.                              
      179 +- `receive_and_checksum` (0x052F) reads the message type byte,
          + then R6 data                                                 
      180 +  bytes, adds them to R2, then adds the local checksum byte an
          +d 1 and expects                                               
      181 +  zero. The starting values come from the caller.

      239  ## Command 2xh: read the channel
      240  
      241  - Bits 4–6 = 010 means a data request (`(cmd & 70h) == 20h`).
      186 -- The reply has five bytes, built at 0x0F7C:                  
      242 +- The reply is built at 0x0F7C:                               
      243  
      244    ```
      189 -  30 | value(E1h+2n) hi lo | value(E0h+2n) hi lo              
      245 +  30 | value(E1h+2(n−1)) hi lo | value(E0h+2(n−1)) hi lo      
      246    ```
      247  
      192 -  It goes to destination 30h+n.                               
      193 -- A parity failure puts `11 2B` in place of that value.       
      248 +  It goes to destination 2Fh+n.                               
      249 +- A parity failure depends on which reading fails:            
      250 +  - **First reading** (0x0F91): only `30` has been written, so
          + the message is                                               
      251 +    `30 11 2B 11 2B`, 5 bytes. Both values are invalidated.   
      252 +  - **Second reading** (0x1002): the first value's two bytes a
          +re already in                                                 
      253 +    the FIFO. 0x1005 jumps to the same handler, which appends 
          +four bytes:                                                   
      254 +    `30 hh ll 11 2B 11 2B`, 7 bytes. See "Suspected firmware b
          +ugs".                                                         
      255  - Each 7-bit reading goes through a lookup table: 128 entries 
           of 2 bytes,
      256    held in 4 tables at 0x31A7, 0x32A7, 0x33A7 and 0x34A7.
      257  - The tables join into one monotonic curve of about 360 entrie
           s. 0x31A7 and
      258    0x32A7 are contiguous. 0x33A7 starts 110 entries before 0x31
           A7's first
      259    entry, and 0x34A7 starts 18 entries after it.
      260  - Command bits 0 and 1 choose the starting offset: bit 0 picks
            −110 (0x33A7)
      200 -  or +18 (0x34A7) for the first reading (E1h+2n), bit 1 picks 
          -0 (0x31A7) or                                                 
      201 -  +128 (0x32A7) for the second (E0h+2n). In effect each bit se
          -lects one of two                                              
      202 -  ranges.                                                     
      261 +  or +18 (0x34A7) for the first reading (E1h+2(n−1)), bit 1 pi
          +cks 0 (0x31A7)                                                
      262 +  or +128 (0x32A7) for the second (E0h+2(n−1)). In effect each
          + bit selects                                                  
      263 +  one of two ranges.                                          
      264  - Entries 251–255 hold a sentinel value, `17 0B` (out of range
           ).
      265  - So the controller converts raw 7-bit samples into a calibrat
           ed quantity
      266    before forwarding them. That points to sensor or control val

      222 -- **Byte 0's low nibble is a decimal exponent.** It drops by 1
          - every 60                                                     
      223 -  entries while everything else stays the same: `2405 → 2305 →
          - 2205`,

      225 -- **The mismatches are small and in the low digits.** That fit
          -s each decade                                                 
      226 -  being calculated separately and rounded.                    
      227 -- **Precision runs out at the bottom.** Once the exponent reac
          -hes 0 it can't                                                
      228 -  drop further, so the values decay to small integers and then
          - to `00 00`.                                                  
      229 -  That explains the plateaus at the end of the curve.         
      230 -- **The values are decimal floating point:** a mantissa of abo
          -ut 3 digits, in                                               
      231 -  an unusual nibble encoding, times 10 to the exponent. The va
          -lue falls by a                                                
      232 -  factor of 10 every 60 input counts.

      287 +- The low nibble takes only the values 0–4. It drops by 1 ever

      241 -### Mantissa digits: unresolved                               
      306 +### A second landmark: the A–F → 7 wrap                       
      307  
      243 -- The low nibble of byte 0 takes only the values 0–4 and steps
          - down along the                                               
      244 -  curve: the exponent, above.                                 
      308 +The high nibble of byte 0 runs through the codes A–F, then 7 d
          +own to 1. The                                                 
      309 +wrap from A–F to the 0–7 codes also recurs every 60 entries, a
          +t positions                                                   
      310 +−70, −10, 50 and 110, 25–26 entries after each exponent change
          +. So each                                                     
      311 +60-entry cycle has two fixed landmarks. The split between code
          +s A–F and                                                     
      312 +0–7 is part of the number's structure.                        
      313 +                                                              
      314 +### Two anomalies                                             
      315 +                                                              
      316 +- Position −70 (0x33A7 entry 40): `14 01`, exponent 4 between 
          +neighbours                                                    
      317 +  with exponent 3 (`E30A → 1401 → 7301`).                     
      318 +- Position 110 (0x31A7 entry 110, also 0x34A7 entry 92): `11 0
          +1`, exponent 1                                                
      319 +  between neighbours with exponent 0 (`E00B → 1101 → 600B`).  
      320 +                                                              
      321 +Both sit exactly on the A–F → 7 wrap, 180 entries (3 × 60) apa
          +rt, with the                                                  
      322 +same shape. The other two wraps (−10, 50) have no such entry. 
          +They look like                                                
      323 +a renormalisation carry or a deliberate special code, not tabl
          +e defects. A                                                  
      324 +test: decode the values either side. If `1x01` is 1.0 × 10^(e+
          +1) and its                                                    
      325 +neighbours are about 9.99 × 10^e, it is a carry.              
      326 +                                                              
      327 +### Mantissa: unresolved                                      
      328 +                                                              
      329  - The high nibble of byte 0 counts cleanly if each nibble is d
           ecoded as
      330    `x ≥ 8 ? x − 8 : 15 − x`. That gives the order 8, 9, A–F, 7,
            6 … 0. Only 13
      331    small glitches remain, at boundaries.
      332  - Byte 1 reverses direction depending on its own low nibble, w
           hich looks like
      333    a reflected code.
      250 -- 8 decodings tested, none monotonic:                         
      334 +- None of these produced a monotonic 12-bit value (also checke
          +d                                                             
      335 +  independently by a reviewer):

      338    - signed-digit forms;
      254 -  - reflected mixed-radix forms.                              
      339 +  - reflected mixed-radix forms;                              
      340 +  - the transform above applied to all three nibbles.         
      341

          -main. If they

      444  ### What the design implies
      445  
      446  | Feature | Implication |
      447  |---|---|
      341 -| Six-decade log scale, decimal floating-point output | A phys
          -ical quantity measured across decades |                       
      448 +| Six-decade log scale, decimal exponent | A physical quantity
          + measured across decades |                                    
      449  | Two readings per channel, on different parts of the curve | 
           Two related measurements, or two detectors/ranges per head |
      450  | Sentinel for out of range, `11 2B` for "invalid" | The recei
           ver displays or logs these values |
      451  | Per channel: command byte, two readings, a configuration blo
           ck with checksum, six device types | Smart remote sensor heads
           , not bare analog inputs |
      345 -| 7-bit odd parity everywhere, NAK (15h) and SYN (16h) handled
          - specially | The heads talk to the board over ASCII-style asyn
          -chronous serial links |                                       
      452 +| 7-bit data plus odd parity on all channel bytes | Consistent
          + with a serial-style link to each head |                      
      453  | ROM checksum, RAM tests, watchdog, stack check, reset cause 
           reported to the host | Built for high reliability. Unusual for
            consumer equipment |
      347 -| Host peek/poke debug monitor | Intelligent I/O card under a 
          -supervisory CPU, serviced in the field |                      
      348 -| Mask ROM, customer part number | Made in volume for one comp
          -any's product |                                               
      454 +| Host debug monitor | Intelligent I/O card under a supervisor
          +y CPU, serviced in the field |

⏺ I've applied all the agreed changes and committed them to romdump-analysis as
  dbc622f. Nothing has been pushed.

  ROM_ANALYSIS.md, which I rewrote in full:
  - Channel numbering: a notation note at the top fixes n = 1..8. Every channel
    address is now written that way: EFh+n, E0h+2(n−1), E1h+2(n−1), 2Fh+n,
    FFh+n.
  - Factual corrections:
    - Parity failure is described for each case: 5 bytes when the first reading
      fails, 7 when the second does.
    - The type 3 config block is E1h–E4h with the checksum at E5h.
    - C4h is never read back.
    - There are 37 watchdog writes, not 35.
    - The debug monitor's reach is stated exactly.
    - The unused range ends at 3FFCh.
    - The three unused tables (308Fh, 319Fh, 36C2h) now have their own section.
  - Encoding:
    - The claim is softened to "4-bit decimal exponent plus 12-bit mantissa,
      encoding unresolved".
    - The 70 matches are given out of about 300, with an example of a large
      mismatch.
    - New subsections cover the A–F → 7 wrap as a second landmark every 60
      entries, and the two anomalies with a test for the carry idea.
  - Bugs: the section is now "Suspected firmware bugs", with the 7-byte readings
    message as the second.
  - Wording:
    - NAK, SYN and "ASCII-style" are replaced with neutral descriptions.
    - EA behaviour is stated as observed on this chip.
    - Confidence on the markings is lowered, with both readings of 79X1396
      listed.
    - "Appears online" now says no datasheet or independent listing.
  - Additions:
    - the two memory pages;
    - link register 1 bit 6 and register 3's 08h command;
    - the 12-byte record layout;
    - the checksum conventions;
    - the full host-start behaviour;
    - photometry and PBX as candidates, with PBX ranked weak.

  Also fixed: a garbled sentence left over from an earlier edit in the
  corrections list.

  Something I got wrong in the new text and fixed before committing: I first
  wrote that WIN_E0 bit 7 sets MSG_FLAGS bit 7. The code at 021Dh stores it
  inverted, and the document now says so.

  Annotations and listing: updated config-block, link-setup and readings
  descriptions, a comment flagging the jump at 1005h as a suspected bug, a
  record-layout comment block at 2E34h, the bit-reverse table marked unused, and
  the debug monitor's reach. I regenerated romdump_annotated.lst (8,576 lines).

⏺ Probably not worth it: the SCN8031H is the ROMless member of the 8051 family.
  These are standard 8051-family part markings, so I'm reading them from memory:

  ┌──────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Marking  │                            Reading                            │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SCN8031H │ Signetics 8031, HMOS (the H). It's an 8051 with no ROM: it    │
  │          │ must run from external memory with EA tied low.               │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8450     │ Date code: 1984, week 50.                                     │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │          │ Ordering-code suffix. In Signetics numbering, A44 usually     │
  │ CCA44    │ means a 44-pin PLCC package. The DIP version is N40, e.g.     │
  │          │ SCN8031HCCN40. The CC covers temperature range and speed      │
  │          │ grade (commonly 0–70 °C, 12 MHz).                             │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ E        │ Probably a revision or lot letter.                            │
  └──────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Is there anything to dump? Usually not. Two reasons it might still be worth
  one try:
  - Leftover ROM: some makers sold 8051 dies with unwanted or failed mask ROMs
    as 8031s, disabled only by the marking. Dumping one occasionally turns up a
    leftover program.
  - The method suits this chip: on NMOS 8051s, EA is generally sampled
    continuously rather than latched at reset. That's the basis of the classic
    8051 ROM dump, so it should work at least as well as it did on the OKI.

  Practical issues:
  1. Package. If these really are PLCC-44, they won't fit the breadboard's
     DIP-40 socket. You'd need a PLCC-44 to DIP-40 adapter. The pin functions
     are the same; only the physical layout differs. Can you check whether
     they're square chips with J-leads?
  2. Clock speed. The board runs at 13.875 MHz. If these are 12 MHz parts,
     that's about 15% over their rating. It will probably work for a quick test,
     but a 12 MHz or 11.0592 MHz crystal would be safer.
  3. Size. A real 8051 has 4 KB of internal ROM, so only 0x0000–0x0FFF of the
     dump would mean anything. Above that the dumper just copies the SRAM onto
     itself. No firmware change is needed, though I could limit the output to 4
     KB to tidy it.
  4. Power. HMOS parts draw much more current than the CMOS OKI: around 125 mA
     versus a few mA. Check that the breadboard supply can handle it.

  Reading the result: all FFh, all 00h, or repeating bus noise in the first 4 KB
  means there's no ROM, the expected outcome. Anything that disassembles as
  plausible 8051 code, such as vectors at 0000h and sensible jumps, would be a
  leftover mask program.

  If the package and supply are sorted, the existing setup should run as-is.
  Want me to limit the dump to 4 KB and add a check to the capture script that
  tells real code from an empty result?

  - Power: HMOS draws roughly 125 mA, against a few mA for the OKI. Make sure

⏺ The SCN8031H wasn't empty. Despite being sold as ROMless, its 4 KB internal
  ROM holds a complete program, which supports the leftover-8051-die idea. It's
  unrelated to the OKI firmware.

  How much to trust the dump

  - 0000h–0FFFh holds real code. It disassembles cleanly: 3,824 of 4,096 bytes
    are reached as code, with sensible interrupt vectors and 74 called routines.
  - 1000h–3FFFh is the AVR's RAM-test pattern. That's expected above 4 KB, and
    it confirms the copy worked.
  - No checksum to verify against. I found no obvious checksum: the 8-bit sum is
    09h, not 00h. A second dump compared byte for byte would rule out read
    errors.

  What the program appears to do: a two-axis stepper motor controller on a 
  serial bus

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Evidence    │                         Detail                          │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ The Timer 0 interrupt writes a 4-bit value to P0's low  │
  │ Stepper phase │ nibble (02EEh). The Timer 1 interrupt writes a 4-bit    │
  │  outputs      │ value to P1's low nibble (0443h). That's one coil-phase │
  │               │  output per motor.                                      │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ The tables at 05C5h (C9 64 4A 3E 36 31 …) and 05E3h (AC │
  │ Acceleration  │  7E 78 6D 64 …) follow roughly 1/√n, the classic        │
  │ ramps         │ constant-acceleration stepper profile. Timers 0 and 1   │
  │               │ run in 16-bit mode, reloaded from them.                 │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Home/limit    │ INT1 is read twice in a row as a debounced check        │
  │ sensing       │ (044Dh). P2.5 is polled in several places.              │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ SCON = D0h: 9-bit UART, the 8051's multiprocessor mode. │
  │ Serial link   │  TH1 = FDh with Timer 1 auto-reload gives 9600 baud if  │
  │               │ the original crystal was 11.0592 MHz.                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ Received bytes are forwarded with their 9th bit (MOV    │
  │ Daisy chain   │ TB8,RB8; MOV SBUF,SBUF), 8 bytes per frame. Nodes pass  │
  │               │ traffic down a chain.                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Startup       │ TXD/RXD toggled and checked (loopback or neighbour      │
  │ checks        │ detection). A5h at 3Bh marks a warm restart.            │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Bit-banged    │ Clock/data sequences on P2.0/P2.1 and P2.3/P2.4 look    │
  │ peripheral    │ like a serial EEPROM or shift register.                 │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ External      │ INT0 forces a software reset.                           │
  │ abort         │                                                         │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Possible applications: a camera pan/tilt head (two axes, home sensors,
  daisy-chained serial control), a small XY positioner or plotter axis pair, or
  reel/mechanism drives in vending or gaming machines. Confidence is moderate on
  "dual stepper controller with serial bus" and low on the specific product.

  The marking says 8031, but the program uses P0 and P2 as plain I/O and never
  uses external memory. It was written for an 8051 running from internal ROM.
  That suggests a mask-ROM 8051 run, perhaps surplus or rejected, remarked as
  8031s.

  scn8031.bin is sitting untracked in the repo root. Want me to:
  1. Move it to a separate folder (e.g. scn8031/), with a short analysis like
     ROM_ANALYSIS.md?
  2. Generalise tools/annotate.py so it can produce an annotated listing for
     this dump too?

❯ I wonder if the two chips were used together?                                 

⏺ Checking for direct evidence: whether the SCN8031's serial format and
  transmitted byte values match what the OKI expects on its channels...

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Checking the SCN8031 for any of the OKI protocol's byte values...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Probably not, at least not directly. I compared the two programs and found
  nothing linking them, and several things against it.

  Evidence against a direct link

  ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │           │        OKI MSM83C154         │      Signetics SCN8031H      │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ Marked '85, so made in 1985  │                              │
  │ Date      │ or later. Probably 1994      │ 1984, week 50 (8450)         │
  │           │ (94000, low confidence).     │                              │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Serial    │ 7 data bits + odd parity per │ 8 data bits + a parity bit   │
  │ format    │  channel byte, through       │ in the 9th bit (UART mode 3, │
  │           │ external hardware            │  odd or even selectable)     │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Command   │ 06h, 07h, 0Bh, 15h, 16h,     │ Mostly C0h–DFh (C0, C1, D0,  │
  │ values    │ 20h–23h                      │ D1, D2, D4, D8, DE) and      │
  │           │                              │ 60h/61h                      │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ What it   │ Log-scale readings across 6  │ Two stepper motors with      │
  │ handles   │ decades, from 8 smart heads  │ acceleration ramps and home  │
  │           │                              │ sensors                      │
  └───────────┴──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  - Formats don't match. The SCN8031 sends 8 data bits plus parity. The OKI
    expects each channel byte as 7 bits whose 8th bit is the parity. If one fed
    the other, either the OKI would see parity errors or the SCN would lose data
    bits. The OKI's channel hardware is unknown, so this isn't conclusive, but
    nothing suggests a translator.
  - No shared protocol values. None of the OKI's command codes, message types
    (30, 26 04 88, 66), or its invalid code 11 2B appear in the SCN8031. The
    only overlap is a single comparison with 20h, which proves nothing.
  - Different eras. The SCN8031 predates OKI's '85 copyright, and the OKI chip
    was likely made up to ten years later.

  What would still fit

  - Same source, different products. Parts in one drawer often come from one
    donor or employer. Two generations of one company's equipment would explain
    both.
  - One instrument, separate subsystems. If the OKI board serves a photometer or
    other log-range instrument, a motion controller could belong to the same
    system, for example driving a filter wheel, a grating or a sample stage.
    They'd sit on different buses, so neither ROM would mention the other. This
    is the only way I can see them being "used together", and nothing in the
    code supports it beyond plausibility.

  What would settle it

  - Matching markings or labels (a common customer prefix like 79X, or tube
    labels) on both sets of parts.
  - Knowing whether they came from the same donation or the same drawer.
  - If the original equipment is ever found: whether a stepper controller and
    the 8-channel board sit in the same chassis.

  So it's a coincidence of storage unless provenance says otherwise. The code
  itself gives no reason to connect them.

⏺ I've done both: the SCN8031H dump has its own folder with an analysis and an
  annotated listing, and the annotator now works for either chip. Everything is
  committed to romdump-analysis (dc9c358 and 51a24b4); nothing is pushed.

  1. The scn8031/ folder
  - scn8031.bin is the 4 KB ROM image. scn8031_capture.bin is the full 16 KB
    capture, kept unaltered; above 4 KB it's just the RAM-test pattern.
  - ROM_ANALYSIS.md covers the markings, how it was dumped, the vectors, and the
    stepper outputs and ramp tables. It also covers the inputs, the serial link
    and its start-up sequence, the factory-test mode, possible applications,
    the comparison with the OKI chip, and open questions.
  - scn8031_annotated.lst is the annotated disassembly, 2,685 lines.

  2. The annotator
  - tools/annotate.py now takes --annotations MODULE. All the patterns that were
    specific to the OKI chip are now switched on by the annotation file.
  - I regenerated the OKI listing afterwards and it came out byte-identical.
  - The SCN8031H's names, comments and data tables are in
    tools/scn8031_annotations.py.

  Things I got wrong in my last reply, now corrected:
  - Three phase outputs, not two. P0's high nibble is a third 4-bit output.
    Timer 0 drives either the P0 low or P0 high motor, and Timer 1 drives the P1
    motor, so at most two move at once.
  - The serial "pass-through" isn't continuous forwarding. At start-up a node
    accepts only frames starting C0h. A C0 80 or C0 C0 frame makes it echo the
    next 8 bytes back out, after which it accepts all frames. That looks like a
    start-up sequence for a chain of nodes.

  Second dump: your scn8031-2.bin matches the first capture byte for byte, so
  the read is confirmed, and the analysis now says so. I left the file untracked
  since it's a duplicate, so you can delete it.

  README.md now introduces the second chip and lists the new files.

⏺ Bytes 07BBh–07FFh are unprogrammed FFh padding, so the eight "references"
  there are chance matches. I'm correcting the earlier read: the serial ISR
  writes to bank 0 R7/R6, not bank 1, and I'm turning to the command dispatcher
  next. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Command decoding maps the frame's first byte to a motion command, fixed codes,
  or error flags, and every frame gets a 1-byte status reply. I'm now checking
  how C0h, C1h and Dxh are handled. · summary

⏺ Found that the target byte at 0F77h must be in the 01h–60h range and takes the
  shortest path around a 96-position circular axis, so this is a wheel driven
  by motor B. I'm checking motor C's sweep next, since 83h × 18h stays just
  inside its 0C60h travel limit. · summary

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Address   │                         What it is                         │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Next coil pattern for motors A and C. Starting from Eh it  │
  │ 04CCh (new) │ cycles E 6 7 5 D 9 B A, which inverted is a standard       │
  │             │ 8-step half-step sequence. Bad patterns fall back into the │
  │             │  cycle.                                                    │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 039Ah       │ Next pattern for motor B: 1, 3, 2, 6, 4, 5, the 6-step     │
  │             │ sequence for a 3-phase motor. Bit 3 is always on.          │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 01DFh       │ Three speed settings for Timer 0, one per 2-bit speed      │
  │             │ class.                                                     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0FBEh       │ A 2-bit speed class for each sweep position, packed 4 to a │
  │             │  byte.                                                     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Motor C's speed in three stages per move: start, after 12  │
  │ 058Ah       │ steps, and the last 7 steps. Values past the used range    │
  │             │ look like filler.                                          │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 07BBh–07FFh │ Not hidden code: unprogrammed FFh bytes up to the 2 KB     │
  │             │ boundary at 0800h.                                         │
  └─────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Both coil tables overlap code to save space. The entries that land on
  instruction bytes are all invalid patterns, and those still lead back into the
  cycle.

  What the three motors do

  - Motor B: a 96-position wheel or carousel. The target must be 1–96. If the
    move would be more than 48 positions, it goes the other way round (0F77h).
  - Motor C: a linear axis. Its position is tracked and kept within 0–3168
    half-steps. The sweep moves it 24 half-steps per position, and 131 positions
    × 24 fills that range.
  - Motor A: plain moves. Up to 2047 half-steps in either direction, with no
    position kept.

  Commands

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    First byte     │                    What it does                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 00h–7Fh           │ Move motor B to a position and nudge motor C at the │
  │                   │  same time                                          │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 80h–8Fh           │ Move motor A                                        │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 90h–9Fh           │ Move motor C                                        │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A0h–AFh           │ Move motor B to a position                          │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ C0h               │ Control: start-up echo, re-initialise, leave read   │
  │                   │ mode                                                │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ C1h               │ Enter read mode, or one of four mechanism actions   │
  │                   │ (one returns motor B to position 1)                 │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ D0h, D1h, D2h,    │ In read mode, read back a stored value              │
  │ D4h, D8h, DEh     │                                                     │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Every frame gets a 1-byte status reply. I couldn't tell what three of the four
  C1h actions do, nor which inputs on the shift register are which.

  Mistakes in my earlier SCN8031 write-up, now fixed

  - Motor A drives P0's high nibble and motor B the low nibble; I had them
    swapped.
  - Incoming serial frames are stored in the main loop's registers, not the
    serial interrupt's.
  - 3Bh isn't a warm-start marker. It records whether P2.3 reads high at reset,
    probably an option jumper or presence line. Two of the C1h actions refuse to
    run without it.

  What it probably is

  A positioning controller with a 96-position carousel, a sweeping linear arm
  and a third motor. A lab autosampler or sample changer fits best, since 96 is
  the standard sample-plate well count; confidence is low on the exact product.
  If the OKI board is from a light-measuring instrument, the two could have been
  parts of one lab analyser. Nothing in either ROM links them, though, and they
  were made years apart.

  Still open: what three of the C1h actions do, what the shift-register inputs
  are wired to, and what the two extra Timer 0 settings control. Real hardware
  or documentation would probably be needed to settle those.

❯ It sounds like we know enough to actually _use_ this for something with the   
  appropriate hardware design?                                                  

⏺ Mostly, yes. The ROM tells us enough to wire up a working board and drive it
  from a PC. A few unknowns are best settled at the bench.

  What we know well enough to build

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Need      │                      From the ROM                       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ 11.0592 MHz, so TH1 = FDh gives exactly 9600 baud. A 12 │
  │ Clock         │  MHz crystal would be about 7% off, too much for a      │
  │               │ UART.                                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Run mode      │ EA tied high. The dump showed this chip executes from   │
  │               │ its internal ROM.                                       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ 9600 baud, 8 data bits plus a parity bit: that's        │
  │ Serial format │ standard 8O1 framing, which any USB-serial adapter can  │
  │               │ do. Odd parity is the default after reset, because the  │
  │               │ parity flag starts cleared. C0 C0 switches to even.     │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Start-up      │ Send C0 80. The node replies, then echoes the next 8    │
  │ sequence      │ bytes. After that it accepts all commands.              │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ Moves for all three motors, motor B's 96-position       │
  │ Commands      │ targets, and register reads (table in                   │
  │               │ scn8031/ROM_ANALYSIS.md).                               │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Coil outputs  │ Motor A: P0 bits 4–7. Motor B: P0 bits 0–2. Motor C: P1 │
  │               │  bits 0–3.                                              │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ Almost certainly active-low. All outputs idle high at   │
  │ Coil polarity │ reset, and if high meant "on", every coil would be      │
  │               │ energised at power-up.                                  │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Coil order    │ The pattern sequence 1, 9, 8, A, 2, 6, 4, 5 energises   │
  │ (half-step    │ bits 0 → 3 → 1 → 2. So motor phases 1–4 go to bits 0,   │
  │ motors)       │ 3, 1, 2 (bits 4, 7, 5, 6 for motor A).                  │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Hardware sketch

  - Core: the SCN8031H on a PLCC-44 socket, 5 V supply (allow about 150 mA for
    the chip), an RC reset (10 µF + 8.2 kΩ to RST, which is active-high), EA to
    VCC, and the 11.0592 MHz crystal with two 33 pF capacitors.
  - P0 pull-ups: P0 is open-drain when used as I/O, so it needs pull-ups, e.g. a
    10 kΩ resistor network. P1 and P2 have internal pull-ups.
  - Motor drivers: the outputs are active-low, so a ULN2003 straight on the pins
    would invert the patterns. Use 74HC04 inverters in front of each ULN2003.
    Cheap 28BYJ-48 unipolar steppers would work for motors A and C.
  - Motor B: 3-phase. A small 3-phase stepper would suit it, or just LEDs to
    watch the pattern.
  - Inputs:
    - INT1: home switch, needs a pull-up.
    - P2.5: input, function unknown. Leave it pulled up, with a jumper to ground
      it.
    - P2.3: leave high, so the option byte reads A5h and the C1h 08h/04h actions
      are allowed.
    - INT0: leave high. Pulling it low resets the chip.
  - Shift register: optional. With nothing on P2.2 it reads FFh, which the
    firmware sees as "no inputs active". A 74HC165 with switches would let us
    find out what each bit does.
  - Serial: the chip's TXD/RXD at 5 V go to a USB-serial adapter. Don't loop TXD
    back to RXD: at reset that triggers the factory test mode.

  Unknowns

  - Busy and error conditions: the firmware refuses moves while flags 22h.6,
    23h.1 or 20h.5 are set. If something at start-up (homing, a sensor check)
    leaves one set, moves will be rejected until we know how to clear it. The
    1-byte status reply and the D0h–D8h register reads should show what's going
    on.
  - Homing: which motor uses the INT1 sensor, and what three of the four C1h
    actions do, aren't clear. Trying them with a switch attached would show it.
  - Speeds: the speed classes and ramp parameters are only partly understood, so
    expect some experimenting.
  - Motor B's patterns: the 3-phase sequence works with either polarity, but bit
    3 being held high suggests it isn't a coil. Check on a scope or with LEDs
    before attaching a motor.

  Suggested order of work

  1. Minimal board: chip, crystal, reset and serial only. Confirm it replies to
     C0 80 at 9600 8O1.
  2. Put LEDs on P0 and P1, send move commands, and watch the patterns step.
  3. Add drivers and small steppers, then the home switch, then the 74HC165.

  I can write a Python host tool for step 1 (8O1 at 9600, the start-up sequence,
  each command as a function, replies decoded). I can also turn the hardware
  sketch into a design document, or a KiCad schematic once you've decided on the
  drivers.

❯ How would the motors be physically arranged?                                  

⏺ The firmware doesn't describe the mechanism directly, but it constrains it.
  First, decoding the per-position speed classes at 0FBEh, in case they show
  zones along motor C's travel...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Without the original hardware this is inference. The firmware rules some
  layouts in and others out. Here is the most consistent arrangement, with the
  evidence for each part.

  Most likely layout

              top view                              side view

          . - ~ ~ ~ - .                       motor A (vertical, relative moves)
       .'   96 slots    '.                          ┃
      :    on a ring     :                    ┌─────┸─────┐
      :       ◯ ◯ ◯      :  ◀── motor C ──▶   │ carriage  │◀── motor C
      :     ◯       ◯    :      linear arm    └─────┰─────┘    (linear, 0–3168 
  half-steps)
       '.    turntable  .'      (radial or          ┃ probe / needle
          ' - ~ ~ ~ - '         across)       ══════╧══════  carousel
               ▲                                     ▲
         motor B (3-phase, 96 positions)        motor B underneath

  Motor: B
  Probable role: Turntable or carousel with 96 positions
  Evidence: Positions 1–96 wrap around, and moves take the shorter direction
    (0F77h). Only a circular axis needs that.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Motor: C
  Probable role: Linear carriage or arm moving over the carousel
  Evidence: Position tracked within 0–3168 half-steps, with an end limit
    enforced. The combined command rotates the carousel and offsets C by up to
  62
     half-steps, so C's position matters relative to the carousel. That could
    mean reaching inner or outer rings of positions, or centring over a slot.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Motor: A
  Probable role: Vertical axis (probe, needle, plunger) or a pump
  Evidence: Only relative moves of up to 2047 half-steps, no stored position.
    That fits an axis that goes down a set distance and back, or one that homes
    to a switch each time.

  Two further clues:
  - Moves that stop on a sensor: the wait routine (0ED7h) ends early if
    shift-register inputs 5 or 6 go active. That's the shape of "move until
    something is detected", such as a probe touching liquid or a surface, or a
    crash/limit switch. Which motor it's tied to isn't certain.
  - The sweep: motor C moves back and forth in 24-half-step increments across
    its travel. That suits scanning past something (a reader or detector across
    a row), or agitation/mixing.

  What the ROM can't tell us

  - Speed zones: the per-position speed-class table (0FBEh) shows no clear
    pattern over positions 0–99: mostly classes 2 and 3, mixed. There are no
    obvious slow zones near ends or obstacles. For indexes above 99 the lookup
    reads into code, so either the sweep never goes that far in practice, or
    those values don't matter.
  - Radial or linear: whether C moves radially over the turntable, or along a
    straight line past it, isn't determinable. Both fit the commands.
  - Scale: the steps-per-millimetre and the motors' step angles are unknown, so
    the physical size is open. With a typical 200-step motor at 400 half-steps
    per revolution, C's travel is about 8 revolutions of whatever leadscrew or
    belt it drives.

  Alternatives that also fit

  - Filter wheel plus scanning stage: B selects one of 96 filters or apertures,
    C scans a sample past an optical path, A focuses. This pairs neatly with the
    photometry hypothesis for the OKI board.
  - Rotary sample table with a pick-and-place arm: B indexes the table, C
    reaches out to a slot, A lowers a gripper.

  The autosampler layout fits best: 96 is a standard carousel and microplate
  size, and the three axes match rotate, reach and dip.

  For a hardware design of our own, the physical layout is free to choose. The
  firmware only cares about step patterns and sensors. A 3D-printed turntable on
  motor B, a belt or leadscrew carriage on motor C and a small vertical axis on
  motor A would exercise every command. If you go that way, I can sketch the
  mechanism and the sensor placement: home switch on INT1, end and contact
  switches on the 74HC165.

✻ Crunched for 45s · done 10:00 PM

❯ I asked the same other party to review your analysis again. THey like it!     
  This is a very different chip from the OKI, and the analysis is good. The     
  headline is that the “ROMless 8031” is functionally an 8051 with a valid 4 KB 
  mask ROM, and the program is a three-motor positioning controller on a        
  multidrop serial bus. The most likely product shape is an autosampler/sample  
  changer, probably an HPLC/GC autosampler or a 96-position laboratory          
  carousel.                                                                     
  The marking surprise                                                          
  SCN8031HCCA44 is the ROMless 8031 order code: 12 MHz, 0–70 °C, PLCC-44. But   
  the dump contains a coherent 4 KB program at 0000h–0FFFh, and above 1000h it  
  is the AVR RAM-test pattern. If the part were truly ROMless, raising EA would 
  have read the external SRAM and produced the stub/pattern, not a program.     
  The code itself confirms what it was written for:                             
  - no MOVX anywhere;                                                           
  - P0 and P2 used as plain I/O;                                                
  - P3.6/P3.7 used as status outputs, not WR/RD.                                
  So this is an 8051 program, and the die is an 8051, not an 8031. The          
  “probably mask-ROM 8051s, surplus or rejected, remarked as 8031s” explanation 
  is close, but I would drop “rejected”: the ROM is valid and the program is    
  complete. Surplus 8051 dies or a die/package mix-up is more likely. The top   
  marking is simply not trustworthy.                                            
  There is a useful precedent: the 8051dumper project dumped an SCN8051H from a 
  Micropolis hard disk. That is the ROM version of this same family. Your chip  
  behaves like that die, not like an 8031.                                      
  What the program does                                                         
  The decode is convincing:                                                     
  - Motor B: 96-position circular axis, 3-phase 6-step, P0 bits 0–2,            
  shortest-path positioning via 60h - distance.                                 
  - Motor C: linear axis, 16-bit position tracked over 0000h–0C60h, 4-wire      
  half-step, P1 bits 0–3, with a back-and-forth sweep.                          
  - Motor A: relative moves only, up to 2047 half-steps, 4-wire half-step, P0   
  bits 4–7.                                                                     
  - Timer 0 steps A or B; Timer 1 steps C. At most two motors move at once.     
  - Serial link: 9600 baud, 8 data bits + 9th-bit parity, 2-byte commands,      
  1-byte replies, a startup echo/enumeration sequence, read mode, and a command 
  set for combined/relative moves.                                              
  That is a motion-control node, not a general-purpose controller.              
  Most likely product                                                           
  The combination of a 96-position carousel, a linear needle/stage axis, and a  
  third relative-move motor fits an autosampler very well:                      
  - carousel selects one of 96 vials or tubes;                                  
  - linear axis moves the needle/probe between the vial and the injection or    
  wash position;                                                                
  - third motor drives the syringe/plunger or a probe;                          
  - serial bus connects the node to the instrument controller;                  
  - shift-register inputs are likely vial-present, needle-home, syringe-home,   
  door, etc.;                                                                   
  - P2.3 is a hardware option/presence line that the host can read with DE 00;  
  - C1h 20/10/08/04 are likely homing, wash, purge, or inject actions.          
  The 96-position carousel is the strongest clue. It is the standard layout for 
  96-vial HPLC/GC autosamplers and for 96-tube laboratory counters. A           
  microplate is 96 wells but is usually a rectangular plate, not a carousel; a  
  96-position carousel is more likely vials/tubes.                              
  My ranking for the Signetics chip alone:                                      
  1. HPLC/GC autosampler or sample injector — carousel, needle arm, syringe.    
  Best single-unit fit.                                                         
  2. Laboratory sample changer / gamma counter carousel — 96-tube carousel,     
  elevator, pipettor.                                                           
  3. Microplate/plate handler — possible, but a carousel of 96 positions is     
  less plate-like.                                                              
  4. Filter wheel/turret with a scanning stage — possible but the third motor   
  and command set look more like a sample handler.                              
  5. Camera pan/tilt — now much less likely; the 96-position wrap-around axis   
  is too instrument-like.                                                       
  Relation to the OKI chip                                                      
  The document says there is no evidence they were used together, and that is   
  fair. But the functional shapes are complementary:                            
  - OKI: 8 channels of 7-bit log-scale readings, two readings per channel,      
  6-decade range, per-channel configuration/checksum, addressed messages. That  
  is an 8-channel optical or radiation measurement front end.                   
  - Signetics: 96-position sample carousel + linear stage + third motor, serial 
  bus. That is a sample handler.                                                
  That combination is exactly a laboratory analyzer:                            
  - Microplate reader/autosampler: 8 optical channels + 96-well plate/sample    
  handling.                                                                     
  - Gamma counter / liquid scintillation counter: 96-position sample carousel + 
  multi-channel log detector.                                                   
  - HPLC autosampler with a multi-channel detector: 96-vial carousel +          
  8-channel absorbance/fluorescence detector.                                   
  The dates (1984 vs 1994) and serial protocols differ, so they may be          
  different generations or different instruments from the same donor. But “same 
  instrument family” is a better joint story than “unrelated.” If the OKI       
  board is the photometric/radiation front end and the Signetics board is the   
  sample changer, a microplate reader or gamma counter is the most coherent     
  pair.                                                                         
  Accuracy notes and one likely bug                                             
  The analysis is largely right. The things I would change:                     
  - “Rejected” is too strong. The ROM is valid and the program is coherent;     
  surplus/mis-marked is more likely. The die is functionally an 8051/SCN8051H.  
  - The 12 MHz rating is not “likely.” CCA44 is the 12 MHz commercial PLCC      
  order code. The board’s 13.875 MHz clock is a definite ~16% overclock, not    
  just “above likely.”                                                          
  - Sweep speed table overrun — likely a second bug. 0FBEh–0FD6h is 25 bytes =  
  100 speed-class entries (2 bits each). The sweep index runs 01h–83h (1–131).  
  sweep_speed_class indexes R7/4, so for R7 > 99 it reads past the table into   
  code at 0FD8h onward (75 A8 80 …), treating opcodes as speed classes. The     
  analysis notes that the lookup runs past the table but does not call it a     
  bug. If the sweep or a command can actually reach R7 > 63h, the speed becomes 
  arbitrary near the end of the sweep. That is worth flagging as a suspected    
  bug, or as evidence that the sweep is never commanded past 99 in practice.    
  - The 05C5h table fit is “about 96/√n + 4” but the first entry C9h (201) is   
  an outlier; the rest fit well. Minor.                                         
  - The 05E3h second ramp is still unverified; the analysis is honest about     
  that.                                                                         
  Everything else — motor tables, command decoding, startup echo,               
  shift-register inputs, P2.3 option, status pins, memory layout — reads        
  consistently.                                                                 
  What I would do next                                                          
  1. Check the original board or connector: the serial bus host, the            
  96-position carousel, the needle/syringe mechanism, and the P2.3 option       
  jumper would identify the product quickly.                                    
  2. Look for 1980s autosampler service manuals with a 96-vial carousel and a   
  three-motor arrangement; the command set is distinctive enough to match.      
  3. Check whether the 0FBEh table overrun is reachable from the host command   
  set. If it is, that is a real bug in the original firmware.                   
  4. Treat the chip as an 8051/SCN8051H for any reuse; the SCN8031H marking is  
  misleading. The 4 KB ROM is valid, and the code expects internal ROM.         
  The short version: a 96-position sample handler with a needle/syringe and a   
  multidrop serial command interface, built on a mis-marked 8051. The OKI chip  
  is plausibly the measurement front end of the same kind of laboratory         
  instrument, but the two are not proven to be from one unit.                   

⏺ The review is solid, and I agree with nearly all of it. The one new technical
  claim worth checking is whether the speed-table overrun can actually happen.
  Tracing the callers of sweep_speed_class...
  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I agree with nearly all of it, and I can settle the one open technical point.
  The speed-table overrun can't happen, though tracing it showed I had attached
  that table to the wrong routine.

  The overrun: not reachable

  sweep_speed_class isn't called from the sweep at 06F4h at all. Its only
  callers (0D0Bh and 0D51h) are in a different automatic sequence at 0CFDh,
  which runs when flag 20h.2 is clear. The sweep runs when it's set. In that
  sequence:

  - R6 counts down from 30h (48) on each call. R7 increments on most calls, and
    the carousel (motor B) moves at fixed points:

  | R7 reaches    | Carousel move                                        |
  |---------------|------------------------------------------------------|
  | 1             | 26h positions                                        |
  | 2             | 10h positions                                        |
  | 32h           | 18h positions                                        |
  | anything else | 23h positions back the other way, and R7 resets to 0 |

  - Working through the counts, R7 peaks at 52h (82), so the lookup index stays
    within the 100-entry table. The table is longer than needed, not too short.
  - So it isn't a bug. The real mistake was mine: I described 0FBEh as speed
    classes "for the sweep" and wrote that indexes above 99 run into code. Both
    claims need correcting.
  - This sequence is also its own thing: an automatic pattern of carousel moves,
    with motor C's direction flipping as it goes. Something like a scripted
    tour of carousel positions, alongside the 06F4h sweep.

  Points I accept

  - Drop "rejected": the ROM is valid and complete. "Surplus or mis-marked 8051"
    is the better wording.
  - 12 MHz is definite for the CC grade, so 13.875 MHz is a firm ~16% overclock,
    not a likely one.
  - The 05C5h fit: the first entry (C9h) is an outlier. The rest fit.
  - Autosampler ranking: HPLC/GC autosampler first, sample changer or gamma
    counter second. A 96-position carousel suggests vials or tubes more than a
    rectangular microplate. That's a fair refinement of my "microplate" remark.

  Points I'd hold more loosely

  - The 8051dumper / Micropolis SCN8051H precedent: plausible and relevant, but
    I haven't checked it, so I'd cite it only after confirming.
  - "Same instrument family" for the two chips: the complementary roles do make
    it the better story. A gamma counter in particular fits well: count rates
    spanning 6 decades on a log scale, several detectors, and a 96-tube
    carousel. But the evidence for a link is still only functional fit. The eras
    differ by about a decade, the serial formats differ, and neither ROM refers
    to the other. I'd write it as a leading hypothesis, not a conclusion.

  I can update scn8031/ROM_ANALYSIS.md and the annotations to:
  - correct which routine uses 0FBEh, with the R7 ≤ 52h bound;
  - describe the 0CFDh carousel sequence;
  - apply the wording changes;
  - add the gamma counter / HPLC pairing as the leading joint hypothesis.

  Shall I make those changes and commit them?

✻ Worked for 53s · done 10:03 PM

❯ Response:                                                                     
  Good catch — I was wrong about the overrun. I traced the caller graph and     
  agree with your reading:                                                      
  - sweep_step at 06F4h does not call sweep_speed_class.                        
  - Its only callers are 0D0Bh and 0D51h, both in the 0CFDh automatic sequence  
  when 20h.2 is clear.                                                          
  - R7 peaks at 52h, so the table byte index is at most 14h — well inside the   
  25-byte table. The table is oversized, not undersized.                        
    So the real correction is naming and attribution, not a bug. I’d change:    
  - sweep_speed_class → carousel_sequence_speed_class or sequence_speed_class.  
  - 0FBEh title: “Carousel sequence speed classes”, not “Sweep speed classes”.  
  - 0F9Fh summary: “Speed class for R7 in the 0CFDh carousel sequence; R7 ≤     
  52h.”                                                                         
  - Add the 0CFDh description: fixed carousel moves of 26h/10h/18h at R7 =      
  1/2/32h, otherwise 23h back with R7 reset, while R6 counts down from 30h and  
  motor C’s direction flips.                                                    
  - Keep the 06F4h sweep as the actual sweep, with its own behavior.            
    I’d call the 0CFDh thing a scripted carousel sequence / tour, not a sweep.  
  Its exact purpose — homing, self-test, or a fixed presentation pattern — is   
  still open.                                                                   
    Your other proposed changes all look right:                                 
  - Drop “rejected”; the ROM is valid and complete. “Surplus or mis-marked      
  8051” is better.                                                              
  - CCA44 makes the 12 MHz rating definite; the 13.875 MHz board clock is a     
  firm ~16% overclock.                                                          
  - The 05C5h first entry is an outlier; the rest fit the 96/√n + 4 shape.      
  - Autosampler ranking: HPLC/GC autosampler first, sample changer or gamma     
  counter second. A 96-position carousel reads more like vials/tubes than a     
  rectangular microplate.                                                       
    On the two loose points:                                                    
  - 8051dumper / Micropolis precedent: I checked the README. It explicitly      
  names a Signetics SCN8051H from a Micropolis 1325 (DEC RD53-A). So the        
  precedent is real, but it is a different program/product. Cite it as evidence 
  that this die/EA behavior is dumpable, not as evidence that this ROM came     
  from a disk drive.                                                            
  - Gamma counter / HPLC pairing: I agree as a leading hypothesis, not a        
  conclusion. I’d word it as:                                                   
    “A gamma counter is the best joint fit — 6-decade count rates, several      
  detectors/energy windows, and a 96-tube carousel. An HPLC/GC autosampler is   
  the best single-unit fit for the Signetics chip alone. The decade-wide era    
  gap, different serial formats, and absence of cross-references keep the link  
  speculative.”                                                                 
    That seems like the right set of edits. Go ahead with them.

  scn8031/ROM_ANALYSIS.md
  - The marking: explains why a truly ROMless part would have dumped the SRAM
    contents instead of a program. It now calls the chip a surplus or mis-marked
    8051 (SCN8051H) and says the top marking isn't trustworthy. "Rejected" is
    gone.
  - Clock: the 12 MHz rating is now definite, with the 13.875 MHz board clock
    described as a ~16% overclock.
  - Precedent: cites the 8051dumper project's SCN8051H from a Micropolis 1325
    (DEC RD53-A) as evidence the dump method works on this chip family, not as a
    hint about where this chip came from.
  - New "Automatic sequences" section: the 06F4h sweep and the scripted carousel
    sequence at 0D05h, which flag 20h.2 chooses between. It includes the R7 →
    carousel move table and the R7 ≤ 52h bound, and leaves the sequence's
    purpose open.
  - 0FBEh table: retitled as the carousel sequence's speed classes, with the
    note that its 100 entries are more than the sequence ever uses, so there is
    no overrun.
  - 05C5h ramp: the first entry is noted as an outlier.
  - Applications: re-ranked, with HPLC/GC autosampler first, then a sample
    changer or gamma-counter carousel, microplate handler, filter wheel, and
    camera pan/tilt last.
  - Relation to the OKI chip: uses the reviewer's wording: a gamma counter is
    the best joint fit, the HPLC/GC autosampler the best fit for this chip
    alone, and the link stays speculative.

  Annotations and listing
  - sweep_speed_class is now sequence_speed_class, described as "R7 ≤ 52h".
  - New comment blocks introduce auto_sequence (0CFDh) and carousel_sequence
    (0D05h).
  - The 0FBEh table is retitled, and the listing header now says the chip is
    functionally an 8051.
