✻ Cooked for 1m 23s · done 10:05 PM

❯ What sort of motors would this've driven?>                                    

⏺ First checking how the ramp values turn into timer periods, so I can estimate
  step rates...

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The step patterns, timing code and port behaviour narrow this down quite well.

  Motors A and C: 4-phase (two-winding) steppers, half-stepping

  - Winding pairs are visible in the sequence. The half-step cycle energises
    bits 0 → 3 → 1 → 2 (with overlaps), and bits 0 and 1 are never on together,
    nor bits 2 and 3. So bits 0/1 are the two ends (or halves) of one winding,
    and bits 2/3 the other.
  - Unipolar, most likely. In mid-1980s instrument design, 5- or 6-wire unipolar
    steppers with one transistor per phase were the norm. The pattern would
    also drive a bipolar motor through two H-bridges, since opposite directions
    are never driven at once.
  - Size class: probably small permanent-magnet or hybrid motors, e.g. 1.8° or
    7.5° per step. A 200-step motor gives 400 half-steps per revolution.
  - Motor A's scaling: a move count is multiplied by 3 unless a flag is set
    (0298h). That suggests moves in units tied to a gear or leadscrew ratio, as
    on a syringe or vertical axis.

  Motor B: a 3-phase motor, half-stepped

  - The 6-step sequence alternates one and two phases on: half-stepping for a
    3-phase motor. With no feedback inputs this is open-loop, so it's a stepper,
    not a brushless DC motor.
  - Most likely a 3-phase variable-reluctance stepper, typically 15° or 7.5° per
    step. They were common and cheap at the time, fast, with low torque and no
    detent, which suits a light carousel. A 15° motor gives 48 half-steps per
    revolution, so 96 positions would be 2 motor revolutions per carousel turn,
    or a gear ratio. That's speculative.
  - Alternative: a 3-phase hybrid stepper. Berger Lahr made these, popular with
    European instrument makers.
  - Bit 3 held high: not a phase. It could be a driver enable, a
    current-reduction line or a brake. If active-low like the others, high would
    mean off.

  Driver stage

  - Active-low drive: all outputs idle high, so a coil is on when its pin is
    low. That's a sensible choice: 8051 ports come up high at reset, so every
    coil stays off until the firmware runs. 8051 pins also sink current far
    better than they source it.
  - Driver type unknown. Common choices that fit are PNP transistors,
    optocouplers (pin sinks the LED), or an inverting stage ahead of a
    Darlington array.
  - Holding current unknown. I haven't checked whether the firmware turns the
    coils off after a move.

  Step rates (assuming the 11.0592 MHz crystal)

  The timer interrupts take the step period as a table value times a scale
  factor (0515h, 049Ah). One machine cycle is about 1.085 µs.

  ┌────────────┬──────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │   Motor    │  Scale   │ Slowest → fastest step │         Rate          │
  │            │          │         period         │                       │
  ├────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ B (fast    │ ×10h     │ 201 → 20 entries: 3.5  │ about 290 → 2,900     │
  │ range)     │          │ ms → 0.35 ms           │ half-steps/s          │
  ├────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ B (slow    │ ×80h     │ 27.9 ms → 2.8 ms       │ about 36 → 360        │
  │ range)     │          │                        │ half-steps/s          │
  ├────────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ C          │ ×B0h or  │ 1C×B0h = 5.3 ms …      │ about 190 → 1,300     │
  │            │ ×40h     │ 0B×40h = 0.76 ms       │ half-steps/s          │
  └────────────┴──────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  About 2,900 half-steps/s suits a variable-reluctance motor; they run fast.
  Motor C's 190–1,300 half-steps/s is typical of a small unipolar stepper on a
  leadscrew or belt. These are approximate: interrupt overhead adds a little to
  each period.

  For a reproduction

  - Motors A and C: 28BYJ-48 (5-wire unipolar, geared), or any small 4-phase
    unipolar stepper, through an inverter and a ULN2003. Wire winding 1 to bits
    0/1 and winding 2 to bits 2/3.
  - Motor B: a true 3-phase stepper is hard to find now. Options:
    - three LEDs, just to watch it;
    - a small 3-phase gimbal BLDC motor, run open-loop through a 3-phase driver
      at low speed;
    - an old 3-phase VR stepper, if you can find one in surplus.

✻ Baked for 57s · done 10:08 PM

❯ COuld this actually be useful, for example, for a 3D printer (for novelty     
  purposes - would it _work_, not be better than state of the art)              

⏺ With the stock ROM it would only work as a very crude raster machine. Used as
  a plain 8051 running your own code, it could drive a real (slow) printer. A
  printer needs four things that this firmware handles poorly or not at all.

  The four requirements

  Requirement: 3–4 axes (X, Y, Z, extruder)
  This firmware: 3 motors per chip, but only one is a tracked linear axis (C).
    Motor A makes relative moves only, and B is a 96-position wrap-around axis.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement: Coordinated motion (diagonals and curves need X and Y stepped in
  a
    fixed ratio)
  This firmware: None. Timer 0 runs A or B, Timer 1 runs C, each with its own
    ramp. Only the combined command moves two motors at once (B plus a small C
    offset), and even then nothing keeps them in step. Other moves are refused
    while one is running.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement: Feed-rate control
  This firmware: 3–4 fixed speed classes and built-in ramp tables. No way to set

    an arbitrary speed.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Requirement: Heaters, thermistors, fan
  This firmware: None.

  What could work with the original ROM

  The printer would draw only straight lines along one axis, like a dot-matrix
  printer filling in a shape:

  - Y = motor C: a belt or leadscrew. With 3168 half-steps of travel at, say,
    0.1 mm per half-step, that's about 317 mm. Top speed around 1,300
    half-steps/s, about 130 mm/s.
  - X = motor A: relative moves between lines, extruder idle.
  - Extruder = motor B: a cute fit. The extruder only ever turns forwards, and
    B's shortest-path rule turns forwards whenever the target is less than 48
    positions ahead. Keep raising the target by small amounts and B rotates
    continuously. The combined command (00h–7Fh) extrudes on B while moving C,
    though only ±62 half-steps of C per command and with no ratio control, so
    lines would be blobby.
  - Z: use a second chip. It's a multidrop bus and you have a set of them. One
    node for Z.
  - Hotend: a separate modern temperature controller, outside this system.

  The result would be shapes filled with parallel lines along one axis, with no
  true outlines or curves. A recognisable test cube is plausible; anything finer
  isn't. A pen plotter or a cold paste/frosting extruder would avoid the heater
  problem and be more forgiving.

  The other bad fit is a polar printer, with B as a rotating bed: 96 positions
  is 3.75° per position, about 6.5 mm of arc at 100 mm radius.

  What would work properly: use it as a plain 8051

  The chip is a working 8051. Hold EA low and it runs code from external memory
  instead of its mask ROM, just like the dumper did. Then you write your own
  firmware:

  - Steps for X/Y/Z/E from timer interrupts, with simple linear interpolation.
    An 8051 at 11–12 MHz could manage a few kHz of steps in total.
  - G-code parsed on a host PC, which streams simple move commands over the UART
    (128 bytes of internal RAM rules out parsing on-chip).
  - Heater control with on/off switching and a thermistor through a simple ADC
    (e.g. an RC timing circuit or an external ADC chip).
  - It could reuse the original's ideas: the half-step tables, active-low
    drivers and multidrop bus. The existing breadboard (AVR loading SRAM) is
    already a development rig for this.

  That would genuinely print, slowly. A 1984 NMOS 8051 driving a 3D printer is a
  good novelty project. The original firmware is better suited to a demo of its
  intended job: a 96-position carousel, a sweeping arm and a plunger.

✻ Brewed for 42s · done 10:11 PM

❯ Okay. What sort of novelty projects could it drive? I'm thinking thre would   
  be a PC or modern microcontroller sending sommands over serial.               

⏺ The best projects play to what the firmware already does well:
  - a 96-position wheel that always takes the short way round;
  - a linear axis with absolute positioning and a built-in back-and-forth sweep;
  - a third motor for short relative moves such as a poke, lift or strike;
  - sensor inputs, status lights, and a multidrop bus that lets several boards
    share one serial line.

  Best fits

  1. ASCII character drum display. This is my favourite.
  - There are exactly 96 printable ASCII characters (20h–7Fh). Put them round a
    drum on motor B and each board becomes one character, with the shortest-path
    logic choosing the quicker way to the next letter.
  - Chain several boards on the bus and you have a mechanical message board, a
    close cousin of your LED sign.
  - Motor C and motor A could flip a cover or tap a bell when the text changes.

  2. Photogrammetry or 3D-scanning turntable.
  - Motor B turns the object in 3.75° steps: 96 shots per revolution, a typical
    photogrammetry count.
  - Motor C raises the camera for multiple rings of shots, and motor A tilts it
    or triggers the shutter.
  - The PC script loops: move, wait for the status reply, take a photo.

  3. Polar "spirograph" plotter.
  - Paper on the turntable (B), a pen on the linear arm (C), pen up and down on
    A.
  - The combined command moves B and C together, and the sweep draws zigzags, so
    spirals and rosettes come naturally.
  - The angular resolution is coarse, but art forgives that.

  4. Xylophone or glockenspiel robot.
  - Motor C positions a striker over a row of bars, using absolute positions as
    notes; motor A strikes.
  - At about 130 mm/s it suits slow melodies, and the sweep gives you a
    glissando.
  - Two boards on the bus would give two hands.

  5. Carousel dispenser or "mini lab".
  - What the original probably was, played straight: 96 tubes or cups, an arm,
    and a dipper or plunger.
  - Ideas: a 96-cell seedling-tray waterer, a sweet or spice dispenser by slot,
    a tea-bag dunker. The robot sorting M&Ms by colour into 96 cups is the
    showpiece version.

  6. Card or disc carousel with a picker.
  - A 96-slot rack for CDs, trading cards or tarot cards: B selects the slot, C
    reaches, A lifts.
  - Ideas: a jukebox, a "draw a random card" machine, a mechanical Rolodex.

  Others that work

  - 24-hour dial clock: 96 positions = 24 hours × 4 quarter-hours. B turns the
    dial every 15 minutes and A strikes a chime.
  - Kinetic sculpture: the sweep routine drives a slow back-and-forth wave, with
    the wheel turning on a timer.
  - Prize wheel or roulette: spin to a host-chosen position. The status reply
    tells the host when it has landed.

  Practical notes

  - Motor B is 3-phase. For a first build, use three LEDs to watch it, a small
    3-phase gimbal motor run open-loop, or a surplus 3-phase stepper.
    Two-winding steppers (28BYJ-48 etc.) suit A and C.
  - Several characters or hands: each board needs its own chip. You have a set,
    and the bus was designed for chaining. Expect to experiment with how the
    start-up echo sequence assigns or discovers boards.
  - Sensors: a home switch on INT1 for the wheel, and buttons or limit switches
    on a 74HC165.
  - Host: anything with a UART at 9600 baud, 8 data bits, odd parity: a PC with
    a USB-serial adapter, an RP2040 or ESP32 (via a level shifter, the chip runs
    at 5 V), or the ATmega already on your bench. The host sends C0 80 and 8
    echo bytes at start-up, then 2-byte commands, and checks each 1-byte reply
    before sending the next move.

  I'd start with the character drum or the photogrammetry turntable: both mainly
  use motor B, which is the most distinctive part of the firmware. Next step
  could be a small Python host library: the start-up sequence, each command as a
  function, and replies decoded.

✻ Cogitated for 36s · done 10:13 PM

❯ What about the OKI one?                                                       

⏺ Much harder, and less rewarding. The SCN8031's firmware drives motors directly
  from its own pins. The OKI's firmware only talks to custom external chips
  we've never seen. Without them it has nothing to read or report.

  Why it's hard

  ┌────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              It needs              │               Status               │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The channel/host chip at FFxxh:    │                                    │
  │ command registers, ready mask,     │ Unknown part, probably a custom    │
  │ data window, status registers, and │ chip. Would have to be emulated.   │
  │  interrupts on INT0                │                                    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The link controller at             │ Unknown part. Setup reads back 20  │
  │ 0000h–000Ah: message buffer,       │ register values and flags a fault  │
  │ indirect registers, ready flags    │ if any differ. The handshake also  │
  │                                    │ expects 66 04 88 replies.          │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Something on the far end that      │ The readings come out as 30 hh ll  │
  │ makes sense of its output          │ hh ll in a format whose mantissa   │
  │                                    │ we haven't decoded.                │
  └────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  Its actual job is a table lookup plus message passing, which isn't visible.
  Any modern microcontroller could do the lookup in one line. The novelty would
  be in getting the original firmware running, not in what it produces.

  What is worth doing

  1. A software "virtual instrument". Best value, no hardware.
  - Turn dis51.py into a small 8051 emulator (the decoder already exists, so
    it's maybe 300 lines). Add Python models of the two unknown chips and run
    the real ROM.
  - Inject channel commands and readings, watch the messages it sends, and step
    through the 35-state channel state machine. Confirm the suspected bugs (the
    7-byte message, the register clobber at 2C5Ch) by actually triggering them.
  - Talk to its debug monitor through the emulated host interface: peek and poke
    a 1990s instrument through its own service backdoor.
  - The emulator would then work for the SCN8031 too: you could test the Python
    host library against the real stepper firmware before building any hardware.

  2. Running it on real hardware with emulated peripherals. Ambitious.
  - The 8051's bus cycles are too fast for the AVR to answer as a fake
    peripheral: a read strobe lasts about 430 ns.
  - An RP2040 using its PIO state machines could answer bus reads and writes in
    time, presenting the mailbox, channel registers and link controller to the
    real chip. A CPLD or dual-port RAM would also work.
  - Payoff: the original silicon running its original code, connected through a
    modern bridge. A good retro-computing project, but most of the work is in
    the emulation.

  3. A cheap, crude demonstration on the existing breadboard.
  - Pre-load the SRAM with fake register values, so the firmware reads them as
    peripherals, then let it run from internal ROM.
  - Some of its waits would pass: for example, it polls link register 1 bit 4,
    so pre-load 10h.
  - The link controller's read-back checks would fail, because SRAM just returns
    the last value written. The firmware would then run in its fault state, and
    you'd see the P3.5 health output blinking. That's a nice "it's alive"
    moment for little effort, and you could read the RAM afterwards to see what
    it wrote.

  4. Use it as a plain 80C51.
  - Your LED sign project already does this: with EA held low, it runs your code
    and its mask ROM is irrelevant.

  A possible end-to-end demo

  Once the emulation exists (option 1 or 2):
  1. Feed 8 light sensors (through log amplifiers or ADCs) in as channel
     readings.
  2. Let the original firmware convert them.
  3. Catch its 30 messages and show the results on your LED sign.

  The output values could be read back by reverse lookup in the known table,
  even with the mantissa undecoded.

  I'd start with option 3 for a quick result on the existing bench, then option
  1. The emulator is reusable and would let you test both ROMs and the stepper
  host library. I can start on the emulator, beginning with execution semantics
  in tools/.

✻ Brewed for 54s · done 10:15 PM

❯ The reviewer said "the OKI board could be a novelty 8-channel log-scale       
  sensor display — temperature, humidity, current, filament tension, etc."      

⏺ It's workable as a novelty, but the sensor list doesn't fit what the firmware
  does.

  Sensor choice. The firmware's one distinctive trick is a log scale: 60 counts
  per decade, about 6 decades in total. That suits quantities spanning many
  orders of magnitude, and suits poorly ones that don't:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Fits a 6-decade log scale       │             Poor fit             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Light level (starlight to sunlight   │ Temperature: a narrow, roughly   │
  │ is 6+ decades)                       │ linear range                     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sound level / dB meters              │ Humidity: 0–100%, linear         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Current, from µA leakage to amps     │ Filament tension: narrow range   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Radiation count rate (a Geiger tube) │                                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ RF signal strength                   │                                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Vacuum pressure, resistance across   │                                  │
  │ decades                              │                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Temperature, humidity and filament tension would use a sliver of the scale and
  gain nothing from it. That's the same argument that put building control last
  in the hypotheses. Of the reviewer's list, only current fits. A light, sound,
  Geiger and current panel would show off the log scale properly.

  Other caveats:
  - The OKI doesn't take the log itself. Each channel delivers a 7-bit value
    that is already logarithmic, and the firmware converts it to an
    exponent-and-mantissa value. Each sensor would need a log amplifier, or a
    small microcontroller computing the log. At that point the front end could
    produce the final value directly, so the OKI's role is purely ceremonial.
  - It has no display. Readings leave as 30 hh ll hh ll messages through the
    link controller. Something has to catch them and drive a display; your LED
    sign would be a nice match.
  - The peripherals still have to be emulated, as discussed: the channel/host
    chip and the link controller, either an RP2040 on the bus or a software
    emulator.
  - The output format is only half decoded. Converting messages back to numbers
    needs either the mantissa decoded or a reverse lookup in the table. The
    reverse lookup just recovers the log reading that went in, so we'd compute
    the displayed value ourselves.

  Verdict: as "the original 1990s firmware is in the loop", it's a fun project,
  especially with sensors that span decades and readings shown on your LED sign.
  As a functional instrument the OKI adds nothing a modern MCU couldn't do in a
  line of code.

  The quickest route to a demo is the software emulator: fake sensor values in,
  real firmware in the middle, messages out to the LED sign. The emulator also
  exposes the mantissa: feed every input value through the real firmware and
  capture the outputs, which may make the encoding easier to see.

✻ Churned for 21s · done 10:16 PM

❯ Ah - reading your table again: I didn't get at first that there were          
  memory-mapped other chips?                                                    

⏺ Yes. The OKI firmware reads and writes other chips as if they were memory. On
  the original board those chips sat in the 8051's external data space, where
  your breadboard has its SRAM.

  How it works on an 8051

  - MOVX instructions read or write external data memory. Each access drives the
    address out on P0/P2, strobes RD or WR, and moves a byte over P0.
  - The CPU can't tell RAM from anything else at those addresses.
    Address-decoding logic on the board decides which chip responds. So
    peripheral chips get "memory-mapped": each register is just an address.
  - The OKI ran its program from internal ROM, so it used the external bus only
    for those peripherals.

  The OKI's two regions

  Address: FFD9h–FFFFh
  Accessed with: MOVX @R0/@R1 with P2 left at its reset value FFh
  Contents (inferred): Channel/host interface: status registers (D9h, DAh, DEh,
    DFh), the per-channel data window (E0h–EFh), channel command bytes
  (F0h–F7h),
     the host mailbox (F8h–FFh), and write-only controls such as the watchdog at

    E3h. Possibly several chips, separated by address decoding.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Address: 0000h–000Ah
  Accessed with: MOVX @DPTR with DPH = 00h
  Contents (inferred): Link controller: status, command, indirect index/data,
    message buffer, destination.

  The OKI also gets interrupts from that hardware: an INT0 signal, with D9h/DFh
  saying which part needs attention.

  Clues about what the chips were

  - Every read is done twice (MOVX A,@R1 twice in a row). That's typical of a
    slow peripheral, or one where the first read latches the value and the
    second returns it. It suggests a custom gate array or older NMOS peripherals
    rather than fast RAM.
  - Different registers for read and write at the same address (e.g. FBh reads
    the host's debug command but writes the channel toggle bits). Typical of
    peripheral chips and latches, not RAM.
  - Host mailbox at F8h–FFh: possibly a dual-port RAM or mailbox chip shared
    with the host CPU. That would fit the idea of an I/O card in a bigger
    machine.
  - Link controller: a message buffer behind one register, plus indirect
    registers and command codes. That looks like a dedicated network or
    serial-link chip. I haven't been able to identify its part number from the
    register layout alone.

  Original board versus your breadboard

   Original board                          Your breadboard
   ──────────────                          ───────────────
   OKI 8051 (code from internal ROM)       8051 (code from SRAM, or internal ROM
  for the dump)
     │ P0/P2 bus, RD/WR                       │ P0/P2 bus, RD/WR
     ├── decode FFxxh → channel/host chips    ├── 32 KB SRAM (code half + data 
  half)
     └── decode 0000h → link controller       └── ATmega328 (loads SRAM, then 
  watches)

  - Why the dump worked: the dumper used MOVX writes, and on your board all MOVX
    space goes to the SRAM.
  - If the OKI's own firmware ran on your board: its "peripheral" accesses would
    hit SRAM. That's the crude demo from earlier: pre-load fake register values
    into the SRAM and watch what the firmware writes.

  The Signetics chip, by contrast, never uses MOVX. Everything it controls
  (motors, sensors, serial) is wired straight to its port pins. That's why it
  can be reused without emulating any missing chips.

⏺ I've rewritten the start of the OKI analysis so the external chips are
  explained up front, and committed it to romdump-analysis as b7f616b. Nothing
  is pushed.

  In ROM_ANALYSIS.md, the old "Revised picture" heading is now "The hardware the
  firmware talks to", and it opens with:

  - How memory mapping works: MOVX reads and writes go out over the bus, and
    address-decoding logic on the board picks which chip answers. So peripheral
    registers look like memory to the CPU. This firmware uses no external RAM,
    so every MOVX in it is a peripheral access.
  - A table of the two regions: FFD9h–FFFFh is the channel/host interface
    (status registers, channel data, command bytes, host mailbox, watchdog), and
    0000h–000Ah is the link controller. It also says how the code reaches each
    one.
  - A diagram of the inferred original board: the CPU, the address decoder, both
    peripheral blocks, and what each connects to. INT0 is shown coming from the
    channel/host interface.
  - Clues about the unidentified chips: every read is done twice, reads and
    writes at one address reach different registers, the mailbox might be a
    dual-port RAM, and the link controller's register map would be the best
    fingerprint for identifying the equipment.
  - What happens on your breadboard: the same accesses land in the SRAM. That's
    why the dump worked, and why the firmware can't do its real job there
    without emulating the missing chips.

  The existing sections on the host mailbox, the eight channels and the link
  controller follow on unchanged.

  In scn8031/ROM_ANALYSIS.md, the overview table now has an "External
  peripherals" row: none, everything is on its port pins, so it needs no missing
  chips to run.

✻ Churned for 1m 11s · done 10:19 PM
